Title
Philipp Jettkant

Abstract
We introduce a novel class of semilinear nonlocal backward stochastic partial differential equations (BSPDE) on half-spaces driven by an infinite-dimensional càdlàg martingale. The equations exhibit a degeneracy and have no explicit condition at the boundary of the half-space. To treat the existence and uniqueness of such BSPDEs we establish a generalisation of Itô’s formula for infinite-dimensional càdlàg semimartingales, which addresses the occurrence of boundary terms. Next, we employ these BSPDEs to study the McKean–Vlasov control problem with killing and common noise proposed in [ HJ23 ] . The particles in this control model live on the real line and are killed at a positive intensity whenever they are in the negative half-line. Accordingly, the interaction between particles occurs through the subprobability distribution of the living particles. We establish the existence of an optimal semiclosed-loop control that only depends on the particles’ location and not their cumulative intensity. This problem cannot be addressed through classical mimicking arguments, because the particles’ subprobability distribution cannot be reconstructed from their location alone. Instead, we represent optimal controls in terms of the solutions to BSPDEs of the above type and show those solutions do not depend on the intensity variable.



Semilinear BSPDEs and Applications to McKean-Vlasov Control with Killing

Ben Hambly and Philipp Jettkant

Mathematical Institute, University of Oxford

1
Introduction

The aim of this article is two-fold: first, we introduce a novel class of semilinear backward stochastic partial differential equations (BSPDE) driven by an infinite-dimensional càdlàg martingale. Then, we employ these BSPDEs to establish the equivalence between the open- and closed-loop formulation of the McKean–Vlasov control problem with killing at a state-dependent intensity recently proposed in
[
HJ23
]
. In this control model, the location of the representative particle is described by a diffusion process
X=(X_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
on the real line with McKean–Vlasov dynamics that feature a common noise
W
. Whenever the particle is located in the negative half-line, its cumulative intensity process
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
increases at a positive rate
\lambda(t,X_{t})
. Once the accumulated intensity
\Lambda_{t}
exceeds a standard exponential random variable
\theta
, the particle is killed. Accordingly, the particles do not interact through the conditional law
\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|W)
of all particles but rather the conditional subprobability distribution
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W)
of the living particles.

In
[
HJ23
]
, we establish the existence of an optimal weak control
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
of the form
\gamma_{t}=g_{t}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t})
for an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)\to G
. Here
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
extends the filtration generated by the common noise and
G\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
is the control space. The need for an enlarged filtration comes from the tightness arguments used to establish the existence of the optimal weak control
\gamma
. In this weak formulation of the control problem, the common noise in the conditioning of the subprobability of the living particles is replaced by the larger
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{T}
. That is, the particles interact through the term
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})
. The function
g
is called a semiclosed-loop control, because while it can only depend on the idiosyncratic information through the state
(X_{t},\Lambda_{t})
, it has access to the entire history of the common noise
W
. For simplicity, we will nonetheless refer to such types of control as closed-loop in this work, as we will not encounter proper closed-loop controls.

Because the exponential random variable
\theta
has the memoryless property, we would not expect that it is necessary to keep track of the cumulative intensity
\Lambda_{t}
to optimally control the system. Only the increments of
\Lambda_{t}
, which are completely determined by the particle’s location
X_{t}
, are relevant. To make this intuition precise, it is advantageous to reformulate the mean-field control (MFC) problem as the control of the nonlinear nonlocal stochastic Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by the conditional joint law
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})
. We derive a necessary stochastic maximum principle (SMP) for this infinite-dimensional control problem, which allows us to express the optimal closed-loop control
g
from above in terms of a suitable adjoint process
\tilde{u}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
, which depends on the particle’s location
x
and cumulative intensity
y
. The adjoint process solves a linear BSPDE with nonlocality in
\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}
, arising from the nonlocalities of the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation, and a degeneracy in the intensity variable
y
. Moreover, because
g
and, therefore, the conditional joint law
\mu_{t}
are not necessarily adapted to the filtration of the common noise but the potentially larger filtration
\mathbb{F}
, to guarantee adaptedness of
\tilde{u}_{t}
an additional martingale driver may appear next to
W
in the BSPDE. This martingale is an infinite-dimensional càdlàg process adapted to
\mathbb{F}
. Now, by inserting the expression for the optimal closed-loop control
g
in terms of
\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)
into the BSPDE satisfied by the adjoint process
\tilde{u}
, we deduce a semilinear BSPDE for
\tilde{u}
. The last step in our analysis is to prove that the unique solution to this semilinear BSPDE takes the form
\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}u_{t}(x)
for a random function
u\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
. In view of the SMP, this implies that
g
does not depend on the particle’s intensity
y
.

The coupled forward-backward system of stochastic Fokker–Planck equation and semilinear BSPDE can be naturally interpreted as a mean-field game (MFG) system
[
LL06
]
. From this point of view the BSPDE can be understood as a stochastic Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman (HJB) equation. Stochastic HJB equations were first studied by
[
Pen92
]
and our analysis follows a similar strategy: we view the stochastic HJB equation as a backward stochastic evolution equation on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))
. This requires rather restrictive growth assumptions on the coefficients and cost functions, but we are able to lift them through a limit procedure. The main novelty in the analysis of this evolution equation are the infinite-dimensional càdlàg martingale driver and the degeneracy in the intensity variable which appears together with a seemingly underspecified boundary at
y=0
. To deal with these challenges, we establish a generalisation of Itô’s formula for
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
- and
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,\infty))
-valued processes, which is of independent interest.

1.1
Related Work

Linear BSPDEs were initially studied in the context of the optimal control of stochastic partial differential equations, particularly those arising in the filtering of partially observed diffusions
[
Ben83
,
NN90
,
Par80
,
Zho93
,
Zho92
]
. The solutions to these BSPDEs serve as adjoint processes that appear in the stochastic maximum principles formulated for these control problems. Specifically, the necessary SMP appearing in
[
Ben83
]
closely resembles the one established in this work, although unlike
[
Ben83
]
we have to deal with the nonlocalities of the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation as well the càdlàg martingale driver. For this, we again crucially draw on our generalisation of Itô’s formula. Structurally closest to ours are the nonlinear BSPDEs analysed in
[
HP91
,
Pen92
]
, while BSPDEs with nondegeneracies were studied in
[
MY97
,
MY99
,
HMY02
]
. In this work, we make frequent use of the BSPDE estimates derived by Ma and Yong
[
MY99
]
. To our knowledge the only article that considers a similar class of BSPDEs in a non-Brownian filtration is
[
Al-06
,
Al-09
]
. However, their work explicitly assumes that the filtration only generates continuous martingales in order to avoid jumps in the dynamics.

MFC and MFGs have seen a surge of interest in recent years and have been studied both from the probabilistic and analytic perspective. Particularly for MFGs, the analytic approach, which leads to a coupled forward-backward PDE consisting of the Fokker–Planck equation and the Hamilton–Jacobi–Bellman equation, has received a lot of attention
[
LL06
,
LL07
,
Car+14
,
GP16
]
. The corresponding HJB in the MFC setting is nonlocal and, thus, more challenging to analyse, so the literature is comparatively sparse
[
BFY13
,
AL15
]
. An additional challenge we face in this work is the stochasticity in the PDE system generated by the common noise. One trick that has been exploited in
[
Car+19
]
and, more recently, in
[
CSS22
]
is to tackle stochastic MFG PDE systems is to apply a time-dependent random shift to the particles’ conditional law to remove the stochastic integral appearing in the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation, leading to a PDE with random coefficients. The correspondingly transformed stochastic HJB equation exhibits a simpler structure and can be analysed more easily. Exploring the applicability of this technique in our setting is an interesting avenue for future research.

Equivalence of open- and closed loop formulations of (stochastic) control problems is a classical topic in the control literature. Usually, equivalence is established either through a probabilistic
[
Dav73
,
FR75
,
Bis76
,
Kar79
]
or analytic
[
Kry80
,
Lio83
,
Lio83a
]
study of the value function or Markovian selection and mimicking theorems
[
Hau86
,
KNJ87
,
HL90
,
Lac15
]
. The analysis of the value function in MFC and the associated HJB equation on the space of probability measures is an ongoing and challenging area of research, particularly in the common noise setting, so the most general equivalence results for MFC draw on mimicking theorems, see
[
LSZ20
]
. However, a straightforward application of the results in
[
LSZ20
]
only establishes the existence of optimal closed-loop controls that depend both on the particle’s location and cumulative intensity. To approach the more subtle notion of equivalence we consider in this work, new ideas are required.

The equivalence problem was previously studied in the related context of MFGs with absorption by Campi and coauthors
[
CF18
,
CGL21
]
. In an MFG with absorption, a particle is removed as soon as it leaves a prescribed domain
D
. The McKean–Vlasov dynamics with killing at state-dependent intensity described above can be interpreted as a regularisation of such a system: instead of killing particles immediately when they leave the domain, they accumulate intensity whenever they are located outside of
D
. The framework with immediate killing is recovered in the limit as the intensity tends to infinity (cf.
[
HJ23
, Theorem 2.14]
). Campi and Fischer
[
CF18
]
consider a conditional MFG framework with interaction through the conditional law of the living particles instead of their subprobability distribution. Here the conditioning is not with respect to a common noise, which is absent in
[
CF18
]
, but rather the event that a particle has not been killed yet. They establish the existence of a Nash equilibrium using the weak formulation of MFGs analysed in
[
CL15
]
. A verification argument shows that this equilibrium is in fact Markovian, which corresponds to an optimal closed-loop control in the MFC setting. The weak formulation and the related verification argument are not applicable to MFC, so this strategy does not apply in our context.

Concurrently with our work, Carmona, Laurière, and Lions
[
CLL23
]
addressed the equivalence problem for the control of conditional processes, originally introduced by Lions in a lecture at the Collège de France
[
Lio16
]
. In this problem, the running and terminal cost are computed not from the subprobability distribution of the living particles but their conditional law. This is closely related to the framework in
[
CF18
]
discussed above, though in the latter the conditional law only appears in the interaction between particles while the cost is computed based on the particles’ subprobability distribution.
[
CLL23
]
employs a strategy similar to the one followed in this article to establish the equivalence of the open- and closed-loop formulation for the control of conditional processes, albeit in a setting with constant coefficient and without common noise. Their article also considers regularised dynamics, where particles are killed according to a state-dependent intensity instead of simply being killed at the boundary of a given domain
D
. The original problem proposed by Lions was formulated for dynamics with immediate killing. Diffusions with immediate killing are less regular and consequently more challenging to deal with analytically, which means that the strategy developed in this article (and
[
CLL23
]
) cannot be straightforwardly extended to cover this case. However, in contrast to the regularised framework, by absorbing, i.e. stopping, the state process in the model with immediate killing
once it hits the boundary of the domain
D
, it is possible to read a particle’s status (alive or killed) from its location. This observation enables one to apply the mimicking theorem, from which one can easily establish the equivalence of the open- and closed-loop formulation for the control of conditional processes in the case with immediate killing. See Appendix
A.4
for details. Compare this with the regularised model, in which a particle’s location reveals little about its cumulative intensity.

1.2
Main Contributions and Structure of the Paper

In Section
2
we present the main results of the paper. In the course of this we introduce a novel class of BSPDEs with semilinear nonlocal coefficients, a degeneracy and underspecified boundary condition, as well as an infinite-dimensional càdlàg martingale driver.

The analysis of this BSPDE is provided in Section
3
. We first establish a generalisation of Itô’s formula for càdlàg semimartingales with values in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty))
, see Theorems
3.2
and Theorem
3.3
, and then prove existence and uniqueness for the BSPDE based on a Galerkin approximation and fixed-point arguments.

In Section
4
we discuss the equivalence of the open- and closed loop formulation for the McKean–Vlasov control problem with killing at a state-dependent intensity. First, we reformulate this control problem as the control of the associated stochastic Fokker–Planck equation, see Subsection
4.1
. Next, in Subsection
4.2
, we derive the necessary SMP by computing the Gateaux derivative of the value function. Subsequently, we study the adjoint process
\tilde{u}_{t}
through the lens of the BSPDE theory developed in Section
3
and, thereby, demonstrate its separable structure
\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}u_{t}(x)
(Subsection
4.3
). We conclude Section
4
by extending the equivalence result to more general coefficients and cost functions. To achieve this, in Subsection
4.4
, we reformulate stochastic Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by the conditional subprobability distribution of the living particles as a martingale problem and show the stability of the martingale problem under perturbations of the coefficients. As a byproduct we introduce a relaxed formulation for the control of stochastic Fokker–Planck equations, which is of independent interest.

2
Main Results and Outline of the Equivalence Proof

In this section we state the main results of this paper and provide an outline of the proof of equivalence for the open- and closed loop formulation of the McKean–Vlasov control problem discussed in the introduction. In what follows we will use
\nabla
and
\nabla^{2}
to denote the gradient and the Hessian, and write
a\cdot b=a^{\top}b
for
a
,
b\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as well as
A:B=\trace(A^{\top}B)
for
A
,
B\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, where
(\cdot)^{\top}
is the transpose.

2.1
Semilinear BSPDE with Càdlàg Noise

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a complete probability space equipped with a complete filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and a
d
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motion
W=(W_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. We study two types of semilinear backward SPDEs. First, for the domain
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
, with
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
, we consider the BSPDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}u_{t}(x,y)&=-\Bigl(a_{t}(x,y):\nabla_{x}^{2}u_{t}(x,y)+H_{t}\bigl(x,y,u_{t}(x,y),\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)\bigr)+F_{t}(x,y,u_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t})\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ -\lambda_{t}(x)\partial_{y}u_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t-\bigl(\alpha_{t}:\nabla_{x}q_{t}(x,y)+\beta_{t}(x,y)\cdot q_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +q_{t}(x,y)\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}m_{t}(x,y)\end{split}

(2.1)

for
(x,y)\in D
with terminal condition
u_{T}(x,y)=\psi(x,y)
, where
\psi\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\mathbb{P};L^{2}(D))
. Here
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the first
d
variables and
y\in(\ell,\infty)
the remaining one. The process
m=(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a càdlàg
\mathbb{F}
-martingale with values in the Hilbert space
L^{2}(D)
and together with
q
ensures that the solution
(u,q,m)
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable. Note that the BSPDE is only defined on a half-plane, even though we have not introduced any boundary condition. Since we assume that
\lambda
is nonnegative, this does not cause any issues. Indeed, since the SPDE proceeds backward in time, the nonnegativity of
\lambda
implies that the value of
u_{t}(x,y)
for
(t,\omega,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times D
only depends on the future values
u_{s}
,
q_{s}
, and
m_{s}
,
(s,\omega)\in[t,T]\times\Omega
, on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[y,\infty)
. Hence, we do not need to specify the behaviour of
u
on the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\{\ell\}
.

The second type of equation has a similar structure but is not defined on the half-plane
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[\ell,\infty)
but instead on the full space
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}u_{t}(x)&=-\Bigl(a_{t}(x):\nabla_{x}^{2}u_{t}(x)+H_{t}\bigl(x,u_{t}(x),\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)+F_{t}(x,u_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t})\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ -\bigl(\alpha_{t}:\nabla_{x}q_{t}(x)+\beta_{t}(x)\cdot q_{t}(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+q_{t}(x)\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}m_{t}(x).\end{split}

(2.2)

In both cases the coefficients are
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random functions
a\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times D\to\mathbb{S}^{d}
,
H\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times D\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
F\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times D\times L^{2}(D)\times L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbb{R}
,
\lambda\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
, and
\beta\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times D\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
\mathbb{S}^{d}
denotes the space of symmetric
d\times d
matrices. We will formulate appropriate assumptions on those coefficients below. The BSPDEs are nonlinear, nonlocal, and have a noise that takes values in an infinite-dimensional Hilbert space.

To define our notion of solutions to BSPDE (
2.1
), we first need to introduce some parabolic function spaces. Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space with scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. If
\mathcal{H}
is an an
L^{2}
-space, we will drop the subscript
\mathcal{H}
from the scalar product. Then, we let
L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
denote the space of
\mathcal{H}
-valued
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable stochastic processes
h=(h_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
such that
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\langle h_{t},h_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. The function
(h^{1},h^{2})\mapsto\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\langle h^{1}_{t},h^{2}_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\,\mathrm{d}t
for
h^{1}
,
h^{2}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
defines an inner product and turns
L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
into a Hilbert space. Next, we let
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
and
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
be the space of
\mathbb{F}
-adapted continuous and càdlàg processes
h
with values in
\mathcal{H}
, such that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}<\infty
. We endow both spaces with the norm

h\mapsto\bigl(\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\bigr)^{1/2}

which turns them into Banach spaces.

We let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
be the space of
\mathcal{H}
-valued
\mathbb{F}
-martingales
m=(m_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with a.s. càdlàg trajectories for which
\mathbb{E}[\langle m_{T},m_{T}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}]<\infty
. We equip
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
with the inner product
(m^{1},m^{2})\mapsto\mathbb{E}\langle m^{1}_{T},m^{2}_{T}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
which turns
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
into a Hilbert space as well.
We say that two martingales
m^{1}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}_{1})
,
m^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}_{2})
with values in separable Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, respectively, are
very strongly orthogonal
if for all elements
h_{1}\in\mathcal{H}_{1}
,
h_{2}\in\mathcal{H}_{2}
the real-valued càdlàg martingales
\langle m^{1},h_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}
and
\langle m^{2},h_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
are strongly orthogonal, i.e.
\bigl[\langle m^{1},h_{1}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\langle m^{2},h_{2}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\bigr]_{t}=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Lastly, we define

\mathbb{H}(D)=L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];H^{1,0}(D))\times L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))

if the domain
D
is
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
and

\mathbb{H}(D)=L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];H^{1}(D))\times L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d}))\times\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))

if
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here
H^{1,0}(D)
denotes the space of functions
f\in L^{2}(D)
that have a weak derivative
\nabla_{x}f\mathpunct{:}D\to\mathbb{R}
in the first
d
variables
x
, such that
\lVert\nabla_{x}f\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty
. It becomes a Hilbert space when equipped with the inner product

(f_{1},f_{2})\mapsto\int_{D}f_{1}(x,y)f_{2}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y+\int_{D}\nabla_{x}f_{1}(x,y)\cdot\nabla_{x}f_{2}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

We denote its dual space by
H^{-1,0}(D)
. Furthermore, for
k\geq 1
,
H^{k}(D)
is the classical Sobolev space with dual
H^{-k}(D)
and
H^{1}_{0}(D)
denotes the subspace of elements in
H^{1}(D)
that vanish on the boundary of
D
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
solution
to BSPDE (
2.1
) consists of a triple
(u,q,m)\in\mathbb{H}(D)
such that
m
is started from zero and is very strongly orthogonal to
W
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle u_{t},\varphi\rangle&=\langle\psi,\varphi\rangle+\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl\langle H_{s}\bigl(\cdot,\cdot,u_{s}(\cdot,\cdot),\nabla_{x}u_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigr)+F_{s}(\cdot,\cdot,u_{s},\nabla_{x}u_{s})+\beta_{s}\cdot q_{s},\varphi\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\nabla_{x}u_{s},\nabla_{x}\cdot(a_{s}\varphi)\bigr\rangle+\langle u_{s},\lambda_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle+\langle\alpha_{s}q_{s},\nabla_{x}\varphi\rangle\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\langle q_{s},\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\langle m_{T}-m_{t},\varphi\rangle\end{split}

(2.3)

for all
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(D)
and
\textup{Leb}\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
. A
solution
to BSPDE (
2.2
) is defined in an analogous manner but with test function from
H^{1}(D)
.

We call a solution
(u,q,m)
to BSPDE (
2.1
) or BSPDE (
2.2
), respectively,
unique
if for all other solutions
(u^{\prime},q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
to BSPDE (
2.1
) or BSPDE (
2.2
), respectively, it holds that
(u,q,m)=(u^{\prime},q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
as elements of
\mathbb{H}(D)
.

In Equation (
2.3
) we write
H_{s}\bigl(\cdot,\cdot,u_{s}(\cdot,\cdot),\nabla_{x}u_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigr)
to indicate that we integrate the function
(x,y)\mapsto H_{s}\bigl(x,y,u_{s}(x,y),\nabla_{x}u_{s}(x,y)\bigr)
in the scalar product. In contrast,
F_{s}
depends on
u_{s}
and
\nabla_{x}u_{s}
nonlocally, so that we integrate
(x,y)\mapsto F_{s}(x,y,u_{s},\nabla_{x}u_{s})
in the scalar product. The test functions for BSPDE (
2.1
) are in
H^{1}_{0}(D)
to avoid boundary terms from appearing. Note that Definition
2.1
implicitly assumes that the coefficient
a
is differentiable in
x
and the coefficient
\lambda
is differentiable in
y
. We shall always assume that this is the case.

Assumption 2.2
.

We assume that
\psi\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{T},\mathbb{P};L^{2}(D))
and that there exist
c_{a,\alpha}>0
,
C>0
, and
h_{H}
,
h_{F}\in L^{2}(D)
such that

(i)

for all
(t,\omega,z)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times D
,
(r,r^{\prime},p,p^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
, and
(u,u^{\prime},v,v^{\prime})\in L^{2}(D)^{2}\times L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d})^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\lvert H_{t}(z,r,p)-H_{t}(z,r^{\prime},p^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(\lvert r-r^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert p-p^{\prime}\rvert),

\displaystyle\lvert F_{t}(z,u,v)-F_{t}(z,u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq h_{F}(z)(\lVert u-u^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert v-v^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}});

(ii)

the map
z\mapsto a_{t}(z)
has a weak derivative in the first
d
variables and
z\mapsto\lambda_{t}(z)
has a weak derivative in the last variable for
\mathbb{P}\otimes\leb
-a.e.
(t,\omega)
;

(iii)

the coefficients
a
,
\nabla_{x}\cdot a
,
\alpha
,
\beta
,
\lambda
, and
\partial_{y}\lambda
are bounded by
C
and for all
(t,\omega,z)
,
(r,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
(u,v)\in L^{2}(D)\times L^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d})
we have

\displaystyle\lvert H_{t}(z,r,p)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(h_{H}(z)+\lvert r\rvert+\lvert p\rvert),

\displaystyle\lvert F_{t}(z,u,v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq h_{F}(z)(1+\lVert u\rVert_{L^{2}}+\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}});

(iv)

for all
(t,\omega,z)
we have
a_{t}(z)-\frac{\alpha_{t}\alpha_{t}^{\top}}{2}\geq c_{a,\alpha}I_{d}
.

Here
I_{d}
is the identity matrix in
d
dimensions and for two matrices
A
,
B\in\mathbb{S}^{d}
we write
A\geq B
if
A-B
is positive semidefinite.

Note that Assumption
2.2
covers both BSPDE (
2.1
) and BSPDE (
2.2
). Of course, the regularity and boundedness condition on
\lambda
is not needed for the latter, as the coefficient
\lambda
does not enter this BSPDE.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. Then BSPDE (
2.1
) and BSPDE (
2.2
) have a unique strong solution
(u,q,m)
for which
u\in D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. Moreover, it holds that

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert q_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\bigl(1+\mathbb{E}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)

(2.4)

for a constant
C>0
that only depends on the coefficients and
T
.

2.2
Application to a McKean–Vlasov Control Problem with Killing at a State-Dependent Intensity

From now on we set
d=1
. Let us now assume that on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
there exists another one-dimensional Brownian motion
B
as well as two real-valued random variables
\xi
and
\zeta\geq 0
with joint distribution
\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, such that the quadruple
(\xi,\zeta,B,W)
is independent.
Then we consider the McKean–Vlasov SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{t}=\lambda(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

(2.5)

with initial conditions
X_{0}=\xi
,
\Lambda_{0}=\zeta
, and
conditional subprobability measure

\nu_{t}
defined through
\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\Lambda_{t}}\varphi(X_{t})|W]
for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
. The control process
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
takes values in some nonempty, compact, and convex subset
G
of
\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
with
d_{G}\geq 1
. We assume that
\gamma
is
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,\zeta,B,W}
-progressively measurable. Next, we introduce the
cost functional

J(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\Lambda_{t}}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]

(2.6)

and denote by
V
the infimum of
J
over control processes
\gamma
with the properties outlined above. We call
V
the
value
of the control problem. We refer to this control problem as the
strong formulation
for reasons that will become clear later on.

We studied the limit theory of a generalisation of this McKean–Vlasov control problem in a previous paper
[
HJ23
]
. In particular, we showed that the McKean–Vlasov control problem is the limit of a control problem for a suitable particle approximation of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) as the number of particles tends to infinity. What we are interested in here is whether we can replace the progressively measurable
open-loop control

\gamma_{t}
by a
semiclosed-loop control
of the form
g_{t}(X_{t})
, for a
\mathbb{F}^{W}
-progressively measurable random function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to G
, without increasing the value
V
. Now, if
x\mapsto g_{t}(x)
is only measurable, it is not clear if McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
), with the control
g
as input, admits a strong solution. Consequently, we need to work with a weakened formulation of the control problem. In the weaker formulation,
\nu_{t}
is the conditional subprobability with respect to a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{T}
for a filtration
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
that suitably extends that of the common noise
W
, and
g
may be progressively measurable with respect to
\mathbb{F}
instead of
\mathbb{F}^{W}
.

However, even if we admit this change, one might expect that the value over weak semiclosed-loop controls is larger than
V
unless we allow the “weak” semiclosed-loop controls to also depend on
\Lambda_{t}
. But as stated in the introduction, this is in fact not the case.
Before we make this more precise, let us discuss the coefficients of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) as well as the cost functions. For
p\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
denote the space of measures
v
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
v(\mathbb{R})\leq 1
and
M_{p}^{p}(v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert x\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}v(x)<\infty
. We equip
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
with the metric

d_{p}(v_{1},v_{2})=W_{p}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})+\lvert\nu_{1}(\mathbb{R})-\nu_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rvert,

(2.7)

where
\eta_{i}=v_{i}+(1-v_{i}(\mathbb{R}))\delta_{0}
,
W_{p}
denotes the
p
-Wasserstein distance for probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
, and
v_{1}
,
v_{2}\in\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
. By the Rubinstein-Kantorovich duality, we can rewrite
d_{1}
as
d_{1}(v_{1},v_{2})=\sup_{\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\text{Lip}}\leq 1}\langle v_{1}-v_{2},\varphi\rangle
, where for a Lipschitz continuous function
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
we denote by
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\text{Lip}}
the maximum between the Lipschitz constant of
\varphi
and
\lvert\varphi(0)\rvert
. This motivates us to further introduce the metric
d_{0}
defined as
d_{0}(v_{1},v_{2})=\sup_{\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\text{bLip}}\leq 1}\langle v_{1}-v_{2},\varphi\rangle
, where now
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\text{bLip}}
denotes the maximum between the Lipschitz constant and
\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty}
for a bounded Lipschitz continuous function
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

With this in mind, we let
b
and
f\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G\to\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma
and
\lambda\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\sigma_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\psi\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable functions. Further, we assume that there exist measurable maps
b_{0}
and
f_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
,
b_{1}
and
f_{1}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G\to\mathbb{R}
such that
b(t,x,v,g)=b_{0}(t,x,v)+b_{1}(t,x,g)
and
f(t,x,v,g)=f_{0}(t,x,v)+f_{1}(t,x,g)
for all
(t,x,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G\to\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption 2.4
.

We assume there exists constant
C>0
and
c>0
such that

(i)

the map
b_{1}
is continuous in
g
and for
(t,x,x^{\prime},v,v^{\prime},g)
and
h\in\{b_{1}(\cdot,\cdot,g),\sigma,\lambda\}
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b_{0}(t,x,v)-b_{0}(t,x^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\rvert+\lvert h(t,x)-h(t,x^{\prime})\rvert\leq C(\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert+d_{1}(v,v^{\prime}));

(ii)

the maps
(x,v)\mapsto f_{0}(t,x,v)
and
(x,g)\mapsto f_{1}(t,x,g)
are uniformly continuous for
(t,x,v,g)
in compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
, and
\psi
is continuous;

(iii)

the coefficients
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
are bounded by
C
and for all
(t,x,v,g)
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b_{0}(t,x,v)\rvert+\lvert b_{1}(t,x,g)\rvert+\lvert\lambda(t,x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}(v)+\lvert g\rvert),

\displaystyle\lvert f_{0}(t,x,v)\rvert+\lvert f_{1}(t,x,g)\rvert+\lvert\psi(v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{2}(\nu));

(iv)

for all
(t,x)
we have
\sigma^{2}(t,x)\geq c
;

(v)

for all
(t,x)
we have
\lambda(t,x)
if
x<0
and
\lambda(t,x)=0
otherwise.

Definition 2.5
.

A
weak setup
is a tuple
\mathbb{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{F},\xi,\zeta,B,W)
such that

(i)

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P})
is a complete filtered probability space and
\mathbb{F}
is a complete subfiltration of
\mathbb{G}
;

(ii)

\xi
and
\zeta
are
\mathcal{G}_{0}
-measurable random variables,
B
and
W
are
\mathbb{G}
-Brownian motions, and
W
is
\mathbb{F}
-adapted;

(iii)

\xi
,
\zeta
,
B
, and
\mathcal{F}_{T}
are independent and for
t\in[0,T]
we have
\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{F}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{F}_{T})
a.s. for any
A\in\mathcal{G}_{t}\lor\sigma(B)
.

By an
extension
of a weak setup
\mathbb{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{F},\xi,\zeta,B,W)
we mean a weak setup
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{\mathbb{G}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}},\tilde{\xi},\tilde{\zeta},\tilde{B},\tilde{W})
such that
\tilde{\Omega}=\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime}
,
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\prime}
,
\mathbb{G}=(\mathcal{G}_{t}\otimes\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, and
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t}\otimes\{\varnothing,\Omega^{\prime}\})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, where
(\mathcal{G}^{\prime}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a filtration on the measurable space
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
, and the random variables
\tilde{\xi}
,
\tilde{\zeta}
,
\tilde{B}
, and
\tilde{W}
on
\tilde{\Omega}
are defined by
\tilde{\xi}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\xi(\omega)
,
\tilde{\zeta}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=\zeta(\omega)
,
\tilde{B}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=B(\omega)
, and
\tilde{W}(\omega,\omega^{\prime})=W(\omega)
for
(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\in\tilde{\Omega}
. The probability
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}})
satisfies
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(A\times\Omega^{\prime})=\mathbb{P}(A)
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}
.

For a given weak setup
\mathbb{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{F},\xi,\zeta,B,W)
and every
\mathbb{G}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, referred to as an
\mathbb{S}
-admissible control
, we can consider the McKean–Vlasov SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{t}=\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

(2.8)

with initial conditions
X_{0}=\xi
,
\Lambda_{0}=\zeta
, and
\nu_{t}
defined through
\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\Lambda_{t}}\varphi(X_{t})|\mathcal{F}_{T}]
for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
. In
[
HJ23
, Proposition 3.12]
we proved that if Assumption
2.4
is in place, then McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
) has a unique strong solution for any
\mathbb{S}
-admissible control
\gamma
. We say that the control
\gamma
is a
semiclosed-loop control
if there exists an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to G
such that
\gamma_{t}=g_{t}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t})
. We call
g
the
feedback function
associated to
\gamma
.
Next, we define the
cost functional

J_{\mathbb{S}}(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\Lambda_{t}}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]

(2.9)

and the corresponding
value

V_{\mathbb{S}}
, i.e. the infimum of
J_{\mathbb{S}}
over all
\mathbb{S}
-admissible controls. We say that an
\mathbb{S}
-admissible control
\gamma
is
optimal
if
J_{\mathbb{S}}(\gamma)=V_{\mathbb{S}}
. According to
[
HJ23
, Appendix A.2]
, if Assumption
2.4
is in place, the value
V_{\mathbb{S}}
is independent of the setup
\mathbb{S}
and is, in fact, equal to
V
. We call this control problem the
weak formulation
.

With all this in mind, we can now formulate our central claim: there exists an optimal semiclosed-loop control
\gamma^{\ast}
on some weak setup
\mathbb{S}
, such that the corresponding feedback function
\tilde{g}^{\ast}
does not depend on the intensity, i.e. there exists an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random function
g^{\ast}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to G
such that
\tilde{g}^{\ast}_{t}(x,y)=g^{\ast}_{t}(x)
for a.e.
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\mathbb{P}\otimes\text{Leb}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.
In what follows, we will provide an outline that explains how and under what assumptions we prove this result.

Assumption 2.6
.

The map
g\mapsto b_{1}(t,x,g)
is linear and the map
g\mapsto f_{1}(t,x,g)
is convex for all
(t,x)
.

If Assumption
2.6
holds in addition to Assumption
2.4
, then
[
HJ23
, Proposition A.5]
states that there exists a weak setup
\mathbb{S}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{F},\xi,\zeta,B,W)
which admits an optimal
\mathbb{S}
-admissible semiclosed-loop control. We will fix this weak setup from now on.

If
g
is any admissible semiclosed-loop control on
\mathbb{S}
, then the
conditional joint law

\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})
of
X
and
\Lambda
satisfies the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation

\mathrm{d}\langle\mu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\mathcal{L}_{2}\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\mu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

(2.10)

for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with initial condition
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)
. Here
\mathcal{L}_{2}
denotes the generator of the two-dimensional process
(X,\Lambda)
which is given by

\mathcal{L}_{2}\varphi(t,x,y,v,h)=b(t,x,v,h)\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y)+\lambda(t,x,v)\partial_{y}\varphi(x,y)+a_{t}(x)\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x,y)

(2.11)

for
\varphi\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
(t,x,y,v,h)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
. The function
a\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by
a_{t}(x)=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{2}(t,x)+\sigma_{0}^{2}(t))
. Note, that
\nu_{t}
is determined by the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation, so that the SPDE (
2.10
) is nonlinear. Indeed, we can introduce the function
\mathcal{S}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))\to\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

\langle\mathcal{S}(m),\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}e^{-y}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}m(x,y)

(2.12)

for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
, which allows us to write
\nu_{t}=\mathcal{S}(\mu_{t})
.

Since on our chosen setup
\mathbb{S}
the infimum of
J_{\mathbb{S}}
over arbitrary
\mathbb{S}
-admissible controls and semiclosed-loop controls coincides, we can consider the control problem for McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
) from the point of view of the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation for
\mu=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. That is, we study the optimal control of SPDE (
2.10
). We will adopt
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
as a state space for this control problem. We justify this with Proposition
2.8
below. But first we need to introduce some stronger assumptions.

Assumption 2.7
.

The coefficients
b
,
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}
, and
\lambda
and costs
f
and
\psi
satisfy Assumption
2.4
and there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(i)

the initial distribution
\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)
has a density in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
;

(ii)

the coefficients
b_{0}
,
b_{1}
, and
\lambda
are bounded by
C
;

(iii)

for all
v
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
(t,x)
we have
\lvert b_{0}(t,x,v)-b_{0}(t,x,v^{\prime})\rvert\leq C\lVert v-v^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}}
.

In Assumption
2.7
we identified an element of
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
with its density in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. Let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{k}
denote the space of
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random functions
[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{k}\to G
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let Assumption
2.4
be satisfied. Then for any semiclosed-loop control
g
, the process
\mu=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
defined by
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})
solves SPDE (
2.10
).

Next, if Assumption
2.7
holds and
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
, then SPDE (
2.10
) has a unique strong solution
\mu=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with values in
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
such that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t})<\infty
and
\supp\mu_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
. Moreover,
\mathbb{P}
-a.s. the probability measure
\mu_{t}
has a density
\rho_{t}
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\rho=(\rho_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}\in C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
with

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert\rho_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\rho_{t}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\lVert\rho_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

(2.13)

for a constant
C>0
depending only on the coefficients and
T
.

Finally, for
g
and
\mu
as above, extending the weak setup if necessary, there exists a solution
(X,\Lambda)
of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
) with semiclosed-loop control
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
given by
\gamma_{t}=g_{t}(X_{t})
and such that
\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})=\mu_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
.

From now on we will no longer distinguish between
\mu_{t}
and its density and simply write
\mu\in C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

Note that the last statement of Proposition
2.8
implies that under Assumption
2.7
, up to changing the weak setup, any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
induces a semiclosed-loop control and vice versa. Thus, the control problem for the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation (
2.10
) with controls
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
, which we refer to as the
SPDE formulation
, is equivalent to the control problem for McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
), i.e. the weak formulation, with semiclosed-loop controls on an arbitrary weak setup. We can now introduce a cost functional
J_{\text{cl}}
for controls
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
defined by

J_{\text{cl}}(g)=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\tilde{f}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr],

(2.14)

where
\tilde{f}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
\tilde{f}(t,x,y,v,h)=e^{-y}f(t,x,v,h)
. We similarly define
\tilde{f}_{0}
and
\tilde{f}_{1}
. Note that because of the equivalence alluded to above, the feedback function
\tilde{g}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
of the optimal semiclosed-loop control
\gamma^{\ast}
, whose existence is guaranteed under Assumption

2.6
, is an optimal control for the SPDE formulation. That is,
J_{\text{cl}}(\tilde{g}^{\ast})=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}}J_{\text{cl}}(g)
.

For the control problem for the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation, we can derive a necessary stochastic maximum principle (SMP). Indeed, let
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
and let
\mu
denote the corresponding solution to SPDE (
2.10
) by
\mu
. Then we define the functions
\tilde{K}^{\mu}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times G\to\mathbb{R}
and
\tilde{F}^{\mu}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(x,y,p,h)=b(t,x,\nu_{t},h)p+\tilde{f}(t,x,y,\nu_{t},h)

for
(t,\omega,x,y,p)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\times G
and

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{t}(x,y,p)

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\mu_{t},e^{-y}Db_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)p\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle\mu_{t},e^{-y}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)\bigr\rangle

for
(t,\omega,x,y,p)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
. Here
D
denotes the linear functional derivative of a function
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, which we define next.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be closed and assume that
\mathcal{E}
is a closed and convex subset of
\mathcal{M}^{p}(E)
for
p\geq 1
. A map
F\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}
is said to have a
linear functional derivative
if there exists a continuous function
DF\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{E}\times E\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(i)

for any bounded subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{E}
the function
x\mapsto DF(m)(x)
has at most polynomial growth of order
p
in
x\in E
uniformly in
m\in\mathcal{K}
;

(ii)

for any
m
,
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}
it holds that

F(m)-F(m^{\prime})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{E}DF(tm+(1-t)m^{\prime})(x)\,\mathrm{d}(m-m^{\prime})(x)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(2.15)

With this at hand, we consider the adjoint process

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)&=-\Bigl(\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)+\lambda_{t}(x)\partial_{y}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)+\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ -\bigl(a_{t}(x)\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)+\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}\tilde{m}_{t}(x,y)\end{split}

(2.16)

with terminal condition
\tilde{u}_{T}(x,y)=e^{-y}D\psi(\nu_{T})(x)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty)
, where
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. Here we set
\lambda_{t}(x)=\lambda(t,x)
. Usually, we will assume that
\ell=0
, but for the proof of the SMP it is convenient to have a solution that is defined in an arbitrarily small extension of the interval
(0,\infty)
. We interpret BSPDE (
2.16
) in the sense of Definition
2.1
. Before we can state an existence result for this BSPDE, we have to impose additional assumptions.

Assumption 2.10
.

We assume that there exist
C>0
and
h_{b}
,
h_{f}
and
h_{\psi}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that

(i)

for all
(t,x,v,g)
we have
\lvert f_{0}(t,x,v)\rvert+\lvert f_{1}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq h_{f}(x)
;

(ii)

for all
(t,x)
the maps
v\mapsto b_{0}(t,x,v)
,
v\mapsto f_{0}(t,x,v)
, and
v\mapsto\psi(v)
have linear functional derivatives
Db_{0}
,
Df_{0}
and
D\psi
, which are measurable functions
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, respectively;

(iii)

for all
(t,x,v)
and
y
we have

\lvert Db_{0}(t,x,v)(y)\rvert\leq h_{b}(y),\qquad\lvert Df_{0}(t,x,v)(y)\rvert\leq h_{f}(y),\qquad\lvert D\psi(v)(y)\rvert\leq h_{\psi}(y).

Under Assumptions
2.7
and
2.10
the existence of solutions to BSPDE (
2.16
) is a simple application of Theorem
2.3
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
and
2.10
be satisfied. Then, for any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
, BSPDE (
2.16
) has a unique strong solution
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
.

Note that since
\mu_{t}(x,y)=0
if
y\leq 0
, the uniqueness implies that if we have two distinct boundaries
\ell<\ell^{\prime}\leq 0
but use the same control
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
, then the solutions to BSPDE (
2.16
) on the domains
\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty)
and
\mathbb{R}\times(\ell^{\prime},\infty)
coincide on the smaller half-plane
\mathbb{R}\times(\ell^{\prime},\infty)
. Hence, the choice of
\ell
is irrelevant and we will suppress it from now on.

Assumption 2.12
.

We assume that the drift coefficient
b_{1}
and the running cost
f_{1}
satisfy Assumption
2.6
and that there exists a
C>0
such that

(i)

for all
(t,x)
and
y
the maps
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto Db_{0}(t,x,v)(y)
,
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto Df_{0}(t,x,v)(y)
and
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto D\psi(v)(y)
are continuous with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
;

(ii)

there exists an extension of
f_{1}
(also denoted by
f_{1}
) to a measurable function
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times O\to\mathbb{R}
for some open set
O\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
that includes
G
, such that for all
(t,x)
the map
O\ni g\mapsto f_{1}(t,x,g)
is convex and continuously differentiable and for all
\partial_{g}f_{1}
is bounded by
C
.

We can now state the SMP.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.12
be satisfied. Suppose further that
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
is an optimal control and let
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
be a solution to BSPDE (
2.16
). Then it holds that

\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)=\inf_{h\in G}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),h\bigr)

(2.17)

for
\mu_{t}
-a.e.
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
and
\textup{Leb}\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.

We would like to use the SMP to derive a semilinear BSPDE from BSPDE (
2.16
) satisfied by the adjoint process. However, this is not completely straightforward, since the equality in (
2.17
) only holds
\mu_{t}
-a.e. and there is no guarantee that
\mu_{t}
is equivalent to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
. To deal with this issue, we need to modify
g_{t}
on the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\mathpunct{:}\mu_{t}(x,y)=0\}
. Of course, this has no impact on
\mu
, i.e.
\mu
will solve SPDE (
2.10
) with the modification
g^{\ast}
of
g
as an input, but the adjoint process corresponding to
g^{\ast}
may differ. To describe the modification, we have to consider the one-dimensional semilinear BSPDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}u_{t}(x)&=-\Bigl(a_{t}(x)\partial_{x}^{2}u_{t}(x)+H^{\nu}_{t}\bigl(x,u_{t}(x),\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)+F^{\nu}_{t}\bigl(x,\partial_{x}u_{t}\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ -\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}q_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t+q_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}m_{t}(x)\end{split}

(2.18)

with terminal condition
u_{T}(x)=D\psi(\nu_{T})(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Here, the functions
H\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
and
F\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
are given by

H^{\nu}_{t}(x,p,q)=\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(b(t,x,\nu_{t},h)q+f(t,x,\nu_{t},h)\bigr)-\lambda_{t}(x)p

(2.19)

for
(t,\omega,x,p,q)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
and

\displaystyle F^{\nu}_{t}(x,p)=\bigl\langle\nu_{t},Db_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)p\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle\nu_{t},Df_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)\bigr\rangle

for
(t,\omega,x,p)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. BSPDE (
2.18
) is understood in the sense of Definition
2.1
and under the following additional assumptions we can deduce existence and uniqueness of solutions from Theorem
2.3
.

Proposition 2.14
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
and
2.10
be satisfied. Then BSPDE (
2.18
) has a unique strong solution
(u,q,m)
.

Assumption
2.6
guarantees the existence of a measurable function
g_{\ast}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to G
such that
g_{\ast}(t,x,p)
is the minimiser of the map
h\mapsto b_{1}(t,x,h)p+f_{1}(t,x,h)
for
(t,x,p)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
.
Let us define the control
g^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
by

g^{\ast}_{t}(x,y)=\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{t}(x,y)>0}g_{t}(x,y)+\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{t}(x,y)=0}g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr).

(2.20)

Next, we introduce the function
\tilde{H}^{\mu}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{t}(x,y,p)=\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{t}(x,y)>0}\inf_{h\in G}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(x,y,p,h)+\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{t}(x,y)=0}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,p,g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\bigr).

(2.21)

Then, in view of Theorem
2.13
, if
g
and, therefore,
g^{\ast}
are optimal, then for the adjoint process
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
corresponding to
g^{\ast}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g^{\ast}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{t}(x,y)>0}\inf_{h\in G}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),h\bigr)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{t}(x,y)=0}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\Bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)\bigr),

so that
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
satisfies the semilinear BSPDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)&=-\Bigl(a_{t}(x)\partial_{x}^{2}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)+\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)+\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ -\bigl(\lambda_{t}(x)\partial_{y}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)+\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}\tilde{m}_{t}(x,y)\end{split}

(2.22)

with terminal condition
\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}D\psi(\nu_{T})(x)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
. Again, we interpret this BSPDE in the sense of Definition
2.1
.

Proposition 2.15
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
and
2.10
be satisfied and let
(u,q,m)
be the unique strong solution to BSPDE (
2.2
). Then the unique strong solution
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
of BSPDE (
2.22
) is given by
\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}u_{t}(x)
,
\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}q_{t}(x)
,
\tilde{m}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}m_{t}(x)
for
(t,\omega,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
.

Let us assume that
h\mapsto f_{1}(t,x,h)
is strictly convex, so that
h\mapsto b_{1}(t,x,h)p+f_{1}(t,x,h)
has a unique minimum in
G
for all
(t,x,p)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
. Then combining Proposition
2.15
with the necessary SMP, Equation (
2.17
), implies that on the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\mathpunct{:}\mu_{t}(x,y)>0\}
it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g^{\ast}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{h\in G}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),h\bigr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{h\in G}e^{-y}\bigl(b(t,x,\nu_{t},h)\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)+f(t,x,\nu_{t},h)\bigr)

for
\mathbb{P}\otimes\text{Leb}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
. The infimum on the right-hand side is uniquely attained at
g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)
, so we deduce
g^{\ast}_{t}(x,y)=g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)
. On the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)\mathpunct{:}\mu_{t}(x,y)=0\}
this equality holds by definition of
g^{\ast}
, so altogether we find
g^{\ast}_{t}(x,y)=g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
and
\mathbb{P}\otimes\text{Leb}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
. Hence, we have proved the following result.

Corollary 2.16
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.12
be satisfied and suppose that
h\mapsto f_{1}(t,x,h)
is strictly convex. Then there exists an optimal control
g^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
of the form
g^{\ast}_{t}(x)=g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{P}\otimes\text{Leb}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
, where
(u,q,m)
is the unique strong solution of BSPDE (
2.2
). In fact, every optimal control is of this form.

The assumptions of Corollary
2.16
are rather strong, particularly the assumption that the functions
x\mapsto f(t,x,v,g)
,
x^{\prime}\mapsto Db_{0}(t,x,v,g)(x^{\prime})
,
x^{\prime}\mapsto Df_{0}(t,x,v,g)(x^{\prime})
, and
x^{\prime}\mapsto D\psi(v)(x^{\prime})
are in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. However, if we relax them, Proposition
2.8
is no longer applicable, so we do not know whether SPDE (
2.10
) has a unique strong solution for any
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
. Hence, the cost functional
J_{\text{cl}}
is not necessarily defined for all
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
and, consequently, the SPDE formulation is not well-posed. Thus, if we weaken the assumptions, we have to revert back to the weak formulation. In that case we can establish the following theorem.

Theorem 2.17
.

Let Assumptions
2.4
and
2.6
be satisfied and suppose that
\mathcal{L}(\xi)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
\zeta=0
. Then, changing the weak setup if necessary, there exists an optimal semiclosed-loop control that does not depend on the intensity.

The proof of the theorem is based on tightness arguments, so changing the weak setup may be necessary to accommodate the subsequential limits of the tight sequence.

Note that if
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
and
\mu=(\mu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a (possibly weak) solution to SPDE (
2.10
) with input
g
, then
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
defined by
\nu_{t}=\mathcal{S}(\mu_{t})
satisfies the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation

\mathrm{d}\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\bigl\langle\nu_{t},\mathcal{L}\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\nu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

(2.23)

for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with initial condition
\nu_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi)
. The generator
\mathcal{L}
is defined by

\mathcal{L}\varphi(t,x,v,h)=b(t,x,v,h)\partial_{x}\varphi(x)-\lambda(t,x)\varphi(x)+a_{t}(x)\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x)

(2.24)

for
\varphi\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
(t,x,v,h)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
.
Hence, we can rewrite the cost

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\tilde{f}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu_{t},f(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr].

3
Semilinear BSPDEs with Càdlàg Noise

3.1
Càdlàg Martingales with Values in separable Hilbert Spaces

We recall a few notions from the theory of martingales with values in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces. For more information we refer to
[
Mét82
, Section 26]
. We fix a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
with inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
and induced norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}
. In Subsection
2.1
, we introduced the space
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
of
\mathcal{H}
-valued
\mathbb{F}
-martingales
m
with a.s. càdlàg trajectories for which
\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}
. We denote the
quadratic variation
of a martingale
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{G}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
by
[m]
and its
tensor quadratic variation
by
[[m]]
. The tensor quadratic variation is a process with values in the trace class operators from
\mathcal{H}
to itself and is symmetric as such. Moreover, it holds that
[m]_{t}=\trace[[m]]_{t}
. We can define the
quadratic covariation

[m^{1},m^{2}]
and
tensor quadratic covariation

[[m^{1},m^{2}]]
of two martingales
m^{1}
,
m^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{G}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
through polarisation. Note that the two martingales
m^{1}
,
m^{2}
are very strongly orthogonal as defined in Section
2.1
if and only if
[[m^{1},m^{2}]]=0
.

For
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{G}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
let
\mathcal{P}_{m}^{2}
denote the space of
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
\mathcal{H}
-valued processes such that
\int_{0}^{T}\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m]_{t}<\infty
. For
h\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{2}
we let
\int_{0}^{t}\langle h_{s},\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
be the stochastic integral of
h
against
m
over the interval
(0,t]
, i.e. we exclude the left endpoint. The indefinite stochastic integral
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle h_{s},\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
is a real-valued local martingale and its quadratic variation is given by
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\langle h_{s},h_{s}\cdot\mathrm{d}[[m]]_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
. Here again the integral excludes the left endpoint, and for a bounded operator
A\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
and an element
h\in\mathcal{H}
we write
h\cdot A
for
Ah
.
Since the operator norm of a symmetric trace class operator
A
is bounded from above by the trace, it holds that
\langle h,h\cdot A\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\lVert h\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\trace A
. We can apply this inequality to simple integrands
h\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{2}
of the form
h=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}h_{i}\mathbf{1}_{(t_{i},t_{i+1}]}
for
h_{i}
bounded and
\mathbb{F}_{t_{i}}
-measurable and
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{n}=T
to see
\int_{0}^{t}\langle h_{s},h_{s}\cdot\mathrm{d}[[m]]_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\lVert h_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m]_{s}
. Then, we extend this inequality to arbitrary
h\in\mathcal{P}_{m}^{2}
by density of the simple integrands. This implies the following Burkholder-Davis-Gundy type inequality.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
m\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{G}}^{2}([0,T];H)
and
h\in\mathcal{P}^{2}_{m}
. Then for all
p\geq 1
and any stopping time
\tau
with values in
[0,T]
we have

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\langle h_{s},\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert^{p}\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\tau}\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m]_{t}\biggr)^{p/2}

(3.1)

for a constant
C_{p}>0
independent of
m
,
h
, and
\tau
.

Proof.

By the classical Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality we have

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\langle h_{s},\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert^{p}\leq C_{p}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\tau}\langle h_{t},h_{t}\cdot\mathrm{d}[[m]]_{t}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr)^{p/2}.

Then the bound
\int_{0}^{t}\langle h_{s},h_{s}\cdot\mathrm{d}[[m]]_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\lVert h_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m]_{s}
allows us to conclude.
∎

3.2
Itô’s formula for Càdlàg Hilbert space-valued Semimartingales

Our next goal is to derive a generalisation of Itô’s formula which is suitable for our analysis of càdlàg semimartingales with values in Hilbert spaces. For a separable Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, we let
B_{2}(\mathcal{H})
be the space of Hilbert-Schmidt operators from
\mathcal{H}
to itself.

Theorem 3.2
.

For
i=1
, …,
k
, let
u^{i}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
\zeta^{i}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
m^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
such that

\langle u^{i}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i}_{s}(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}s+\langle m^{i}_{t},\varphi\rangle

for all
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then
u^{1}
, …,
u^{k}
lie in
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
(or
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}))
if
m^{1}
, …,
m^{k}
, respectively, have continuous trajectories).

Next, let
F\mathpunct{:}L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}
be a twice continuously Fréchet differentiable function and assume that for all
1\leq i,j\leq k
it holds that

(i)

for each
h\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the derivative
D_{i}F(h)
is an element of
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the map
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
h\mapsto D_{i}F(h)
is uniformly continuous on bounded sets of
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
;

(ii)

for each
h\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the second derivative
D_{ij}^{2}F(h)
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator and the map
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to B_{2}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
h\mapsto D_{ij}^{2}F(h)
is uniformly continuous on bounded sets of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Then for any
t\in[0,T]
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}F(u_{t})&=F(m_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i}_{s}(D_{i}F(u_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle D_{i}F(u_{s-}),\,\mathrm{d}m^{i}_{s}\rangle\biggr)\\
&\ \ \ +\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(D^{2}_{ij}F(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{i},m^{j}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)\\
&\ \ \ +\sum_{0<s\leq t}\biggl(F(u_{s-}+\Delta m_{s})-F(u_{s-})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle D_{i}F(u_{s-}),\Delta m^{i}_{s}\rangle\biggr).\end{split}

(3.2)

Here
\Delta m^{\epsilon}_{s}
denotes the jump of
m^{\epsilon}
at time
s
, not the Laplacian.

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

We proceed in two steps.

Step 1
: First, we assume that
\zeta^{i}
is an element of
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
for
i=1
, …,
k
, so that
u^{i}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+m^{i}_{t}
, where
\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s
is understood as a Bochner integral. Then
u^{1}
, …,
u^{k}
are
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-valued càdlàg semimartingales, so we simply apply
[
Mét82
, Theorem 27.2]
, which gives precisely (
3.2
).

Step 2
: Now, we let
\zeta^{i}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. We will reduce to the previous case by mollification. Let
\eta\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be the standard mollifier defined by
\eta(x)=c_{d}e^{-1/(1-\lvert x\rvert^{2})}
if
x
is in the interior of the centered ball of radius
1
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and zero otherwise, and set
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d}}\eta(x/\epsilon)
. Here
c_{d}>0
is a normalisation constant which ensures that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
. For a function
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we let
(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi)(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\epsilon}(x-y)\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}y
. Then we set
u^{i,\epsilon}_{t}=\eta_{\epsilon}\ast u^{i}_{t}
, and
m^{i,\epsilon}_{t}=\eta_{\epsilon}\ast m^{i}_{t}
, and define
\zeta^{i,\epsilon}
via
\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{t}(\varphi)=\zeta^{i}_{t}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi)
. With this we obtain

\mathrm{d}\langle u^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathrm{d}\langle u^{i}_{t},\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi\rangle=\zeta^{i}_{t}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\langle m^{i}_{t},\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi\rangle=\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{t}(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\langle m^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle

(3.3)

for
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Note that for any
\xi\in H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
it holds that
\lvert\xi(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi)\rvert\leq\lVert\xi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lVert\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\varphi\rVert_{H^{1}}\leq C_{\epsilon,d}\lVert\partial_{x}\eta_{\epsilon}\rVert_{\infty}\lVert\xi\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lVert\varphi\rVert_{L^{2}}
for some constant
C_{\epsilon,d}>0
. Hence, the mollification of
\xi
is a bounded linear functional on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, so we may identify it with an element of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Consequently, we may view
\zeta^{i}
as a process in
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Thus, by Step 1 it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}F(u^{\epsilon}_{t})&=F(m^{\epsilon}_{0})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\biggl(\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{s}(D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\langle D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}),\,\mathrm{d}m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\biggr)\\
&\ \ \ +\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(D^{2}_{ij}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{i,\epsilon},m^{j,\epsilon}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)\\
&\ \ \ +\sum_{0<s\leq t}\biggl(F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}+\Delta m^{\epsilon}_{s})-F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}),\Delta m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\biggr).\end{split}

(3.4)

Our goal is to pass to the limit
\epsilon\to 0
in Equation (
3.4
). However, while the sequences
(u^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
,
(\zeta^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
, and
(m^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
converge in their respective Hilbert spaces
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
,
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, and
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we do not even know whether
(u^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
also converges as a sequence in
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. But this is certainly necessary to pass to the limit in the stochastic integral term
\int_{0}^{t}\langle D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}),\,\mathrm{d}m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle
and to make sense of its limit
\int_{0}^{t}\langle D_{i}F(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{i}_{s}\rangle
.

To deduce the convergence in
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, we first apply (
3.4
) to
u^{\epsilon,\delta}=u^{\epsilon}-u^{\delta}
for the specific choice
F(\varphi)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lVert\varphi^{i}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
. This implies that for any
\epsilon
,
\delta>0
that

\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\biggl(\lVert m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}2\zeta^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}(u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}2\bigl\langle u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s-},\,\mathrm{d}m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr\rangle+[m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}]_{t}\biggr),

(3.5)

where
\zeta^{i,\epsilon,\delta}
and
m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}
are defined in the obvious way. We take the supremum over
t\in[0,T]
and then expectation on both sides. Further, we estimate with Proposition
3.1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}2\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s-},\,\mathrm{d}m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr\rangle\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{1}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t-}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}]_{t}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{1}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\sup_{0<t\leq T}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t-}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}[m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}]_{T}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+8C_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}[m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}]_{T}.

Inserting this into (
3.5
) and rearranging gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\biggl(

\displaystyle\lVert m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{0}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}2\lVert\zeta^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\rVert_{H^{-1}}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\rVert_{H^{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+(2C_{1}+1)\mathbb{E}[m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}]_{T}\biggr).

The right-hand side clearly tends to zero as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
, which means that
(u^{i,\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
, for
i=1
, …,
k
, is a Cauchy sequence in the space
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Hence, it has a unique limit, which must coincide with
u
, since
u^{\epsilon}\to u
in
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. Consequently, we have
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{\epsilon}_{t}-u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\to 0
and
u
lies in
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

Let us return to Equation (
3.4
). We can clearly pass to the limit in probability in the terms
F(u^{\epsilon}_{t})
,
F(m^{\epsilon}_{0})
, and
\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{s}(D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s
resulting in the expressions
F(u_{t})
,
F(m_{0})
, and
\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i}_{s}(D_{i}F(u_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s
. Here we make use of Assumption
(i)
. Next, by Lemma
A.1
, the stochastic integrals and the quadratic variation terms converge to
\int_{0}^{t}\langle D_{i}F(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{i}_{s}\rangle
and
\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(D_{ij}^{2}F(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{i},m^{j}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)
, respectively, in probability as
\epsilon\to 0
. This leaves the jump terms. First note that the jumps of
m^{\epsilon}
and
m
align perfectly and, moreover, it holds that
\lVert\Delta m^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert\Delta m_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}
. We can use Taylor’s theorem to bound
F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}+\Delta m^{\epsilon}_{s})-F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}),\Delta m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle
by

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\bigl\langle\Delta m^{i,\epsilon}_{s},D_{ij}^{2}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})m^{j,\epsilon}_{s}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{\lVert D_{ij}^{2}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})\rVert_{B_{2}}}{2}\bigl(\lVert\Delta m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\Delta m^{j,\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{\lVert D_{ij}^{2}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})\rVert_{B_{2}}}{2}\bigl(\lVert\Delta m^{i}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\Delta m^{j}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr),

where
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{B_{2}}
denotes the Hilbert-Schmidt norm. From above we know
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0<s\leq t}\lVert u^{\epsilon}_{s-}-u_{s-}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\to 0
, so since
D_{ij}^{2}F
is bounded on bounded sets of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as a Hilbert-Schmidt operator by Assumption
(ii)
, we deduce that
\lVert D_{ij}^{2}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})\rVert_{B_{2}}
is bounded by some random constant
C>0
uniformly in
\epsilon
. Hence, we can upper bound
F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}+\Delta m^{\epsilon}_{s})-F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\langle D_{i}F(u^{\epsilon}_{s-}),\Delta m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle
by the a.s. summable expression
Ck\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lVert\Delta m^{i}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Thus, we can apply the dominated convergence theorem to pass to the a.s. limit in the sum in the third line of Equation (
3.4
). Putting all convergences together yields the desired formula (
3.2
).
∎

Note that Theorem
3.2
is not applicable to solutions
(u,q,m)
of BSPDE (
2.1
), since in general we would not expect
u
to have a weak derivative in
y
. Consequently, we need a generalisation of Theorem
3.2
to semimartingales in
L^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(D))
, where
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
for
\ell\in\mathbb{R}
. For a separable Banach space
\mathcal{B}
with norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}
we let
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{\infty}([0,T];\mathcal{B})
be the space of
\mathcal{B}
-valued
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable process
h
such that
\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{B}}
is
\text{Leb}\otimes\mathbb{P}
-essentially bounded.

Theorem 3.3
.

For
i=1
,
2
, let
u^{i}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(D))
,
\zeta^{i}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{-1,0}(D))
,
m^{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
, and
h\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, where
h
is nonnegative,
such that

(i)

for
\leb\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
we have
\supp u^{1}_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[\ell+\theta,\infty)
for some
\theta>0
;

(ii)

it holds that

\langle u^{i}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\zeta^{i}_{s}(\varphi)+\langle u^{i}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s+\langle m^{i}_{t},\varphi\rangle

(3.6)

for all
\varphi\in H^{1}(D)
if
i=1
and for all
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(D)
if
i=2
.

Then
u^{1}
and
u^{2}
are elements of
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
(or
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
if
m^{1}
and
m^{2}
, respectively, have continuous trajectories) and for any
t\in[0,T]
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle u^{i}_{t},u^{j}_{t}\rangle&=\langle m^{i}_{0},m^{j}_{0}\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i}_{s}(u^{2}_{s})+\zeta^{2}_{s}(u^{j}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\langle u^{i}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{i}_{s}\rangle+\langle u^{j}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{j}_{s}\rangle\bigr)\\
&\ \ \ +[m^{i},m^{j}]_{t}+2\mathbf{1}_{i=j=2}\xi_{t},\end{split}

(3.7)

for some nondecreasing real-valued process
\xi=(\xi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with
\mathbb{E}\xi_{t}<\infty
started from zero.

Note that the coefficient
h
in the statement of Theorem
3.3
is a random function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and not
D
, so it is independent of the last variable. Hence, the expression
\langle u^{i}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle
stands for the integral
\int_{D}u^{i}_{s}(x,y)h_{s}(x)\partial_{y}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y
.

Proof.

We proceed in two steps.

Step 1
: First, we assume that for
i=2
instead of (
3.6
), it holds that

\langle u^{2}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\zeta^{2}_{s}(\varphi)+\langle u^{2}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle+\langle\xi_{s},\varphi\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s+\langle m^{2}_{t},\varphi\rangle

for all
\varphi\in H^{1}(D)
, where
\xi\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
satisfies
\supp\xi_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[\ell,\ell+\theta/2]
for
\leb\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
. Let
\eta\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the standard mollifier in one dimension defined by
\eta(x)=ce^{-1/(1-x^{2})}
if
x\in(-1,1)
and zero otherwise, and set
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\eta(x/\epsilon)
. Here
c>0
is a normalisation constant which ensures that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=1
. For a function
\varphi\in L^{2}(D)
we let
(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi)(x,y)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta_{\epsilon}(x,y-z)\varphi(x,z)\,\mathrm{d}z
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
be the mollification in the second component, where
\varphi\mathpunct{:}D\to\mathbb{R}
is extended to a function on all of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
by zero. Then, we set
u^{i,\epsilon}_{t}=\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{i}_{t}
,
m^{i,\epsilon}_{t}=\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}m^{i}_{t}
,
\xi^{\epsilon}_{t}=\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\xi_{t}
, and define
\zeta^{i,\epsilon}
via
\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{t}(\varphi)=\zeta^{i}_{t}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi)+\bigl\langle u^{i}_{t},h_{t}\partial_{y}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi)\bigr\rangle+\delta_{i2}\langle\xi^{\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle
. With this we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\langle u^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\langle u^{i}_{t},\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\zeta^{i}_{t}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi)+\bigl\langle u^{i}_{t},h_{t}\partial_{y}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi)\bigr\rangle+\delta_{i2}\langle\xi,\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi\rangle\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\langle m^{i}_{t},\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{t}(\varphi)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}\langle m^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle

for
\varphi\in H^{1}(D)
. Note that
u^{i,\epsilon}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R})
,
\zeta^{i,\epsilon}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{-1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}))
, and
m^{i,\epsilon}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}))
, so we can apply Theorem
3.2
, whereby
u^{i,\epsilon}
is an element of
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}))
. Moreover, applying (
3.2
) with the choice
F(v)=\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
to the process
u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}=u^{i,\epsilon}-u^{i,\delta}
yields

\displaystyle\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}=\lVert m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}2\zeta^{i,\epsilon,\delta}(u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}2\bigl\langle u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s-},\,\mathrm{d}m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr\rangle+[m^{i,\epsilon,\delta}]_{t}.

Now, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bigl\langle u^{i}_{s},h_{s}\bigl(\partial_{y}(\eta_{\epsilon}-\eta_{\delta})\ast_{2}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr)\bigr\rangle&=-\bigl\langle\partial_{y}(\eta_{\epsilon}-\eta_{\delta})\ast_{2}u^{i}_{s},h_{s}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr\rangle=-\bigl\langle\partial_{y}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s},h_{s}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr\rangle=0,\end{split}

(3.8)

where we used in the first equality that
\partial_{y}(\eta_{\epsilon}-\eta_{\delta})
is an odd function and
h_{s}
is independent of the last variable
y
.
Hence, it holds that
