\displaystyle\zeta^{i,\epsilon,\delta}(u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s})

\displaystyle=\zeta^{i}_{s}\bigl((\eta_{\epsilon}-\eta_{\delta})\ast_{2}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr)+\bigl\langle u^{i}_{s},h_{s}\bigl(\partial_{y}(\eta_{\epsilon}-\eta_{\delta})\ast_{2}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr)\bigr\rangle\delta_{i2}\langle\xi^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s},u^{2,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rangle

\displaystyle=\zeta^{i}_{s}\bigl((\eta_{\epsilon}-\eta_{\delta})\ast_{2}u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\bigr)+\delta_{i2}\langle\xi^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s},u^{2,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rangle,

so we can estimate
\bigl\lvert\zeta^{i,\epsilon,\delta}(u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\bigr\rvert\leq 2\lVert\zeta^{i}_{s}\rVert_{H^{-1,0}}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}+\delta_{i2}\lVert\xi^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}
. The right-hand side tends to zero as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
. Hence, as in the proof of Theorem
3.2
, we conclude that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{i,\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\to 0
as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
. Consequently,
(u^{i,\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
is a Cauchy sequence in the space
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}))
and its limit in
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}))
is
u^{i}
.

Next, let us deduce Equation (
3.7
). For that we apply Itô’s formula (
3.2
) with the choice
F(v^{1},v^{2})=\langle v^{1},v^{2}\rangle
to the process
(u^{i,\epsilon},u^{j,\epsilon})
for
\epsilon<\theta/4
, whereby

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle u^{i,\epsilon}_{t},u^{j,\epsilon}_{t}\rangle&=\langle m^{i,\epsilon}_{0},m^{j,\epsilon}_{0}\rangle+\int_{0}^{t}\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{s}(u^{j,\epsilon}_{s})+\zeta^{j,\epsilon}_{s}(u^{i,\epsilon}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\langle u^{j,\epsilon}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle+\langle u^{i,\epsilon}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{j,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s+[m^{i,\epsilon},m^{j,\epsilon}]_{t}.\end{split}

(3.9)

Similarly to Equation (
3.8
), we compute

\displaystyle\bigl\langle u^{i}_{s},h_{s}(\partial_{y}\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{j,\epsilon}_{s})\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle u^{j}_{s},h_{s}(\partial_{y}\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{i,\epsilon}_{s})\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{i}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}u^{j,\epsilon}_{s})\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle\partial_{y}\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{j}_{s},h_{s}u^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle u^{i,\epsilon}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}u^{j,\epsilon}_{s}\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle\partial_{y}u^{j,\epsilon}_{s},h_{s}u^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

where we used in the first equality that
\eta_{\epsilon}
is even,
\partial_{y}\eta_{\epsilon}
is odd, and that
h_{s}
does not depend on the last variable
y
. Further, if
i
or
j
are different from
2
, then since
\xi^{\epsilon}_{t}
is supported in
\mathbb{R}\times[\ell,\ell+3\theta/4)
and
u^{1,\epsilon}_{t}
is supported in
\mathbb{R}\times(\ell+3\theta/4,\infty)
, we have
\delta_{2i}\langle\xi^{\epsilon}_{s},u^{j,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle+\delta_{2j}\langle\xi^{\epsilon}_{s},u^{i,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle=0
. Consequently, we get that

\zeta^{i,\epsilon}_{s}(u^{j,\epsilon}_{s})+\zeta^{j,\epsilon}_{s}(u^{i,\epsilon}_{s})=\zeta^{i}_{s}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{j,\epsilon}_{s})+\zeta^{j}_{s}(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast_{2}u^{i,\epsilon}_{s})+2\mathbf{1}_{i=j=1}\langle\xi^{\epsilon}_{s},u^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle.

This together with Lemma
A.1
allows us to pass to the limit in probability in Equation (
3.9
) as
\epsilon\to 0
. As a result, we obtain (
3.7
) with
\xi_{t}
replaced by
\int_{0}^{t}2\langle\xi_{s},u^{2}_{s}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s
. Note that in contrast to (
3.7
) this process is not necessarily nondecreasing.

Step 2
: Let us define
\kappa_{\epsilon}
by
\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)=\int_{-\infty}^{x}\eta_{\epsilon}(z-\ell-\epsilon)\,\mathrm{d}z
, so that
\kappa_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)=0
for
y\in(-\infty,\ell]
,
\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)\in[0,1]
for
y\in[\ell,\ell+2\epsilon)
, and
\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)=1
for
y\in[\ell+2\epsilon,\infty)
. We set
u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}(x,y)=\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)u^{2}_{t}(x,y)
,
m^{2,\epsilon}_{t}(x,y)=\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)m^{2}_{t}(x,y)
, and define
\zeta^{2,\epsilon}_{t}
via
\zeta^{2,\epsilon}_{t}(\varphi)=\zeta^{2}_{t}(\varphi_{\epsilon})
, where
\varphi_{\epsilon}(x,y)=\kappa_{\epsilon}(y)\varphi(x,y)
for
\varphi\in H^{1}(D)
. This implies that

\langle u^{2,\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\zeta^{2,\epsilon}_{s}(\varphi)+\langle u^{2,\epsilon}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle+\langle u^{2}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\kappa_{\epsilon}\varphi\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s+\langle m^{2,\epsilon}_{s},\varphi\rangle

for
\varphi\in H^{1}(D)
. Thus,
u^{2,\epsilon}
satisfies the assumptions of Step 1 with
\xi_{t}=u^{2}_{t}h_{t}\partial_{y}\kappa_{\epsilon}
, so that
u^{2,\epsilon}
is an element of
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D)
, and (
3.7
) holds with the process
\xi
replaced by
\int_{0}^{t}\langle u^{2}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\kappa_{\epsilon}u^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s
. Hence, we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}2\bigl(\zeta^{2,\epsilon}_{s}(u^{2,\epsilon}_{s})+\langle u^{2}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\kappa_{\epsilon}u^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{t}^{T}2\langle u^{2,\epsilon}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle+[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{T}-[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{t}.

Since
\langle u^{2}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\kappa_{\epsilon}u^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle
and
[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{T}-[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{t}
are nonnegative, we can bound

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}2\zeta^{2,\epsilon}_{s}(u^{2,\epsilon}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}4\int_{0}^{t}\langle u^{2,\epsilon}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle.

(3.10)

We take expectations on both sides, use Proposition
3.1
to estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}4\int_{0}^{t}\langle u^{2,\epsilon}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle

\displaystyle\leq 4C_{1}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t-}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{t}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+8C_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{T},

and then rearrange (
3.10
) to find

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq 2\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+4\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\zeta^{2,\epsilon}_{t}(u^{2,\epsilon}_{t})\rvert\,\mathrm{d}t+16C_{1}^{2}\mathbb{E}[m^{2,\epsilon}]_{T}.

The sequence
(\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2})_{\epsilon>0}
increases to
\lVert u^{2}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
as
\epsilon\to 0
, so the left-hand side above converges by the monotone convergence theorem. For the right-hand side convergence follows from the assumptions on
u^{2}
,
u^{2}_{T}
,
\zeta^{2}
, and
m^{2}
, so we find that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{2}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}<\infty
. Thus,

\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{2,\epsilon}_{t}-u^{2}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert(1-\eta_{\epsilon})u^{2}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\to 0

by the monotone convergence theorem. In particular,
u^{2}
is an element of
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. Obtaining Equation (
3.7
) is now a simple limit procedure. Note that
\xi_{t}
is the
L^{1}
-limit of the integral
\int_{0}^{t}\langle u^{2}_{s},h_{s}\partial_{y}\kappa_{\epsilon}u^{2,\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s
, which is nondecreasing as a function of
t
, so that the same is true for
\xi=(\xi_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
.
∎

3.3
Semilinear BSPDE

In this subsection, we prove the existence and uniqueness for the semilinear BSPDEs introduced in Section
2.1
. The proofs for the two domains
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
and
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}
are rather similar, so we focus on
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
, in which case a few more technicalities arise. We obtain existence and uniqueness to a solution to BSPDE (
2.1
) through a fixed point argument, so we first have to consider linear BSPDEs of the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}u_{t}(x,y)&=-\Bigl(a_{t}(x,y):\nabla_{x}^{2}u_{t}(x,y)+\alpha_{t}:\nabla_{x}q_{t}(x,y)+\beta_{t}(x,y)\cdot q_{t}(x,y)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ -\bigl(\lambda_{t}(x)\partial_{y}u_{t}(x,y)+h_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+q_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}m_{t}(x,y)\end{split}

(3.11)

for
(x,y)\in D
with terminal condition
u_{T}(x,y)=\psi(x,y)
for a random function
\psi\in L^{2}\bigl(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};L^{2}(D)\bigr)
. The process
h
lies in
L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. A solution
(u,q,m)\in\mathbb{H}(D)
is understood in the sense of Definition
2.1
, so the test functions lie in the space
H^{1}_{0}(D)
. We construct a solution to BSPDE (
3.11
) through a Galerkin approximation.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied and let
h\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. Then BSPDE (
3.11
) has a unique solution
(u,q,m)
for which
u\in D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. Moreover, for any
t\in[0,T]
, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}-m_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}&\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}u_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert q_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq C\biggl(\mathbb{E}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle u_{s},h_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr)\end{split}

(3.12)

for a constant
C>0
independent of
h
and
t
.

Proof.

Existence
: Our arguments mirror those given in the proof of
[
MY99
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
Al-09
, Lemma 4.5]
. The main difference lies in the fact that BSPDE (
3.11
) is not defined on the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}
but only on the half-plane
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
.

Let us fix an orthonormal basis
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
L^{2}(D)
consisting of elements in
C^{\infty}_{c}(D)
, such that the linear span of
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
L^{2}(D)
is dense in
H^{1}_{0}(D)
. Note that it is not possible to choose
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
from
C^{\infty}_{c}(D)
with a linear span that is dense in
H^{1}(D)
, since limits in
H^{1}(D)
of functions in
C^{\infty}_{c}(D)
vanish on
\mathbb{R}\times\{\ell\}
. This explains why the test functions in Definition
2.1
are from
H^{1}_{0}(D)
. Now, for
n\geq 1
we consider the BSDE

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u^{ni}_{t}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Bigl(\bigl\langle a_{t}:\nabla_{x}^{2}e_{j}+\lambda_{t}\partial_{y}e_{j},e_{i}\bigr\rangle u^{nj}_{t}+\langle\alpha_{t}:\nabla_{x}e_{j}+\beta_{t}\cdot e_{j},e_{i}\rangle q^{nj}_{t}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\langle h_{t},e_{i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+q^{ni}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}m^{ni}_{t}

with terminal condition
u^{ni}_{T}=\langle\psi,e_{i}\rangle
for
1\leq i\leq n
. The existence of an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable solution
u^{ni}\in D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T])
,
q^{ni}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
m^{ni}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T])
for
1\leq i\leq n
is guaranteed by
[
CD18
, Example 1.20]
. Next, we define
u^{n}\in D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{2}(D))
by
u^{n}_{t}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{ni}_{t}(x,y)e_{i}(x,y)
and, similarly,
q^{n}\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{2}(D;\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and
m^{n}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{2}(D))
. Then let
\pi_{n}
denote the orthogonal projection of
L^{2}(D)
onto the linear span of
e_{1}
, …,
e_{n}
, which keeps
u^{n}_{t}
,
q^{n}_{t}
, and
m^{n}_{t}
fixed. This allows us to rewrite the finite-dimensional linear BSDE above in the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}u^{n}_{t}(x,y)&=-\pi_{n}\Bigl(a_{t}:\nabla_{x}^{2}u^{n}_{t}+\lambda_{t}\partial_{y}u^{n}_{t}+h_{t}+\alpha_{t}:\nabla_{x}q^{n}_{t}+\beta_{t}\cdot q^{n}_{t}\Bigr)(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +q^{n}_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{t}(x,y)\end{split}

(3.13)

for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty)
with terminal condition
u^{n}_{T}(x,y)=(\pi_{n}\psi)(x,y)
.

Next, we apply the generalisation of Itô’s formula from Theorem
3.2
for Hilbert space-valued semimartingales with jumps to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lVert u^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}&=\lVert\pi_{n}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}2\Bigl(\bigl\langle\nabla_{x}\cdot(a_{s}\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s})+\beta_{s}\cdot q^{n}_{s},u^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle a_{s}\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}+\alpha_{s}q^{n}_{s},\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\ \ \ +\int_{t}^{T}\langle h_{s},u^{n}_{s}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{t}^{T}2\langle q^{n}_{s},u^{n}_{s}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\int_{t}^{T}2\langle u^{n}_{s-},\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\rangle\\
&\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\lVert q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s-([m^{n}]_{T}-[m^{n}]_{t}),\end{split}

(3.14)

where we use that
2\langle\lambda_{s}\partial_{y}u^{n}_{s},u^{n}_{s}\rangle=\langle\partial_{y}\lvert u^{n}_{s}\rvert^{2},\lambda_{s}\rangle=0
because
\lambda_{s}
does not depend on
y
and
u^{n}_{s}(x,y)
vanishes for
y=\ell
. The stochastic integrals appearing in (
3.14
) are true martingales by Proposition
3.1
, so disappear upon taking expectations on both sides. Then we apply the same estimates as in the proof of
[
MY99
, Theorem 3.1]
to find that for any
\delta>0
it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}&\leq\mathbb{E}\lVert\pi_{n}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl(\delta\lVert\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\tfrac{C_{\alpha}^{2}+C_{\beta}^{2}+\delta}{\delta}\lVert u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\delta\lVert q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert h_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\ \ \ -\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl(2\lVert\sigma_{s}^{\top}\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\alpha_{s}^{\top}\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}+q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s-\mathbb{E}([m^{n}]_{T}-[m^{n}]_{t}),\end{split}

(3.15)

where
\sigma_{s}(x,y)\sigma_{s}^{\top}(x,y)=a_{s}(x,y)-\tfrac{\alpha_{s}\alpha_{s}^{\top}}{2}
satisfies
\sigma_{s}(x,y)\sigma_{s}^{\top}(x,y)\geq c_{a,\alpha}I_{d}
by Assumption
2.2

(iv)
. For any
\eta\in(0,1)
we have

-\lVert\alpha_{s}^{\top}\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}+q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{(1-\eta)\lvert\alpha_{s}\rvert^{2}}{\eta}\lVert\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-(1-\eta)\lVert q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Now, we first choose
\eta
sufficiently close to
1
such that
\frac{(1-\eta)\lvert\alpha_{s}\rvert^{2}}{\eta}\leq\frac{c_{a,\alpha}}{2}
, then we fix
\delta=\tfrac{c_{a,\alpha}}{2}\land\frac{1-\eta}{2}
. In view of Equation (
3.15
) this yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}([m^{n}]_{T}-[

\displaystyle m^{n}]_{t})+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\lVert\pi_{n}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert h_{s}\rVert+\lVert u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

for a positive constant
C>0
independent of
n\geq 1
and
h
. Now, by Grönwall’s inequality, we can deduce that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert m^{n}_{T}-m^{n}_{t}&\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert q^{n}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq e^{CT}\mathbb{E}\lVert\pi_{n}\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+e^{CT}\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert h_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(3.16)

where we used that
\mathbb{E}([m^{n}]_{T}-[m^{n}]_{t})=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\lVert m^{n}_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}-\lVert m^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)=\mathbb{E}\lVert m^{n}_{T}-m^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
because as a martingale
m^{n}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}\langle m^{n}_{T},m^{n}_{t}\rangle=\mathbb{E}\langle m^{n}_{t},m^{n}_{t}\rangle=\mathbb{E}\lVert m^{n}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Consequently, the sequence
(u^{n},q^{n},m^{n})_{n}
is bounded in
\mathbb{H}(D)
and, consequently, has a weakly convergent subsequence. Then we can proceed as in the proofs of
[
MY99
, Theorem 3.1]
and
[
Al-09
, Lemma 4.5]
to show that any weak limit
(u,q,m)\in\mathbb{H}(D)
is a solution of BSPDE (
3.11
). We will not provide the details and instead refer the reader to the cited references. Note that the test functions for the solutions lie in the closure of the linear of span of
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
H^{1}(D)
. By our choice of
(e_{n})_{n\geq 1}
this closure is given by
H^{1}_{0}(D)
, as desired.

Uniqueness
: To prove uniqueness for BSPDE (
3.11
), it is convenient to first establish (
3.12
) for arbitrary solutions to BSPDE (
3.11
).
So let us fix a solution
(u,q,m)
of BSPDE (
3.11
). We apply the generalisation of Itô’s formula from Theorem
3.3
to
u
, using that
\lambda
is nonnegative, to obtain

\displaystyle\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{t}^{T}2\Bigl(\bigl\langle\nabla_{x}\cdot(a_{s}\nabla_{x}u_{s})+\beta_{s}\cdot q_{s}+h_{s},u_{s}\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle a_{s}\nabla_{x}u_{s}+\alpha_{s}q_{s},\nabla_{x}u_{s}\bigr\rangle\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}2\langle q_{s},u_{s}\rangle\cdot\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\int_{t}^{T}2\langle u_{s-},\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle-\int_{t}^{T}\lVert q_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s-([m]_{T}-[m]_{t}),

where we use that the process
\xi
in (
3.7
) is nonnegative. Note that Theorem
3.3
also tells us that
u
must be an element of
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. Then, we employ the same arguments as above to deduce the inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle([m]_{T}-[m]_{t})+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}u_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert q_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle h_{s},u_{s}\rangle\rvert+\lVert u_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

for a positive constant
C>0
independent of
h
. Then a simple application of Grönwall’s inequality yields the estimate (
3.12
) for
(u,q,m)
. Now, if
(u^{\prime},q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
is another solution to BSPDE (
3.11
) with the same terminal condition
\psi
and input
h
, then the difference
(u-u^{\prime},q-q^{\prime},m-m^{\prime})
satisfies BSPDE (
3.11
) with vanishing initial condition and input. Hence, the just established bound from (
3.12
) immediately implies that the difference
(u-u^{\prime},q-q^{\prime},m-m^{\prime})
vanishes. Thus, BSPDE (
3.11
) has a unique solution.
∎

We can use Proposition
3.4
and, in particular, the estimate (
3.12
) to obtain a solution to BSPDE (
2.1
) via a fixed point argument. Let us note again that we will only prove Theorem
2.3
for the case
D=\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\ell,\infty)
.

Proof of Theorem
2.3
.

We construct a map
\Phi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{H}(D)\to\mathbb{H}(D)
as follows: for any
(u,q,m)\in\mathbb{H}(D)
we define the random function
h^{u}\in L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
by

\displaystyle h^{u}_{t}(x,y)=H_{t}\bigl(x,y,u_{t}(x,y),\nabla_{x}u_{t}(x,y)\bigr)+F_{t}\bigl(x,y,u_{t},\nabla_{x}u_{t}\bigr).

Note that Assumption
2.2

(iii)
implies that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert h^{u}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t&\leq\bigl(3C_{H}^{2}+2\lVert h_{F}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\nabla_{x}u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +T\bigl(3C_{H}^{2}\lVert h_{H}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+2\lVert h_{F}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)\\
&<\infty,\end{split}

(3.17)

so that
h^{u}
is indeed an element of
L^{2}_{\mathbb{F}}([0,T];L^{2}(D))
. Then, we define
\Phi(u,q,m)
as the unique solution
(u^{\prime},q^{\prime},m^{\prime})
of the linear BSPDE (
3.11
) with input
h
replaced by
h^{u}
.

Now, fix two elements
(u^{1},q^{1},m^{1})
,
(u^{2},q^{2},m^{2})\in\mathbb{H}(D)
. Then the difference
\Delta=(\Delta^{u},\Delta^{q},\Delta^{m})=\Phi(u^{1},q^{1},m^{1})-\Phi(u^{2},q^{2},m^{2})
solves the linear BSPDE (
3.11
) with input
h^{u^{1}}-h^{u^{2}}
and initial condition zero. Hence, using (
3.12
) we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{m}_{T}-\Delta^{m}_{t}

\displaystyle\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\Delta^{q}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\bigl\lvert\bigl\langle\Delta^{u}_{s},h^{u^{1}}_{s}-h^{u^{2}}_{s}\bigr\rangle\bigr\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{2\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\bigl\lVert h^{u^{1}}_{s}-h^{u^{2}}_{s}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.18)

Our goal is to estimate the term
\mathbb{E}\bigl\lVert h^{u^{1}}_{s}-h^{u^{2}}_{s}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. First, by Assumption
2.2

(i)
on
H
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{D}\Bigl\lvert H_{s}

\displaystyle\bigl(x,y,u^{1}_{s}(x,y),\nabla_{x}u^{1}_{s}(x,y)\bigr)-H_{s}\bigl(x,y,u^{2}_{s}(x,y),\nabla_{x}u^{2}_{s}(x,y)\bigr)\Bigr\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq\int_{D}2C_{H}^{2}\Bigl(\bigl\lvert u^{1}_{s}(x,y)-u^{2}_{s}(x,y)\bigr\rvert^{2}+\bigl\lvert\nabla_{x}u^{1}_{s}(x,y)-\nabla_{x}u^{2}_{s}(x,y)\bigr\rvert^{2}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{H}^{2}\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}.

Secondly, Assumption
2.2

(i)
on
F
yields

\displaystyle\int_{D}\bigl\lvert F_{s}

\displaystyle\bigl(x,y,u^{1}_{s},\nabla_{x}u^{1}_{s}\bigr)-F_{s}\bigl(x,y,u^{2}_{s},\nabla_{x}u^{2}_{s}\bigr)\bigr\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert h_{F}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigl(\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\nabla_{x}u^{1}_{s}-\nabla_{x}u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq 2\lVert h_{F}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}

Combining the previous two displays implies that
\mathbb{E}\bigl\lVert h^{u^{1}}_{s}-h^{u^{2}}_{s}\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C_{0}\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}
, where
C_{0}=3C_{H}^{2}(\lVert h_{H}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lor 1)+2\lVert h_{F}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. We insert this inequality into (
3.3
) and then fix
\epsilon=C_{0}^{-1}
to find that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{m}_{T}-\Delta^{m}_{t}&\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\Delta^{q}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\frac{CC_{0}}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(3.19)

Now, we proceed as in
[
CD18
, Example 1.20]
. We multiply this inequality by
e^{\eta t}
for
\eta\geq 1
and then integrate from over
[0,T]
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}

\displaystyle e^{\eta t}\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\eta}(e^{\eta s}-1)\bigl(\mathbb{E}\lVert\nabla_{x}\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{q}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{CC_{0}}{2\eta}\int_{0}^{T}e^{\eta s}\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\eta}(e^{\eta s}-1)\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Next, we evaluate Equation (
3.19
) at
t=0
, multiply it by
\frac{1}{2\eta}
, and add it to the inequality above. This yields

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}e^{\eta t}\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2\eta}\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}\theta(s)\bigl(\mathbb{E}\lVert\nabla_{x}\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{q}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3CC_{0}}{4\eta}\int_{0}^{T}e^{\eta s}\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\theta(s)\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{1}_{s}-u^{2}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s,

where
\theta(s)=\frac{1}{2\eta}+\frac{1}{\eta}(e^{\eta s}-1)
.
Then we fix
\eta\geq 1
large enough so that
\frac{3CC_{0}}{4\eta}<\frac{1}{4}
and rearrange, which finally implies

\int_{0}^{T}\theta(t)\bigl(\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{t}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{q}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{2}{3\eta}\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{2}{3}\int_{0}^{T}\theta(t)\mathbb{E}\lVert u^{1}_{t}-u^{2}_{t}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(3.20)

Here we used that
\theta(t)\leq e^{\eta t}
. We can equipped
\mathbb{H}(D)
with the norm

(u,q,m)\mapsto\int_{0}^{T}\theta(t)\bigl(\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{u}_{t}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert\Delta^{q}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{2}{3\eta}\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2},

which turns
\mathbb{H}(D)
into a Banach space. Moreover, owing to (
3.20
), the map
\Phi
is a contraction on this Banach space and by the Banach fixed point theorem has a unique fixed point
(u,q,m)
. This fixed point is a solution to BSPDE (
2.1
) in the sense of Definition (
2.1
). Moreover, since any other solution to BSPDE (
2.1
) is also a fixed point of
\Phi
, the solution
(u,q,m)
is unique.

Lastly, we derive (
2.4
) for
(u,q,m)
. Since
(u,q,m)
solves BSPDE (
3.11
) with input
h^{u}
, the estimate (
3.12
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\lVert u_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\lVert m_{T}-m_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\int_{t}^{T}\lVert\nabla_{x}u_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert q_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq C\biggl(\mathbb{E}\lVert\psi\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lvert\langle u_{s},h^{u}_{s}\rangle\rvert\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr)

Now, we proceed as above, using a bound analogous (
3.17
) to estimate
\mathbb{E}\int_{t}^{T}\lVert h^{u}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s
, in order to establish (
2.4
). We will not provide the details here.
∎

4
Application to a McKean–Vlasov Control Problem with Killing at a State-Dependent Intensity

4.1
Control Problem for the Stochastic Fokker–Planck Equation

In this subsection we reformulate the McKean–Vlasov control problem introduced at the beginning of Subsection
2.2
as the control of the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation (
2.10
), which means proving Proposition
2.8
. Subsequently, we introduce a process that describes the variations of the state process
\mu
under infinitesimal variations of the control
g
.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

We prove the three statements of the proposition in sequence. That
\mu=(\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
solves SPDE (
2.10
) follows along the same lines as the proof of Theorem 1.9 in
[
HŠS21
]
, so we skip the details here and immediately move to the second statement of the proposition.

Our next goal is to construct a solution to SPDE (
2.10
). We proceed in two steps.

Step 1
: For arbitrary coefficients
\beta\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
,
h\in L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{-1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
let us consider the linear SPDE

\mathrm{d}\langle\mu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\bigl(\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\beta_{t}\partial_{x}\varphi+\lambda_{t}\partial_{y}\varphi+a_{t}\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi\bigr\rangle+h_{t}(\varphi)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\mu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

(4.1)

for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with initial condition
\mu_{0}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
It is a straightforward exercise to obtain a unique strong solution
\mu\in C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
to SPDE (
4.1
) through a Galerkin approximation. The continuity of the trajectories of
\mu
as a process with values in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
follows from Theorem
3.3
. Using the generalisation of Itô’s formula from Theorem
3.3
and the fact that the martingale term therein vanishes, since
\langle\mu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\mu_{t}\rangle=0
, we obtain the standard estimate

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C\lVert\mu_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\lvert\langle\mu_{s},\beta_{s}\partial_{x}\mu_{s}\rangle\rvert+\lvert h_{s}(\mu_{s})\rvert\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s

(4.2)

for
0\leq t\leq T
and a constant
C>0
depending on the coefficients
\lambda
,
a
, and
\sigma_{0}
as well as the time horizon
T
. Moreover, if the initial condition
\mu_{0}
is nonnegative with
M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0})<\infty
and
h=0
, then by enlarging the constant if necessary we find that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t})\leq e^{C+C\lVert\beta\rVert_{L^{\infty}}}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{0})
.

Step 2
: Next, we employ a fixed point argument to find a solution for the nonlinear SPDE (
2.10
). Let us define
\mathbb{S}
as the space of elements
\rho
in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
such that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t})\leq e^{C+CC_{b}}\mathbb{E}[\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}+\lvert\zeta\rvert^{2}]
, where
C
is the constant from above and
C_{b}
is the uniform bound on
b
guaranteed by Assumption
2.7

(ii)
. The space
\mathbb{S}
is a closed subset of the Banach space
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
and, hence, a complete metric space. Consequently, we can apply the Banach fixed point theorem to contractions on
\mathbb{S}
. Moreover, we know that
\langle\rho_{t}-\rho_{s},\varphi\rangle\to 0
as
s\to t
for any
\varphi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, because
\rho
has continuous trajectories. Since
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\rho_{t})<\infty
, it is not difficult to deduce that a.s. for all
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
it holds that
\langle\rho_{t}-\rho_{s},f\rangle\to 0
as
s\to t
. However, the Borel
\sigma
-algebras on
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
induced by weak convergence and convergence in the
2
-Wasserstein distance coincide, so that
\rho
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable as a process with values in
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. This ensure that for any
\rho\in\mathbb{S}
, the map
[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\mapsto(t,\omega,x,y)\mapsto b\bigl(t,x,\mathcal{S}(\rho_{t}),g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable.

Now, we construct a map
\Phi
from
\mathbb{S}
to itself as follows: for
\mu\in\mathbb{S}
we define the process
\beta^{\mu}
in
L_{\mathbb{F}}^{\infty}([0,T];L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
by
\beta^{\mu}_{t}(x,y)=b\bigl(t,x,\mathcal{S}(\mu_{t}),g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)
for
(t,\omega,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Then, we let
\Phi(\mu)\in C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
denote the unique strong solution to SPDE (
4.1
) with
\beta
replaced by
\beta^{\mu}
,
h=0
, and initial condition
\mu_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)
, which is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
by Assumption
2.7

(i)
. From (
4.2
) and an application of Grönwall’s inequality, it follows that
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert\Phi(\mu)_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}
is bounded by a constant independent of
\mu
. Moreover, by the discussion below Equation (
4.2
),
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\Phi(\mu)_{t})\leq e^{C+CC_{b}}\mathbb{E}[\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}+\lvert\zeta\rvert^{2}]
, so that
\Phi(\mu)\in\mathbb{S}
. We will show that
\Phi
is a contraction if
T
is sufficiently small. For two flows
\mu^{1}
,
\mu^{2}\in\mathbb{S}
we can consider the difference
\Delta=(\Delta_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
given by
\Delta_{t}=\Phi(\mu^{1})_{t}-\Phi(\mu^{2})_{t}
. The process
\Delta
solves SPDE (
4.1
) with
\beta
replaced by
\beta^{\mu_{1}}
and
h
defined by
h_{t}(\varphi)=\bigl\langle\Phi(\mu^{2})_{t},(\beta^{\mu_{1}}-\beta^{\mu_{2}})\partial_{x}\varphi\bigr\rangle
. Thus, the estimate (
4.2
) implies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\Delta_{t}&\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\Bigl(\lvert\langle\Delta_{s},\beta^{\mu_{1}}_{s}\partial_{x}\Delta_{s}\rangle\rvert+\bigl\lvert\bigl\langle\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s},(\beta^{\mu_{1}}_{s}-\beta^{\mu_{2}}_{s})\partial_{x}\Delta_{s}\bigr\rangle\bigr\rvert\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s.\end{split}

(4.3)

The quantity
\lvert\langle\Delta_{s},\beta^{\mu_{1}}_{s}\partial_{x}\Delta_{s}\rangle\rvert
is easily estimated by
\frac{C_{b}^{2}}{2\epsilon}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}
for an
\epsilon>0
that we shall choose later. Next, by Assumption
2.7

(iii)
it holds that

\displaystyle\bigl\lvert\bigl\langle\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s},(\beta^{\mu_{1}}_{s}-\beta^{\mu_{2}}_{s})\partial_{x}\Delta_{s}\bigr\rangle\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}C_{b}\lVert\mathcal{S}(\mu^{1}_{s})-\mathcal{S}(\mu^{2}_{s})\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{b}^{2}\lVert\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\epsilon}\lVert\mu^{1}_{s}-\mu^{2}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}.

Here we used in the second inequality that the restriction of the map
\mathcal{S}
defined in Equation (
2.12
) to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\cap L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, which takes values in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, is
1
-Lipschitz with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
. Inserting the previous two estimates into (
4.3
), choosing
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2C}
, and rearranging implies that

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\Delta_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C^{2}C_{b}^{2}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert\mu^{1}_{s}-\mu^{2}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Then, we apply Grönwall’s inequality to obtain

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq e^{2C^{2}C_{b}^{2}t}t\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\lVert\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\mu^{1}_{s}-\mu^{2}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Since
\sup_{0\leq s\leq T}\lVert\Phi(\mu^{2})_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
is bounded by a constant independent of
\mu^{2}
, we can choose
t_{1}\in(0,T]
small enough such that
\sup_{0\leq s\leq t_{1}}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\lVert\Delta_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t_{1}}\lVert\mu^{1}_{s}-\mu^{2}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Hence,
\Phi
is a contraction when restricted to the time domain
[0,t_{1}]
and so it possesses a unique fixed point
\mu
. This fixed point is clearly the unique solution to SPDE (
2.10
) in
\mathbb{S}
with input
g
on the interval
[0,t_{1}]
. We can iteratively extend this solution
\mu
to the intervals
[t_{1},2t_{1}\land T]
,
[2t_{1}\land T,3t_{1}\land T]
, and so forth, where (
4.2
) ensures that the expression
\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s
stays bounded for all
t\in[0,T]
. This procedure terminates after finitely many steps with a solution
\mu
on the entire time domain
[0,T]
. The uniqueness of this solution in
\mathbb{S}
follows from the uniqueness on the individual intervals
[kt_{1}\land T,(k+1)t_{1}\land T]
. The estimate (
2.13
) is a direct consequence of (
4.2
).

Next, we check that the trajectories of
\mu
lie in
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. We already know from our definition of
\mathbb{S}
that
\mu
takes values in
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. In addition, we can appeal to the SPDE satisfied by
\mu
to show that a.s.
M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s})\to M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t})
as
s\to t
. Since convergence in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
together with convergence of the second moment is equivalent to convergence in
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
(cf.
[
Vil09
, Definition 6.8]
), we deduce that
\mu
takes values in
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

So far we only proved uniqueness of solutions in the space
\mathbb{S}
, but we need to establish uniqueness within the space of
\mathbb{F}
-adapted
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
-valued processes. Assume we have another
\mathbb{F}
-adapted solution
\mu^{\prime}
of SPDE (
2.10
) that takes values in
C([0,T];\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Then, using a mollification argument together with standard SPDE estimates, we can show that
\mu^{\prime}
must actually take values in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
as well. But then since
\mu^{\prime}
solves SPDE (
2.10
) it follows from the discussion below (
4.2
) that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\mu^{\prime}_{t})<e^{C+CC_{b}}\mathbb{E}[\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}+\lvert\zeta\rvert^{2}]
, so that
\mu^{\prime}\in\mathbb{S}
. Then, uniqueness in
\mathbb{S}
guarantees that
\mu^{\prime}=\mu
.

The third assertion of the proposition is a simple application of
[
LSZ20
, Theorem 1.3.]
.

∎

Next, we want to study the variation of
\mu
for infinitesimal perturbations of
g
. Let
h\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
be a bounded
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random function such that
g^{\epsilon}=g+\epsilon h
maps into
G
for all
\epsilon>0
. Denote the unique solution to SPDE (
2.10
) with input
g^{\epsilon}
by
\mu^{\epsilon}
and define
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}(\mu^{\epsilon}-\mu)
. We want to show that
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}
converges in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
to the solution of the random linear SPDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\varphi\rangle&=\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\mathcal{L}_{2}\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,\cdot)h_{t}\partial_{x}\varphi\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},e^{-\cdot}Db_{0}\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t}\bigr)\bigr\rangle\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(4.4)

for
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with initial condition
\mathcal{V}_{0}=0
. Here we recall that
b_{0}
and
b_{1}
are such that
b(t,x,v,g^{\prime})=b_{0}(t,x,v)+b_{1}(t,x,g^{\prime})
for
(t,x,v,g^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
. Note that
b_{1}
is linear in
g^{\prime}
, so that
\partial_{g}b_{1}
does not depend on
g^{\prime}
and, therefore, we write
\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,x)
instead of
\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,x,g^{\prime})
. Let us begin with the following lemma.

Lemma 4.1
.

Assume that
F\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
has a linear functional derivative. Then
F\circ\mathcal{S}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))\to\mathbb{R}
has a linear functional derivative
D(F\circ\mathcal{S})
given by
(F\circ\mathcal{S})(m)(x,y)=e^{-y}DF(\mathcal{S}(m))(x)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
and
m\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))
.

Proof.

Since
F
has a linear functional derivative
DF
, we have for
m
,
m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
that

\displaystyle(F\circ\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle)(m)-(F\circ\mathcal{S})(m^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}DF\bigl(t\mathcal{S}(m)+(1-t)\mathcal{S}(m^{\prime})\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}\bigl(\mathcal{S}(m)-\mathcal{S}(m^{\prime})\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}e^{-y}DF\bigl(\mathcal{S}(tm+(1-t)m^{\prime})\bigr)(x)\,\mathrm{d}(m-m^{\prime})(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}D(F\circ\mathcal{S})(tm+(1-t)m^{\prime})(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}(m-m^{\prime})(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t,

where we defined
D(F\circ\mathcal{S})(m)(x,y)=e^{-y}DF(\mathcal{S}(m))(x)
for
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
. Clearly,
(x,y)\mapsto D(F\circ\mathcal{S})(m)(x,y)=e^{-y}DF(\mathcal{S}(m))(x)
has at most quadratic growth in
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
uniformly over
m
in bounded subsets of
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))
since
x\mapsto DF(p)(x)
has at most quadratic growth in
x\in\mathbb{R}
uniformly over
p
in bounded subsets of
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
. Hence,
D(F\circ\mathcal{S})
is a linear functional derivative of
F\circ\mathcal{S}
.
∎

Proposition 4.2
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.12
be satisfied. Fix
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
and a bounded
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random function
h\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
, such that
g^{\epsilon}=g+\epsilon h
maps into
G
for all
\epsilon\geq 0
. Let
\mu^{\epsilon}
be the solution to SPDE (
2.10
) with input
g^{\epsilon}
and define
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}(\mu^{\epsilon}-\mu)
, where
\mu=\mu^{0}
. Then
(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
converges in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
to a solution
\mathcal{V}
of SPDE (
4.4
) such that
\supp\mathcal{V}_{t}\subset\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
for
\leb\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.

Proof.

We set
\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y)=b_{0}\bigl(t,x,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t},g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)
for
(t,\omega,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where
\nu^{\epsilon}_{t}=\mathcal{S}(\mu^{\epsilon}_{t})
. Our first objective is to derive an SPDE for
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}
. It holds for
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
that

\mathrm{d}\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon},\varphi\rangle=\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}_{2}\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t},g^{\epsilon}_{t})\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}+\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{t}\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

(4.5)

Let us compute the last term on the right-hand side further. We can decompose the difference
\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y)-\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{t}(x,y)
as

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y

\displaystyle)-b\bigl(t,x,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\bigr)+\bigl(b\bigl(t,x,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)-\mathfrak{b}^{0}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigl(b_{1}(t,x,g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y))-b_{1}(t,x,g_{t}(x,y))\bigr)+\bigl(b_{0}(t,x,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t})-b_{0}(t,x,\nu_{t})\bigr).

The function
b_{1}
is linear in
g
by Assumption
2.6
, which is in place by Assumption
2.12
, so it holds that
b_{1}(t,x,g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y))-b_{1}(t,x,g_{t}(x,y))=\epsilon\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,x)h_{t}(x,y)
. Next, by Assumption
2.10

(ii)
and Lemma
4.1
, for
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
the map
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))\ni m\mapsto b_{0}(t,x,\mathcal{S}(m))
has the linear functional derivative
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto e^{-y^{\prime}}Db_{0}(t,x,\mathcal{S}(m))(x^{\prime})
, where
x^{\prime}\mapsto Db_{0}(t,x,v)(x^{\prime})
denotes the linear functional derivative of the map
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto Db_{0}(t,x,v)
. Thus, we obtain that

\displaystyle b_{0}(t,x,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t})-b_{0}(t,x,\nu_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl\langle\mu^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mu_{t},e^{-\cdot}Db_{0}\bigl(t,x,\eta\nu^{\epsilon}_{t}+(1-\eta)\nu_{t}\bigr)\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl\langle\epsilon\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},e^{-\cdot}Db_{0}\bigl(t,x,\eta\nu^{\epsilon}_{t}+(1-\eta)\nu_{t}\bigr)\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\langle\epsilon\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\eta,

(4.6)

where
B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=e^{-y^{\prime}}Db_{0}\bigl(t,x,\eta\nu^{\epsilon}_{t}+(1-\eta)\nu_{t}\bigr)(x^{\prime})
for
(t,\omega,x,x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We insert the expressions
b_{1}(t,x,g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y))-b_{1}(t,x,g_{t}(x,y))=\epsilon\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,x)h_{t}(x,y)
and (
4.1
) into Equation (
4.5
), which yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},\varphi\rangle&=\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}_{2}\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t},g^{\epsilon}_{t})\big\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}+\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,\cdot)h_{t}\partial_{x}\varphi\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +\biggl(\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)\bigr\rangle\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\mathrm{d}\eta\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(4.7)

for
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Our goal is to show that the family
(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon}
is Cauchy in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

We begin with a uniform bound on
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
. Clearly, the SPDE (
4.7
) has the same form as SPDE (
4.1
) with
\beta
and
h
replaced by
\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}
and

\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon}_{t}(\varphi)=\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,\cdot)h_{t}\partial_{x}\varphi\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\eta+\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)\bigr\rangle\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\mathrm{d}\eta

for
\varphi\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, respectively. Hence, we can apply the estimate (
4.2
) to see that

\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\bigl(\lvert\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{s}\partial_{x}\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\rvert+\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon}_{s}(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s})\rvert\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}s.

(4.8)

Let us estimate the two terms on the right-hand side. For any
\delta>0
we find that
\lvert\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{s}\partial_{x}\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rangle\rvert\leq\frac{C_{b}^{2}}{2\delta}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}
. Next, it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon}_{s}(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{b}^{2}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2}}{2\delta}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}+\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\partial_{x}\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{b}^{2}\lVert h\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\delta}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\delta\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2},

where we used that
\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{s}(x)\bigr\rangle\leq\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}
by Assumption
2.10

(iii)
. Note that here
h_{b}
does not refer to the random function
h\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
, but is the dominating function from Assumption
2.10

(iii)
. Substituting this and the previous estimate into Equation (
4.8
), choosing
\delta=\frac{1}{3C}
, rearranging, and finally applying Grönwall’s inequality implies that

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C

for a possibly enlarged constant
C
independent of
\epsilon
.

Using the uniform bound on the family
(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
we can show that it is Cauchy in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Indeed, let us define
\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}=\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}-\mathcal{V}^{\delta}
. Note that again, the SPDE satisfied by
\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}
is of the same form as SPDE (
4.1
). This time with
\beta
and
h
replaced by
\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}
and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta}_{t}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,1}_{t}(\varphi)+\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,2}_{t}(\varphi)+\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,3}_{t}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{t},(\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mathfrak{b}^{\delta}_{t})\partial_{x}\varphi\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{t},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)\bigr\rangle\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{t},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)-B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)\bigr\rangle\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\mathrm{d}\eta.

for
\varphi\in H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, respectively. As before, we bound the terms
\lvert\langle\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s},\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{s}\partial_{x}\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rangle\rvert
and
\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert
appearing in the estimate for
\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}
provided by (
4.2
). Analogous to the above, for any
\delta>0
, we have
\lvert\langle\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s},\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{s}\partial_{x}\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rangle\rvert\leq\frac{C_{b}^{2}}{2\delta}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}
. Next, by the triangle inequality
\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert\leq\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,1}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert+\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,2}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert+\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,3}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert
and we estimate the three terms on the right-hand side separately. For the first, we get

\displaystyle\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,1}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2\delta}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{s}(\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{s}-\mathfrak{b}^{\delta}_{s})\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{b}^{2}\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\delta}\bigl(\lVert\mu^{\epsilon}_{s}-\mu^{\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\lVert h_{s}\rVert_{L^{\infty}}^{2}(\epsilon-\delta)^{2}\bigr)+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2},

(4.9)

where we used the Lipschitz continuity of
b
in
v
(cf. Assumption
2.4

(i)
, which holds by Assumption
2.7
), the linearity of
b
in the control argument, and that
\lVert\nu^{\epsilon}_{s}-\nu^{\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq\lVert\mu^{\epsilon}_{s}-\mu^{\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}
.
For the second term, we estimate

\displaystyle\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,2}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert\partial_{x}\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert\mu_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\delta}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2},

(4.10)

where as above we used that
\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s},B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{s}(x)\bigr\rangle\leq\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}
by Assumption
2.10

(iii)
. Lastly, it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathfrak{h}^{\epsilon,\delta,3}_{s}(\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\delta}\int_{0}^{1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\lVert B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{s}(x)-B^{\delta,\eta}_{s}(x)\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lvert\mu_{s}(x,y)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}\eta

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}}{2\delta}I^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}+\frac{\delta}{2}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}.

(4.11)

We fix
\delta=\frac{1}{4C}
and insert (
4.1
), (
4.1
), (
4.1
), and the bound on
\lvert\langle\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s},\mathfrak{b}^{\epsilon}_{s}\partial_{x}\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rangle\rvert
into the estimate for
\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}
provided by (
4.2
). Then we rearrange and apply Grönwall’s inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert\Delta^{\epsilon,\delta}_{s}\rVert_{H^{1,0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(\lVert\mu^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mu^{\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}+(\epsilon-\delta)^{2}+I^{\delta,\epsilon}_{t}\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(4.12)

for a possibly enlarged
C
. Let us show that the right-hand side tends to zero as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
. First, we have that
\lVert\mu^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mu^{\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq\epsilon\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}+\delta\lVert\mathcal{V}^{\delta}_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}
, which is bounded uniformly in
\epsilon
and
\delta
and tends to zero as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
. Hence, it remains to take care of the quantity
I^{\delta,\epsilon}_{t}
. It is bounded by
4\lVert h_{b}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\lVert\mu_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}
, so if we prove that
I^{\delta,\epsilon}_{t}
vanishes as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
, then the dominated convergence theorem allows us to conclude the same for the right-hand side of (
4.12
). By Assumption
2.12

(i)
the map
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto Db_{0}(t,x,v)(x^{\prime})
is continuous with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
, so by definition of
B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}
we have
\bigl\lvert B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})-B^{\delta,\eta}_{t}(x)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\bigr\rvert\to 0
as
\epsilon
,
\delta\to 0
. Since by Assumption
2.10

(iii)
it holds that
\lvert B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{t}(x)(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\rvert\leq e^{-y^{\prime}}h_{b}(x^{\prime})
and
e^{-\cdot}h_{b}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty])
, we can conclude that
\bigl\lVert B^{\epsilon,\eta}_{s}(x)-B^{\delta,\eta}_{s}(x)\bigr\rVert_{L^{2}}^{2}\to 0
. This readily implies
I^{\epsilon,\delta}_{t}\to 0
as
\epsilon
and
\delta
are taken to zero. Thus, it follows that
(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
is Cauchy in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
denote the limit of
(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
. Using the convergence of
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}
to
\mathcal{V}
, we can pass to the limit in SPDE (
4.7
) to see that
\mathcal{V}
solves SPDE (
4.4
). Since
\mu^{\epsilon}_{t}
and
\mu_{t}
are supported in
\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
the same holds for
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}_{t}
and its limit
\mathcal{V}_{t}
. This concludes the proof.
∎

4.2
Necessary Stochastic Maximum Principle

The goal of this subsection is to establish the necessary SMP. For that, we first show the existence of an adjoint process satisfying BSPDE (
2.16
), i.e. we prove Proposition
2.11
. Next, we use the adjoint process and the variation
\mathcal{V}
from the previous subsection to derive an expression for the derivative of the cost
J_{\text{cl}}
defined in (
2.14
). Inserting the convexity of
h\mapsto b(t,x,v,h)p+e^{-y}f(t,x,v,h)
implied by Assumption
2.6
into this expression yields the necessary SMP.

Proof of Proposition
2.11
.

We intend to apply Theorem
2.3
, so we simply have to show that the coefficients
\tilde{a}
,
\tilde{H}
,
\tilde{F}
,
\lambda
,
\sigma_{0}
, and
\tilde{\beta}
as well as the terminal condition
\tilde{\psi}
defined by
\tilde{a}_{t}(x,y)=a_{t}(x)
,
\tilde{H}_{t}(x,y,r,p)=\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,p,g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)
,
\tilde{F}_{t}(x,y,u,v)=\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{t}(x,y,v)
,
\tilde{\beta}_{t}(x,y)=0
, and
\psi(x,y)=e^{-y}D\psi(\nu_{T})(x)
for
(t,\omega,x,y,r,p,u,v)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty))^{2}
satisfy the properties stated for
a
,
H
,
F
,
\lambda
,
\alpha
,
\beta
, and
\psi
in Assumption
2.2
. This is obvious for
\tilde{a}
,
\lambda
,
\sigma_{0}
,
\beta
, and
\psi
so we will concentrate on
\tilde{H}
and
\tilde{F}
. Let us begin with the growth conditions Assumption
2.2

(iii)
. We have for
(t,\omega,x,y,r,p,u,v)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty))^{2}
that

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{H}_{t}(x,y,r,p)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\lvert b\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)p\bigr\rvert+\bigl\lvert e^{-y}f\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}\lvert p\rvert+e^{-y}h_{f}(x)

for
h_{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by Assumption
2.10

(i)
and

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{F}_{t}(x,y,u,v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\lvert\bigl\langle\mu_{t},e^{-y}Db_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)v\bigr\rangle\bigr\rvert+\bigl\lvert\bigl\langle\mu_{t},e^{-y}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)\bigr\rangle\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq e^{-y}h_{b}(x)\lVert\mu_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert v\rVert_{L^{2}}+e^{-y}h_{f}(x)

for
h_{b}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by Assumption
2.10

(iii)
. Since
\lVert\mu_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\leq C\lVert\mu_{0}\rVert_{L^{2}}<\infty
by Proposition
2.8
, the right-hand side is of the desired form. Next, we verify the Lipschitz conditions from Assumption
2.2

(i)
. For
(t,\omega,x,y,r,p,u,v)
as above and
(r^{\prime},p^{\prime},u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times(\ell,\infty))^{2}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{H}_{t}(x,y,r,p)-\tilde{H}_{t}(x,y,r^{\prime},p^{\prime})\rvert=\bigl\lvert b\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)p-b\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)p^{\prime}\bigr\rvert\leq C_{b}\lvert p-p^{\prime}\rvert

by the boundedness of
b
and

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{F}_{t}(x,y,u,v)-\tilde{F}_{t}(x,y,u^{\prime},v^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle=\bigl\lvert\bigl\langle\mu_{t},e^{-y}Db_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)v\bigr\rangle-\bigl\langle\mu_{t},e^{-y}Db_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})(x)v^{\prime}\bigr\rangle\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq e^{-y}h_{b}(x)\lVert\mu_{t}\rVert_{L^{2}}\lVert v-v^{\prime}\rVert_{L^{2}}

by Assumption
2.10

(iii)
. These bounds are as desired, so we can apply Theorem
2.3
.
∎

We will use the adjoint process
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
determined by BSPDE (
2.16
) to derive an expression for the Gâteaux derivative of the cost function
J_{\text{cl}}
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let Assumptions
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.12
be satisfied. Then for any control
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
and any
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable random function
h\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{G}}
, such that
g+\epsilon h
maps into
G
, it holds that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}J_{\textup{cl}}(g+\epsilon h)\Big|_{\epsilon=0}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},g_{t})h_{t}\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

(4.13)

Proof.

Set
g^{\epsilon}=g+\epsilon h
and denote the solutions to SPDE (
2.10
) corresponding to the inputs
g^{\epsilon}
and
g
by
\mu^{\epsilon}
and
\mu
, respectively. Further, let
\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon}=\frac{1}{\epsilon}(\mu^{\epsilon}-\mu)
and let
\mathcal{V}
be the limit of
(\mathcal{V}^{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
in
C_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))\cap L_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];H^{1,0}(\mathbb{R}^{2}))
, which exists by Proposition
4.2
. Then, we decompose

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{\textup{cl}}(g^{\epsilon}&)-J_{\textup{cl}}(g)\\
&=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mu_{t},\tilde{f}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t},g^{\epsilon}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\tilde{f}_{1}(t,\cdot,\cdot,g^{\epsilon}_{t})-\tilde{f}_{1}(t,\cdot,\cdot,g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\tilde{f}_{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t})-\tilde{f}_{0}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}[\psi(\nu^{\epsilon}_{T})-\psi(\nu_{T})].\end{split}

(4.14)

We divide the equation by
\epsilon
and then take the limit as
\epsilon
tends to zero. Owing to the continuity of the map
(x,v,g^{\prime})\mapsto f(t,x,v,g^{\prime})
guaranteed by Assumption
2.4

(ii)
(which is in place by Assumption
2.7
) as well as the growth condition on
f
from Assumption
2.10

(i)
, we can pass to the limit in the first expression on the right-hand side above. For the second term, we use Assumption
2.12

(ii)
and apply the fundamental theorem of calculus, whereby

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{1}\bigl(t,x,y,g^{\epsilon}_{t}(x,y)\bigr)-\tilde{f}_{1}\bigl(t,x,y,g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)=\epsilon\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{g}f_{1}\bigl((t,x,g_{t}(x,y)+\epsilon\eta h_{t}(x,y)\bigr)h_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

Since
g^{\prime}\mapsto\partial_{g}f_{1}(t,x,g^{\prime})
is continuous and bounded, we conclude by the dominated convergence theorem that the second expression tends to
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}\tilde{f}_{1}(t,\cdot,\cdot,g_{t})h_{t}\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t
. Next, by Assumption
2.10

(ii)
and Lemma
4.1
, for
(t,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
the map
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))\ni m\mapsto\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\mathcal{S}(m))
has the linear functional derivative
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\mapsto e^{-y^{\prime}}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\mathcal{S}(m))(x^{\prime})
, where
x^{\prime}\mapsto D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,v)(x^{\prime})
denotes the linear functional derivative of the map
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,v)
. Hence, we can write

\displaystyle\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\nu^{\epsilon}_{t})-\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\nu_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigl\langle\mu^{\epsilon}_{t}-\mu_{t},e^{-\cdot}D\tilde{f}_{0}\bigl(t,x,y,\eta\nu^{\epsilon}_{t}+(1-\eta)\nu_{t}\bigr)\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\eta.

Since
x^{\prime}\mapsto D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,v)(x^{\prime})
is bounded by a function
h_{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by Assumption
2.10

(iii)
and
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,v)(x^{\prime})
is continuous with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
by Assumption
2.12

(i)
, divided by
\epsilon
the quantity on the right-hand side above approaches
\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},e^{-\cdot}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\nu_{t})\bigr\rangle
. Consequently, the third summand in (
4.14
) converges to
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},e^{-\cdot}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\nu_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t
. The same reason allows us to pass to the limit as
\epsilon\to 0
in the last term on the right-hand side of Equation (
4.14
), so putting everything together, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}&J_{\textup{cl}}(g+\epsilon h)\Big|_{\epsilon=0}\\
&=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\tilde{f}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}\tilde{f}_{1}(t,\cdot,\cdot,g_{t})h_{t}\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\ \ \ +\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},e^{-\cdot}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\nu_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{T},e^{-\cdot}D\psi(\nu_{T})\bigr\rangle.\end{split}

(4.15)

Next, we apply the generalisation of Itô’s formula from Theorem
3.3
with
\ell=-1
to
\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\tilde{u}_{t}\rangle
. This implies that

\displaystyle\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{T},e^{-\cdot}

\displaystyle D\psi(\nu_{T})\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\mathcal{V}_{0},\tilde{u}_{0}\rangle+\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl\langle b(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\mathcal{V}_{t}-\partial_{x}(a_{t}\mathcal{V}_{t})+\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,\cdot)h_{t}\mu_{t},\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{T}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},e^{-\cdot}Db_{0}(t,x,\nu_{t})\bigr\rangle\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(\bigl\langle b_{t}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}+\tilde{f}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})+\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}),\mathcal{V}_{t}\bigr\rangle\Bigr)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(\langle\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},\partial_{x}(a_{t}\mathcal{V}_{t})\rangle-\langle\tilde{q}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\mathcal{V}_{t}\rangle\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\langle\partial_{x}\mathcal{V}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\tilde{q}_{t}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\rangle+\langle\tilde{q}_{s},\mathcal{V}_{t}\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\langle\tilde{m}_{T},\mathcal{V}_{T}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,\cdot)h_{t}\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\bigr\rangle-\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\tilde{f}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g_{t})\rangle\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t

(4.16)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{0}^{T}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},e^{-\cdot}D\tilde{f}_{0}\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t},g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\langle\mathcal{V}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}\rangle+\langle\tilde{q}_{s},\mathcal{V}_{t}\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\langle\tilde{m}_{T},\mathcal{V}_{T}\rangle,

where we used that
\mathcal{V}_{0}=0
and that

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{t}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime},\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t})-\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}e^{-y^{\prime}}

\displaystyle Db_{0}(t,x,\nu_{t})(x^{\prime})\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)}e^{-y^{\prime}}D\tilde{f}_{0}(t,x,y,\nu_{t})(x^{\prime})\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)

in the second equality. Now, we take expectations on both sides of (
4.2
), so that the martingale terms disappear, and then plug the expression for
\mathbb{E}\bigl\langle\mathcal{V}_{T},e^{-\cdot}D\psi(\nu_{T})\bigr\rangle
provided by the right-hand side of (
4.2
) into Equation (
4.15
). This implies that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}J_{\textup{cl}}(g+\epsilon h)\Big|_{\epsilon=0}=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\Bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}b_{1}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})h_{t}\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}+\partial_{g}\tilde{f}_{1}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t})h_{t}\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t,

which yields the desired expression (
4.13
).
∎

From (
4.13
) and the convexity of the Hamiltonian in the control argument, guaranteed by Assumption
2.6
(which is in place by Assumption
2.12
), we can deduce the necessary SMP.

Proof of Theorem
2.13
.

Let
g
,
h\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
. Since
G
is convex, for any
\epsilon>0
the random function
g^{\epsilon}=(1-\epsilon)g+\epsilon h=g+\epsilon(h-g)
maps into
G
. Hence, we can apply Proposition
4.3
with
h
replaced by
h-g
, which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}J_{\textup{cl}}(g^{\epsilon})\Big|_{\epsilon=0}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\partial_{g}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},g_{t})(h_{t}-g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},h_{t})-\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t,

where we used that the function
h^{\prime}\mapsto\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),h^{\prime}\bigr)
is convex by Assumption
2.6
. On the other hand, we know that
g
is a minimiser of the cost functional
J_{\text{cl}}
, so that

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\epsilon}J_{\textup{cl}}(g^{\epsilon})\Big|_{\epsilon=0}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigl(J_{\textup{cl}}(g+\epsilon(h-g))-J_{\textup{cl}}(g)\bigr)\geq 0.

Combining the previous two inequalities yields

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu_{t},\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},h_{t})-\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\geq 0.

(4.17)

Now, for any
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable set
A\subset[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
and any
h_{\ast}\in G
we can define an
h\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
by
h_{t}(x,y)=h_{\ast}
if
(t,\omega,x,y)\in A
and
h_{t}(x,y)=g_{t}(x,y)
otherwise. Applying (
4.17
) with this choice of
h
implies that

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(t,\cdot,x,y)\Bigl(\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),h_{\ast}\bigr)-\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)\Bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mu_{t}(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}t

is nonnegative. Since
A
and
h_{\ast}
were arbitrary, we conclude that for all
h_{\ast}\in G
it holds that

\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),h_{\ast}\bigr)\geq\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{t}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y),g_{t}(x,y)\bigr)

for
\mu_{t}
-a.e.
(x,y)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
and
\leb\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.
∎

4.3
Existence and Uniqueness of the Semilinear BSPDEs (
2.18
) and (
2.22
)

In this section we establish the existence of a unique strong solution
(u,q,m)
of BSPDE (
2.18
). From this process
(u,q,m)
we construct the unique strong solution
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
of BSPDE (
2.22
) by setting
\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}u_{t}(x)
,
\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}q_{t}(x)
, and
\tilde{m}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}m_{t}(x)
for
(t,\omega,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
.

Proof of Proposition
2.14
.

We apply Theorem
2.3
, so we simply have to verify Assumption
2.2
for the coefficients
a
,
H
,
F
,
\alpha
, and
\beta
defined by
H_{t}(x,r,p)=H^{\nu}_{t}(x,r,p)
,
F_{t}(x,u,v)=F^{\nu}_{t}(x,u,v)
,
\alpha_{t}=\sigma_{0}(t)
, and
\beta_{t}(x)=0
for
(t,\omega,x,r,p,u,v)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times L^{2}(\mathbb{R})^{2}
. We only check the growth and Lipschitz conditions, Assumptions
2.2

(iii)
and
(i)
, for
H
. The coefficient
F
can be dealt with in the same manner as with
\tilde{F}
in the proof of Proposition
2.11
and the properties of the remaining coefficients are obvious. For
(t,\omega,x,r,p)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}^{3}
it holds that

\displaystyle\lvert H_{t}(x,r,p)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\biggl\lvert\inf_{g\in G}\bigl(b(t,x,\nu_{t},g)p+f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\bigr)\biggr\rvert+\lvert\lambda_{t}(x)r\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}\lvert p\rvert+h_{f}(x)+C_{\lambda}\lvert r|,

where the boundedness of
b
and
\lambda
is guaranteed by Assumption
2.7

(ii)
and
h_{f}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by Assumption
2.10

(i)
, so that
H
satisfies Assumption
2.2

(iii)
. Next, if in addition
(r^{\prime},p^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then

\displaystyle\lvert H_{t}

\displaystyle(x,r,p)-H_{t}(x,r^{\prime},p^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\biggl\lvert\inf_{g\in G}\bigl(b(t,x,\nu_{t},g)p+f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\bigr)-\inf_{g\in G}\bigl(b(t,x,\nu_{t},g)p^{\prime}+f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\bigr)\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\lvert\lambda_{t}(x)(r-r^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{g\in G}\Bigl\lvert b(t,x,\nu_{t},g)p+f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)-\bigl(b(t,x,\nu_{t},g)p^{\prime}+f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\bigr)\Bigr\rvert+C_{\lambda}\lvert r-r^{\prime}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}\lvert p-p^{\prime}\rvert+C_{\lambda}\lvert r-r^{\prime}\rvert,

so that
H
also satisfies the Lipschitz condition from Assumption
2.2

(i)
. Thus, we can apply Theorem
2.3
to obtain a unique strong solution to BSPDE (
2.16
).
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.15
.

We first show that the process
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})\in\mathbb{H}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
given by
\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}u_{t}(x)
,
\tilde{q}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}q_{t}(x)
,
\tilde{m}_{t}(x,y)=e^{-y}m_{t}(x)
for
(t,\omega,x,y)\in[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)
, where
(u,q,m)
is the unique strong solution of BSPDE (
2.18
), solves BSPDE (
2.22
). Let
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
and define
\varphi_{0}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
by
\varphi_{0}(x)=\int_{(0,\infty)}e^{-y}\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}y
. Then, using BSPDE (
2.18
), we find

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{u}_{t},\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle u_{t},\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle D\psi,\varphi_{0}\rangle+\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl\langle H^{\nu}_{s}\bigl(\cdot,u_{s}(\cdot),\partial_{x}u_{s}(\cdot)\bigr)+F^{\nu}_{s}(\cdot,\partial_{x}u_{s}),\varphi_{0}\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\bigl\langle\partial_{x}u_{s},\partial_{x}(a_{s}\varphi_{0})\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle q_{s},\sigma_{0}(s)\partial_{x}\varphi_{0}\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{t}^{T}\langle q_{s},\varphi_{0}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\langle m_{T}-m_{t},\varphi_{0}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle e^{-\cdot}D\psi,\varphi\rangle+\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl\langle H^{\nu}_{s}\bigl(\cdot,u_{s}(\cdot),\partial_{x}u_{s}(\cdot)\bigr)+F^{\nu}_{s}(\cdot,\partial_{x}u_{s}),\varphi_{0}\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

(4.18)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\bigl\langle\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s},\partial_{x}(a_{s}\varphi)\bigr\rangle+\langle\tilde{q}_{s},\sigma_{0}(s)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s-\int_{t}^{T}\langle\tilde{q}_{s},\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\langle\tilde{m}_{T}-\tilde{m}_{t},\varphi\rangle.

The terminal condition and the second line are already of the desired form, so it remains to compute the two terms involving
H^{\nu}
and
F^{\nu}
. For the first expression, we write

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\bigl\langle H^{\nu}_{s}(\cdot,u_{s}(\cdot),\partial_{x}u_{s}(\cdot)\bigr),\varphi_{0}\bigr\rangle\\
&=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)}e^{-y}\Bigl(\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(b(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)+f(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\bigr)-\lambda_{s}(x)u_{s}(x)\Bigr)\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.\end{split}

(4.19)

Now, if
\mu_{t}(x,y)>0
then

\displaystyle e^{-y}\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(b(s,x,\nu_{s},h)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)+f(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(b(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{t}(x,y)+\tilde{f}(s,x,y,\nu_{s},h)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\inf_{h\in G}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y),h\bigr).

If, on the other hand,
\mu_{t}(x,y)=0
we compute

\displaystyle e^{-y}

\displaystyle\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(b(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)+f(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\bigr)

\displaystyle=e^{-y}\Bigl(b\bigl(s,x,\nu_{s},g_{\ast}\bigl(s,x,\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)\bigr)\bigr)\partial_{x}u_{s}(x,y)+f\bigl(s,x,\nu_{s},g_{\ast}\bigl(s,x,\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(b\bigl(s,x,g_{\ast}\bigl(s,x,\nu_{s},\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)\bigr)\bigr)\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y)+\tilde{f}\bigl(s,x,y,g_{\ast}\bigl(s,x,\nu_{s},\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)\bigr)\bigr)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y),g_{\ast}\bigl(s,x,\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)\bigr)\bigr),

so together we get

\displaystyle e^{-y}\Bigl(\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(b(s,x,\nu_{s},h)

\displaystyle\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)+f(s,x,\nu_{s},h)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{s}(x,y)>0}\inf_{h\in G}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y),h\bigr)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\mathbf{1}_{\mu_{s}(x,y)=0}\tilde{K}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y),g_{\ast}\bigl(s,x,\partial_{x}u_{s}(x)\bigr)\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y)\bigr),

where in the last step we simply apply the definition of
\tilde{H}^{\mu}
from (
2.21
). Inserting this equality into (
4.19
) yields

\displaystyle\bigl\langle H^{\nu}_{s}(\cdot,u_{s}(\cdot),

\displaystyle\partial_{x}u_{s}(\cdot)\bigr),\varphi_{0}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)}\Bigl(\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(x,y,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y)\bigr)+\lambda_{s}(x)\partial_{y}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y)\Bigr)\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigr),\varphi\bigr\rangle-\langle\tilde{u}_{s},\lambda_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle,

where we used in the first equality that
-e^{-y}u_{s}(x)=\partial_{y}\tilde{u}_{s}(x,y)
and applied integration by parts in the second equality, using that the boundary terms vanish since
\varphi(0)=0
. For the second term, we get

\displaystyle\bigl\langle F^{\nu}_{s}(

\displaystyle x,\partial_{x}u_{s}),\varphi_{0}\bigr\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\nu_{s},Db_{0}(s,\cdot,\nu_{s})(x)\partial_{x}u_{s}\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle\nu_{s},Df_{0}(s,\cdot,\nu_{s})(x)\bigr\rangle\Bigr)\varphi_{0}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty)}\Bigl(\bigl\langle\mu_{s},e^{-y}Db_{0}(s,\cdot,\nu_{s})(x)\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}\bigr\rangle+\bigl\langle\mu_{s},e^{-y}D\tilde{f}_{0}(s,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{s})(x)\bigr\rangle\Bigr)\varphi(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\bigl\langle\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{s}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}),\varphi\bigr\rangle,

where we used in the second equality that
\nu_{s}=\mathcal{S}(\mu_{s})
. Inserting the previous two equalities into (
4.3
) yields

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{u}_{t},\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle e^{-\cdot}D\psi,\varphi\rangle+\int_{t}^{T}\Bigl\langle\tilde{H}^{\mu}_{s}\bigl(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}(\cdot,\cdot)\bigr)+\tilde{F}^{\mu}_{s}(\cdot,\cdot,\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s}),\varphi\Bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\bigl\langle\partial_{x}\tilde{u}_{s},\partial_{x}(a_{s}\varphi)\bigr\rangle+\langle\tilde{u}_{s},\lambda_{s}\partial_{y}\varphi\rangle+\langle\tilde{q}_{s},\sigma_{0}(s)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{t}^{T}\langle\tilde{q}_{s},\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}-\langle\tilde{m}_{T}-\tilde{m}_{t},\varphi\rangle.

Since
\varphi\in H^{1}_{0}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
was arbitrary,
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})\in\mathbb{H}(\mathbb{R}\times(0,\infty))
satisfies BSPDE (
2.22
).

To show that
(\tilde{u},\tilde{q},\tilde{m})
is the unique solution to BSPDE (
2.22
), we apply Theorem
2.3
. Hence, we need to verify that the coefficients of BSPDE (
2.22
) satisfy Assumption
2.2
. We already did that for all coefficients except
\tilde{H}^{\mu}
in the proof of Proposition
2.11
. The assumptions on
\tilde{H}^{\mu}
can be verified in the same manner as the assumptions on
H
in the proof of Proposition
2.14
. This concludes the proof.
∎

From Proposition
2.15
and the necessary SMP, Theorem
2.13
, we can then deduce Corollary
2.16
in the manner outlined above the statement of Corollary
2.16
. Hence, there exists an optimal control
g^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
which takes the form
g^{\ast}_{t}(x)=g_{\ast}\bigl(t,x,\partial_{x}u_{t}(x)\bigr)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbb{P}\otimes\leb
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
. Our next goal is to extend the existence of such an optimal semiclosed-loop control independent of the intensity to more general coefficients and cost functions.

4.4
Extending Equivalence to General Coefficients and Cost Functions
