Throughout this subsection Assumptions
2.4
and
2.6
are in place and we assume that
\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We first establish a weak stability result for SPDE (
2.10
) with varying coefficients and inputs. Let
(b^{n})_{n}
,
(\lambda^{n})_{n}
,
(f^{n})_{n}
, and
(\psi^{n})_{n}
be sequences of functions such that

(i)

b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
satisfy the same properties as
b
,
\lambda
,
f
, and
\psi
in Assumptions
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.12
;

(ii)

there exists a
C>0
such that for
(t,x,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b^{n}(t,x,v,g)\rvert+\lvert\lambda^{n}(t,x)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}(v)),

\displaystyle\lvert f^{n}(t,x,v,g)\rvert+\lvert\psi^{n}(v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}+M^{2}_{2}(v));

(iii)

b^{n}(t,x,v,g)
,
\lambda^{n}(t,x)
,
f^{n}(t,x,v,g)
, and
\psi^{n}(v)
tend to
b(t,x,v,g)
,
\lambda(t,x)
,
f(t,x,v,g)
, and
\psi(v)
uniformly for
(t,x,v,g)
in compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
;

(iv)

the map
G\ni g\mapsto f^{n}_{1}(t,x,g)
is strictly convex for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
.

Since
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
satisfy the same properties as
b
,
\lambda
,
f
, and
\psi
in Assumptions
2.7
,
2.10
, and
2.12
, we can apply Corollary
2.16
, where we replace the coefficients and costs
b
,
\lambda
,
f
, and
\psi
of the control problem by
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
and the initial condition
\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)
by
\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta/n)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The latter ensures that the cumulative intensity will be started from zero in the limit. We will use the assumptions formulated above, in particular the convergence condition
(iii)
, to show that the existence of an optimal semiclosed-loop control independent of the intensity transfers from the control problem with coefficients and costs
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
to the limiting control problem with coefficients and costs
b
,
\lambda
,
f
, and
\psi
.

For
n\geq 1
we define the cost functionals

J_{\text{cl}}^{n}(g)=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\mu^{n}_{t},\tilde{f}^{n}(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu^{n}_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi^{n}(\nu^{n}_{T})\biggr],

(4.20)

for
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
, where
\mu^{n}=(\mu^{n}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
satisfies SPDE (
2.10
) with
b
and
\lambda
replaced by
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
, respectively, initial condition
\mu^{n}_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta/n)
, and control
g
.
For a control
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
, which is independent of the intensity, we can rewrite
J_{\text{cl}}^{n}(g)
as
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu^{n}_{t},f^{n}(t,\cdot,\nu^{n}_{t},g_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi^{n}(\nu^{n}_{T})\bigr]
, where
\nu^{n}=(\nu^{n}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is a solution to SPDE (
2.23
) with
b
and
\lambda
replaced by
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
, respectively, initial condition
\nu^{n}_{0}=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta/n}]\mathcal{L}(\xi)
, and control
g
. The definition of the initial condition is to be understood in the sense that for all
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
it holds that
\langle\nu^{n}_{0},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta/n}]\mathbb{E}[\varphi(\xi)]
.

Let
(E,d)
be a complete separable metric space. We let
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(E)
denote the space of square-integrable measures
m
on
[0,T]\times E
such that
m([s,t]\times E)\leq t-s
for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
. Here square-integrable means that
\int_{E}d^{2}(x,x_{0})\,\mathrm{d}m(x)
for a fixed
x_{0}\in E
.

We say that a random variable
\mathfrak{m}
with values in
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(E)
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable if for any
t\in[0,T]
, the random variable
\mathfrak{m}([0,s]\times A)
is
\mathcal{B}([0,t])\otimes\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for all
s\in[0,t]
and
A\in\mathcal{B}(E)
.

Proposition 4.4
.

Let the sequences
(b^{n})
,
(\lambda^{n})
,
(f^{n})
, and
(\psi^{n})
satisfy Properties
(i)
to
(iv)
above.
Fix a sequence
(g^{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
and denote the solution to SPDE (
2.23
) with coefficients
b
and
\lambda
replaced by
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
, respectively, control
g^{n}
, and initial condition
\nu^{n}_{0}=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta/n}]\mathcal{L}(\xi)
by
\nu^{n}=(\nu^{n}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. Define the
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times G)
-valued random variable
\mathfrak{m}^{n}
by

\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\varphi(t,x,g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}^{n}(t,x,g)=\int_{[0,T]}\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi(t,\cdot,g_{n})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

for
\varphi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G)
. Then the sequence
(\mathfrak{m}^{n},\nu^{n},W)
is tight on
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times G)\times C([0,T];\mathcal{M}^{1}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T])
and any subsequential limit
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
, which we assume is defined on the same probability space with the same Brownian motion
W
,
solves the SPDE

\mathrm{d}\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathcal{L}\varphi(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)+\langle\nu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

(4.21)

for
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with initial condition
\nu_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi)
. Along the chosen subsequence the cost
J_{\textup{cl}}^{n}(g^{n})
converges to

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr].

Note that assuming that
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
is defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
means that we might have to enlarge the probability space. We do this in such a way that
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
are independent of
(\xi,B)
.

Proof of Proposition
4.4
.

It is not difficult to see that
\nu^{n}
is given by
\nu^{n}_{t}=\mathcal{S}(\mu^{n}_{t})
, where
\mu^{n}=(\mu^{n}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
solves SPDE (
2.10
) with input
g^{n}
, coefficients
b
and
\lambda
replaced by
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
, respectively, and initial condition
\mu^{n}=\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta/n)
. By Proposition
2.8
, extending the weak setup
\mathbb{S}^{\ast}
if necessary, we can find a solution
(X^{n},\Lambda^{n})
of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
) with initial condition
(X^{n}_{0},\Lambda^{n}_{0})=(\xi,\zeta/n)
such that
\mu^{n}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X^{n}_{t},\Lambda^{n}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})
.

Step 1
: We first show that the sequence
(\mathfrak{m}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is tight on
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times G)
. By
[
Lac15
, Proposition B.1]
it is enough to show that the sequence of measures
(m^{n})_{n}
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G
defined by
m^{n}(A)=\mathbb{E}[\mathfrak{m}^{n}(A)]
for
A\in\mathcal{B}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G)
is compact in
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times G)
and that the family
(M_{2}^{2}(\mathfrak{m}^{n}))_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable. To establish the former, by Lemma
A.2
, it is enough to show that the sequence
(m^{n})_{n}
is uniformly square-integrable. Now, we have that

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathbf{1}_{\lvert x\rvert\geq c}\lvert x\rvert\,\mathrm{d}m^{n}(t,x,g)\leq\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\lvert X^{n}_{t}\rvert\geq c}\lvert X^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}]\,\mathrm{d}t.

Hence, if we can prove that the family
(X^{n}_{t})_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly square-integrable for all
t\in[0,T]
, the same holds for
(m^{n})_{n}
. Before we establish the former, let us consider the second criterion above, namely that
(M_{2}^{2}(\mathfrak{m}^{n}))_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable. Since
[0,T]
and
G
are compact, uniform integrability of
(M_{2}^{2}(\mathfrak{m}^{n}))_{n\geq 1}
is equivalent to uniform integrability of
\bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\lvert\cdot\rvert^{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\bigr)_{n\geq 1}
. We can estimate

\int_{0}^{T}\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\lvert\cdot\rvert^{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\lvert X^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{T}]\,\mathrm{d}t\leq T\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}\bigr],

which shows that uniform integrability of
\bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\lvert\cdot\rvert^{2}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\bigr)_{n\geq 1}
is implied by uniform integrability of
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}\bigr]
. Now, both the uniform square-integrability of
(X^{n}_{t})_{n\geq 1}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and the uniform integrability of
\bigl(\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}\bigr]\bigr)_{n\geq 1}
are implied by the uniform square-integrability of
(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})_{n\geq 1}
, so that is what we will show. Using standard estimates for the McKean–Vlasov SDE satisfied by
(X^{n},\Lambda^{n})
and, subsequently, applying Grönwall’s inequality, we find

(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{t})^{2}\leq C\lvert\xi\rvert^{2}+C\int_{0}^{t}(1+M_{2}^{2}(\nu^{n}_{s}))\,\mathrm{d}s+CZ^{n}_{t},

(4.22)

where
Z^{n}_{t}=\sup_{0x\leq s\leq t}\bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{s}\sigma(r,X^{n}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}B_{r}+\int_{0}^{s}\sigma_{0}(r)\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}\bigr\rvert^{2}
. Further estimating
M^{2}_{2}(\nu^{n}_{s})\leq\mathbb{E}[\lvert X^{n}_{s}\rvert^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{T}]\leq\mathbb{E}[(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{t})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{T}]
for
s\in[0,t]
, taking conditional expectation with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{T}
on both sides of (
4.22
), and then applying Grönwall’s inequality again yields that
\mathbb{E}[(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{T}]\leq C+C\mathbb{E}[Z^{n}_{T}|\mathcal{F}_{T}]
for a possibly enlarged constant
C>0
. Since the diffusion coefficients
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
are bounded, the sequence
(\mathbb{E}[Z^{n}_{T}|\mathcal{F}_{T}])_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable, so that the same is true for
(\mathbb{E}[(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{T}])_{n\geq 1}
and, therefore,
(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}M_{2}^{2}(\nu^{n}_{t}))_{n\geq 1}
. Thus, in view of (
4.22
), the family
(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})_{n\geq 1}
is uniformly integrable.

Next, we establish the tightness of
(\nu^{n})_{n\geq 1}
on
C([0,T];\mathcal{M}^{1}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R}))
. By Theorem 8.6 of Chapter 3 in
[
EK86
]
to prove tightness it is enough to show that (i)
(\nu^{n}_{t})_{n}
is tight on
\mathcal{M}^{1}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t\in[0,T]
and (ii) there exists a sequence of random variables
(\zeta_{n})_{n}
with
\sup_{n}\mathbb{E}\zeta_{n}<\infty
such that for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, we have
\mathbb{E}\bigl[d_{1}(\nu^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{s})\big|\mathcal{F}_{s}\bigr]\leq\lvert t-s\rvert^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{n}|\mathcal{F}_{s}]
, where we recall that
d_{1}
is introduced in Subsection
2.2
. We already showed (i) and the second property is easily established, using the expression
d_{1}(v_{1},v_{2})=\sup_{\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\text{Lip}}}\langle v_{1}-v_{2},\varphi\rangle
. We do not provide the details here, but note that the required estimates can be deduced along the same lines as the proof of Proposition 3.4 in
[
HJ23
]
.

Step 2
: Let
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
be a weak subsequential limit of
(\mathfrak{m}^{n},\nu^{n},W)_{n\geq 1}
. We show that
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
solves SPDE (
4.21
). To achieve this we reformulate the SPDEs satisfied by
(\mathfrak{m}^{n},\nu^{n},W)
and
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
as martingale problems and deduce the stability of those martingale problems. Let us define the generator
\mathcal{L}^{n}
by

\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi(t,x,v,g)=b^{n}(t,x,v,g)\partial_{x}\varphi(x)-\lambda^{n}(t,x)\varphi(x)+a_{t}(x)\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x)

for
\varphi\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
(t,x,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
. By assumption
\nu^{n}
satisfies SPDE (
2.23
) with
b
and
\lambda
replaced by
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
, respectively, initial condition
\nu^{n}_{0}=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta/n}]\mathcal{L}(\xi)
, and control
g^{n}
. We can rewrite this as

\mathrm{d}\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi(t,x,\nu^{n}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}^{n}(t,x,g)+\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

for
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. We formulate this SPDE as a martingale problem. For
k\geq 1
, let
\Phi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k+1})
and
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{k})
, and let
\langle v,\varphi\rangle
denote the vector
(\langle v,\varphi_{1}\rangle,\dots,\langle v,\varphi_{k}\rangle)
for
v\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
. We define the differential operator
\mathbb{L}^{n}
acting on
\Phi
and
\varphi
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi)(t,x,w,v,g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\partial_{y_{i}}\Phi(\langle v,\varphi\rangle,w)\mathcal{L}^{n}\varphi_{i}(t,x,v,g)+\sum_{i,j=1}^{k}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{i}y_{j}}\Phi(\langle v,\varphi\rangle,w)\langle v,\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi_{j}\rangle\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi_{i}(x)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{i}y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle v,\varphi\rangle,w)\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi_{i}(x)

for
(t,x,w,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
.
We define the operator
\mathbb{L}
analogously, only replacing the appearance of
\mathcal{L}^{n}
in the definition of
\mathbb{L}^{n}
by
\mathcal{L}
. Now, applying Itô’s formula to
\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})
implies that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathbb{L}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi)(t,x,W_{t},\nu^{n}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}^{n}(t,x,g)+\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\sum_{i=1}^{k}\partial_{y_{i}}\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi_{i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\partial_{y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}.

In other words, the process
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi)
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{n}_{t}(\Phi,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})-\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathbb{L}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi)(s,x,W_{s},\nu^{n}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}^{n}(s,x,g)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle\nu^{n}_{s},\varphi\rangle,W_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s

is a martingale for any
\Phi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{k})
. On the one hand, as in
[
HJ23
, Lemma A.7]
, we can show that for any convergent sequence
(m^{n},v^{n},w^{n})_{n\geq 1}
in
\mathbb{M}^{2}_{\leq T}(\mathbb{R}\times G)\times C([0,T];\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R}))\times C([0,T])
with limit
(m,v,w)
, the expression

\displaystyle\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathbb{L}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi)(s,x,w^{n}_{s},v^{n}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}m^{n}(s,x,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle v^{n}_{s},\varphi\rangle,w^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s

converges to

\displaystyle\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathbb{L}(\Phi,\varphi)(s,x,w_{s},v_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}m(s,x,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle v_{s},\varphi\rangle,w_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s,

and
\Phi(\langle v^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle,w^{n}_{t})
tends to
\Phi(\langle v_{t},\varphi\rangle,w_{t})
. On the other hand, it is not difficult to see that
(\mathcal{M}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi))_{n\geq 1}
is tight on
C([0,T])
. Consequently, we see that along a further subsequence
\mathcal{M}^{n}(\Phi,\varphi)
converges weakly to the process
\mathcal{M}(\Phi,\varphi)
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{t}(\Phi,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\Phi(\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle,W_{t})-\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathbb{L}(\Phi,\varphi)(s,x,W_{s},\nu_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(s,x,g)

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}_{y_{k+1}}\Phi(\langle\nu_{s},\varphi\rangle,W_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s.

Moreover, since the local martingale property is stable under weak convergence and the expectation
\mathbb{E}\lvert\mathcal{M}_{T}(\Phi,\varphi)\rvert^{2}
is finite, it follows that
\mathcal{M}(\Phi,\varphi)
is a martingale for all
\Phi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
and
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{R}^{k})
. By Theorem II.7.1 from
[
IW89
]
we can conclude that

\langle\nu_{t},\varphi_{i}\rangle=\langle v_{0},\varphi_{i}\rangle+\int_{[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}\mathcal{L}\varphi_{i}(s,x,\nu_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(s,x,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\langle\nu_{s},\sigma_{0}(s)\partial_{x}\varphi_{i}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}

holds a.s. for any finite collection
\varphi_{1}
, …,
\varphi_{k}
of function in
C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. Since
C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
is separable when equipped with the norm
\varphi\mapsto\lVert\varphi\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\partial_{x}\varphi\rVert_{\infty}+\lVert\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi\rVert_{\infty}
and both sides of the above equation vary continuously as a function of
\varphi_{i}
, we conclude that
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
satisfies SPDE (
4.21
).

Step 3
: It remains to establish the convergence of the costs
J^{n}_{\text{cl}}(g^{n})
. Note that again proceeding as in
[
HJ23
, Lemma A.7]
, we can show that along the subsequence along which
(\mathfrak{m}^{n},\nu^{n},W)
converges weakly to
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
, the integral

\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu^{n}_{t},f^{n}(t,\cdot,\nu^{n}_{t},g^{n}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu^{n}_{T})=\int_{0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}f^{n}(t,x,\nu^{n}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}^{n}(t,x,g)+\psi(\nu^{n}_{T})

converges weakly to
\int_{0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)+\psi(\nu_{T})
. Here, we crucially use the uniform convergence of
f^{n}
and
\psi^{n}
on compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
, Property
(iii)
, as well as the fact that convergent sequences in
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times G)
are uniformly square-integrable together with the uniform quadratic growth of
f^{n}
and
\psi^{n}
, Property
(ii)
. Next, from Step 1 we know that the sequences
(M_{2}^{2}(\mathfrak{m}^{n}))_{n\geq 1}
and
(M_{2}^{2}(\nu^{n}_{T}))_{n\geq 1}
are uniformly integrable. Hence, using Property
(ii)
again,
allows us to upgrade the weak subsequential convergence of
\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu^{n}_{t},f^{n}(t,\cdot,\nu^{n}_{t},g^{n}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu^{n}_{T})
to subsequential convergence in mean. Thus,
J^{n}_{\text{cl}}(g^{n})
converges subsequentially to
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\int_{0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)+\psi(\nu_{T})\bigr]
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Let
(\mathfrak{m},\nu,W)
be as in the statement of Proposition
4.4
. Firstly, let us note that the random variables
\mathfrak{m}
and
\nu
are not necessarily
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable, so we have to introduce a different filtration. We let
\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}
denote the completion of the filtration

\Bigl(\sigma\bigl(\mathfrak{m}([0,s]\times A),\,W_{s}\mathpunct{:}A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}\times G),\,0\leq s\leq t\bigr)\Bigr)_{0\leq t\leq T}.

The random measure
\mathfrak{m}
is by definition
\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}
-progressively measurable. Moreover, owing to Lemma
A.3
there is a family of random probability measures
(\Gamma_{t,x})_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}}
on
G
such that
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)=\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t,x}(g)\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}(x)\mathrm{d}t
. In particular, the process
\nu
is
\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}
-adapted as well. In addition, it follows from
[
Lac15
, Lemma 3.2]
that the map
\Gamma\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\Omega\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{P}(G)
can be chosen in an
\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}
-predictable manner. We now define
\mathbb{S}^{\ast}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},\xi,B,W},\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W},\xi,0,B,W)
, where
\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},\xi,W,B}
is the completion of
\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}\lor\mathbb{F}^{\xi,B}
and
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,B}
denotes the completion of the filtration generated by
\xi
and
B
. One can check that
\mathbb{S}^{\ast}
is a weak setup.

Remark 4.5
.

Let us note that Equation (
4.21
) can be viewed as a relaxed formulation for the control of the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation (
2.23
). Here a relaxed control is a progressively measurable
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times G)
-valued process
\mathfrak{m}
such that the flow of measures
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
obtained through the disintegration
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)=\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t,x}(g)\,\mathrm{d}\nu_{t}(x)\mathrm{d}t
satisfies SPDE (
4.21
). This idea can be extended to arbitrary (stochastic) Fokker–Planck equations or, more generally, measure-valued (S)PDEs, such as those arising in filtering theory, and may be useful to establish the existence of optimal controls.

Next, since Assumption
2.6
is in place, as in the proof of
[
Lac15
, Theorem 3.7]
, we can find
g^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}}^{1}
such that

\int_{G}b(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t,x}(g)=b\bigl(t,x,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t}(x)\bigr),\quad\int_{G}f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t,x}(g)\geq f(t,x,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t}(x)).

(4.23)

This allows us to rewrite SPDE (
4.21
) as

\mathrm{d}\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\bigl\langle\nu_{t},\mathcal{L}\varphi(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\nu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

(4.24)

for
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. For the associated cost we obtain the upper bound

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu_{t},f(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]\leq\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr].

(4.25)

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
\nu
and
g^{\ast}
be as above. Then, extending the weak setup
\mathbb{S}^{\ast}
if necessary, there exists a solution
(X,\Lambda)
to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
) with initial conditions
X_{0}=\xi
,
\Lambda_{0}=0
, subprobability distribution
\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}[e^{\Lambda_{t}}\varphi(X_{t})|\mathcal{F}_{T}]
, and control
\gamma^{\ast}=(\gamma^{\ast}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
given by
\gamma^{\ast}_{t}=g^{\ast}_{t}(X_{t})
. That is,
\gamma^{\ast}
is a semiclosed-loop control with feedback function
g^{\ast}
and
J_{\mathbb{S}^{\ast}}(\gamma^{\ast})=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu_{t},f(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\bigr]
.

Proof.

Let us consider the random linear SPDE

\mathrm{d}\langle\tilde{\mu}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\bigl\langle\tilde{\mu}_{t},\mathcal{L}_{2}\varphi(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\tilde{\mu}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

for
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with initial condition
\tilde{\mu}_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)
. Note that unlike in SPDE (
2.10
), the flow of subprobabilities
\nu
is fixed and not determined by the SPDE. Hence, the SPDE is not nonlinear but linear with random coefficients. Since
\mathcal{L}(\xi,\zeta)\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, using similar arguments as in the proof of Proposition
2.8
, we can show that this SPDE has a unique strong solution. Moreover, if we define
\tilde{\nu}=(\tilde{\nu}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
by
\tilde{\nu}_{t},=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta}]^{-1}\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\mu}_{t})
, then
\tilde{\nu}
solves the random linear SPDE

\mathrm{d}\langle\tilde{\nu}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\bigl\langle\tilde{\nu}_{t},\mathcal{L}\varphi(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\tilde{\nu}_{t},\sigma_{0}(t)\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}

for
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with initial condition
\tilde{\nu}_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi)
. This SPDE again has a unique solution, so since
\nu
solves the same linear SPDE we obtain that
\tilde{\nu}=\nu
. In other words, it holds that
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta}]^{-1}\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\mu}_{t})
.

Now, by
[
LSZ20
, Theorem 3.1]
, extending the weak setup if necessary, we can find a solution
(X,\tilde{\Lambda})
to the random SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=b\bigl(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t}(X_{t})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad\mathrm{d}\tilde{\Lambda}_{t}=\lambda(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

with initial conditions
X_{0}=\xi
,
\tilde{\Lambda}_{0}=\zeta
, such that
\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\tilde{\Lambda}_{t}|\mathcal{F}_{T})=\tilde{\mu}_{t}
. Note that
\nu
is a fixed input and not determined by the SDE. We define
\Lambda_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,X_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
and set
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{T})
. Then it follows that

\langle\mathcal{S}(\mu_{t}),\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\Lambda_{t}}\varphi(X_{t})|\mathcal{F}_{T}]=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta}]^{-1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[e^{-\tilde{\Lambda}_{t}}\varphi(X_{t})\big|\mathcal{F}_{T}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\zeta}]^{-1}\mathcal{S}(\tilde{\mu}_{t})=\nu_{t},

where we used that
\zeta
is independent of
X
,
\Lambda
, and
\mathcal{F}_{T}
. Hence,
(X,\Lambda)
is a solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.8
). This, in particular, implies that

J_{\mathbb{S}}(\gamma^{\ast})=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\langle\nu_{t},f(t,\cdot,\cdot,\nu_{t},g^{\ast}_{t})\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr],

where
\gamma^{\ast}=(\gamma^{\ast}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is defined by
\gamma^{\ast}_{t}=g^{\ast}_{t}(X_{t})
.
∎

We can use the subsequential convergence established in Proposition
4.4
and the representation from Proposition
4.6
to show that if the controls
g^{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
in the statement of Proposition
4.4
are chosen optimally, then a random function
g^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
chosen according to the criterion (
4.23
) yields an optimal semiclosed-loop control which is independent of the intensity.

For convenience, in the following we let
V^{n}
denote the infimum of
J^{n}_{\textup{cl}}(g)
over control
g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}
. From our discussion in Section
2.2
we know that
V^{n}
coincides with the value of the strong formulation introduced at the beginning of Section
2.2
with
b
,
\lambda
,
f
, and
\psi
replaced by
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
, respectively, and initial condition
(\xi,\zeta/n)
.

Proposition 4.7
.

Choose the controls
g^{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
in the statement of Proposition
4.4
optimally.
Then the control
\gamma^{\ast}
from Proposition
4.6
is an optimal semiclosed-loop control and the feedback function
g^{\ast}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}^{\mathfrak{m},W}}^{1}
does not depend on the intensity.

Note that optimal controls
g^{n}\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1}
, i.e. controls for which
J^{n}_{\textup{cl}}(g^{n})=V^{n}_{\textup{cl}}
, exist by Corollary
2.16
.

Proof of Proposition
4.7
.

Note that regardless of which weakly convergent subsequence
(\mathfrak{m}^{n_{k}},\nu^{n_{k}},W)_{k\geq 1}
we choose in Proposition
4.4
, in view of Equation (
4.25
) and Proposition
4.6
, we always obtain that

\lim_{k\to\infty}J_{\text{cl}}^{n_{k}}(g^{n_{k}})=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G}f(t,x,\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{m}(t,x,g)+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]\geq J_{\mathbb{S}^{\ast}}(\gamma^{\ast})\geq V_{\mathbb{S}^{\ast}}=V.

Hence, we find that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}V^{n}\geq V
.

Next, let us deduce the converse inequality. To achieve this we exploit the equivalence between the strong formulation introduced at the beginning of Section
2.2
and the SPDE formulation that we study here. Let us denote the cost functional for the strong formulation with
b
,
\lambda
,
f
, and
\psi
replaced by
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
, respectively, and initial condition
(\xi,\zeta/n)
by
J^{n}
. Then
J_{\textup{cl}}(g^{n})=V^{n}=\inf_{\gamma}J^{n}(\gamma)
, where the infimum is over
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,\zeta,B,W}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, and
V=\inf_{\gamma}J(\gamma)
, where the infimum is over
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,B,W}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. Now, let us choose an
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,B,W}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
with
J(\gamma)\leq V+\epsilon
. It holds that
J_{\textup{cl}}^{n}(g^{n})=V^{n}\leq J^{n}(\gamma)
, so if we can show that
\lim_{n\to\infty}J^{n}(\gamma)=J(\gamma)
, then we have proved that

V\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}V^{n}\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}J^{n}(\gamma)=J(\gamma)\leq V+\epsilon.

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
, then yields the equality
V\leq J_{\mathbb{S}^{\ast}}(\gamma^{\ast})\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{\textup{cl}}^{n}(g^{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}V^{n}=V
.
Hence,
\gamma^{\ast}
is an optimal semiclosed-loop control whose feedback function
g^{\ast}
is independent of the intensity.

It remains to prove that
J^{n}(\gamma)\to J(\gamma)
for the
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,B,W}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process
\gamma
from above. Let
(X^{n},\Lambda^{n})
be the solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) with coefficients
b
and
\lambda
replaced by
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
, respectively, initial condition
(\xi,\zeta/n)
, and control
\gamma
. By
(X,\Lambda)
we denote the solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) with initial condition
(\xi,0)
and control
\gamma
. Then owing to the uniform in
n
linear growth of
b^{n}
and
\lambda^{n}
and the locally uniform convergence of
b^{n}
to
b
and
\lambda^{n}
to
\lambda
(cf. Properties
(iii)
and
(ii)
), we can show that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}=0
. The only difficulty in obtaining this convergence lies in the fact that the mapping
\mathcal{S}
defined in (
2.12
) is only locally Lipschitz continuous as a map
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))\to\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty))
is equipped with the
2
-Wasserstein distance and
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
with the metric
d_{1}
. However, this hurdle can be overcome through stopping time arguments. We do not provide the details here and instead refer to
[
HJ23
, Propositions 3.12 and A.2]
. Once we know that
(X^{n})_{n\geq 1}
converges to
X
in
L^{2}
-
\sup
, the convergence of the costs
J^{n}(\gamma)
to
J(\gamma)
follows straightforwardly from the uniform quadratic growth assumptions placed on
f^{n}
and
\psi^{n}
as well as the locally uniform convergence of
f^{n}
to
f
and
\psi^{n}
to
\psi
(cf. Properties
(iii)
and
(ii)
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, to prove Theorem
2.17
we wish to apply Proposition
4.7
. Starting from the coefficients
b
and
\lambda
as well as cost functions
f
and
\psi
satisfying Assumptions
2.4
and
2.6
, we want to mollify and cut off these functions appropriately to obtain maps
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
that satisfy Properties
(i)
to
(iv)
outlined at the beginning of this subsection. If we find such maps, then Proposition
4.7
implies the existence of an optimal semiclosed-loop control independent of the intensity. To construct the desired maps, we use Proposition
A.4
and Proposition
A.5
.

Proof of Theorem
2.17
.

Owing to Assumption
2.4
, the functions
b
and
f
decompose as
b(t,x,v,g)=b_{0}(t,x,v)+b_{1}(t,x,g)
and
f(t,x,v,g)=f_{0}(t,x,v)+f_{1}(t,x,g)
for
(t,x,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
. Now, we can apply Proposition
A.4
to obtain a sequence
(f_{1}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
that converges locally uniformly to
f_{1}
. Further, we define the sequence
(b_{1}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
by
b_{1}^{n}(t,x,g)=b(t,-n\lor x\land n,g)
. Next, by Proposition
A.5
we can find sequences
(\tilde{b}_{0}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
(\tilde{f}_{0}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
, and
(\psi^{n})_{n\geq 1}
that converge locally uniformly to
b_{0}
,
f_{0}
, and
\psi
, respectively. We then set
b_{0}^{n}(t,x,v)=(\eta_{1/n}\ast\tilde{b}_{0}^{n}(t,\cdot,g))(x)
and
f_{0}^{n}(t,x,v)=(\eta_{1/n}\ast\tilde{f}_{0}^{n}(t,\cdot,g))(x)
, where
\eta\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the standard mollifier and
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\eta(\frac{x}{\epsilon})
. This ensures the Lipschitz continuity of
(x,v)\mapsto b_{0}^{n}(t,x,v)
uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
and the uniform continuity of
(x,v)\mapsto f_{0}^{n}(t,x,v)
for
(t,x,v)
in compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
without destroying Properties
(i)
to
(iv)
from Proposition
A.5
. Then, we define the functions
b^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\lambda^{n}
by
b^{n}(t,x,v,g)=b_{0}^{n}(t,x,v)+b_{1}^{n}(t,x,g)
,
f^{n}(t,x,v,g)=f_{0}^{n}(t,x,v)+f_{1}^{n}(t,x,g)
, and
\lambda^{n}(t,x)=\lambda(t,x)\land n
for
(t,x,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
. It is straightforward to check that the functions
b^{n}
,
\lambda^{n}
,
f^{n}
, and
\psi^{n}
satisfy Properties
(i)
to
(iv)
. Hence, as stated above, we can conclude with Proposition
4.7
.
∎

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Auxiliary Results for Section
3

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a separable Hilbert space. Suppose that
(u^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence in
D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
that converges to some process
u\in D_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
, and that
(m^{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
(\tilde{m}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
are sequences in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
that converge to processes
m
,
\tilde{m}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
, respectively. Next, let
F_{1}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
and
F_{2}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{H}\to B_{2}(\mathcal{H})
be continuous and bounded on bounded sets of
\mathcal{H}
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\langle F_{1}(u^{n}_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{t}\langle F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}},

(A.1)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(F_{2}(u^{n}_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{n},\tilde{m}^{n}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)

\displaystyle\to\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(F_{2}(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m,\tilde{m}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)

(A.2)

uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
in probability.

Proof.

We prove the convergences in (
A.1
) and (
A.2
) separately. For the first one we write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int_{0}^{t}\langle F_{1}(u^{n}_{s-})&,\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}-\int_{0}^{t}\langle F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m_{s}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\\
&=\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u^{n}_{s-})-F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}+\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}(m^{n}_{s}-m_{s})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.\end{split}

(A.3)

We will deal with both terms on the right-hand side in sequence. Our goal is to prove that both converge to zero in probability. For
k\geq 1
let
\pi_{k}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
denote the projection onto the centred ball
B_{k}
of radius
k
in
\mathcal{H}
. Now, since
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert u^{n}_{s}-u_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\to 0
, for any
\delta>0
we can find
K\geq 1
such that for all
k\geq K
it holds that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{s-})=u^{n}_{s-},\,\pi_{k}(u_{s-})=u_{s-}\ \text{for }s\in(0,t]\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert u^{n}_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\lor\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\lVert u_{s}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq k\biggr)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\delta.

Hence, for any
\epsilon>0
we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert&\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u^{n}_{s-})-F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert>\epsilon\biggr)\\
&\leq\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{s-}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{s-})),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert>\epsilon\biggr)+\delta.\end{split}

(A.4)

Next, using Proposition
3.1
we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}

\displaystyle(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{s-}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{s-})),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert>\epsilon\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{t-}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{t-})),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\lVert F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{t-}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{t-}))\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}[m^{n}]_{t}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl\lVert F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{t}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{t}))\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}\bigl([m^{n}]_{T}-[m^{n}]_{0}\bigr)^{1/2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{1}^{2}}{\delta\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl\lVert F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{t}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{t}))\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\delta\mathbb{E}\bigl([m^{n}]_{T}-[m^{n}]_{0}\bigr).

(A.5)

Now, since
m^{n}
converges to
m
in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H})
, the quantity
\mathbb{E}\bigl([m^{n}]_{T}-[m^{n}]_{0}\bigr)
is bounded uniformly in
n\geq 1
by some constant
C>0
. Moreover, the map
F_{1}\circ\pi_{k}
is continuous and bounded, so since
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert u^{n}_{t}-u_{t}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\to 0
by the dominated convergence theorem, we may choose
n\geq 1
large enough so that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl\lVert F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u^{n}_{t}))-F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{t}))\bigr\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\leq\frac{\delta^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{C_{1}^{2}}
. Plugging this into (
A.1
) and combining it with Equation (
A.4
) implies that

\mathbb{P}\biggl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u^{n}_{s-})-F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert>\epsilon\biggr)\leq(2+C)\delta.

Since
\delta
and
\epsilon
were arbitrary this implies that
\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u^{n}_{s-})-F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}m^{n}_{s}\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
converges uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
to zero in probability.

Next, let us consider the second term on the right-hand side of (
A.3
). Arguing as above for any
\delta>0
we can choose
K\geq 1
and
k\geq K
such that for any
\epsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}

\displaystyle(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}(m^{n}_{s}-m_{s})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert>\epsilon\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{s-})),\mathrm{d}(m^{n}_{s}-m_{s})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\biggr\rvert+\delta

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta}{c_{k}^{2}}\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lVert F_{1}(\pi_{k}(u_{t}))\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}^{2}+\frac{C_{1}^{2}c_{k}^{2}}{\delta\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[[m^{n}]_{t}-[m^{\epsilon}]_{0}-([m]_{t}-[m]_{0})\bigr]+\delta

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta+\frac{C_{1}^{2}c_{k}^{2}}{\delta\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[[m^{n}]_{t}-[m^{n}]_{0}-([m]_{t}-[m]_{0})\bigr],

where
c_{k}=\sup_{h\in B_{k}}\lVert F_{1}(h)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}}
. Then we choose
\epsilon
large enough such that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[[m^{n}]_{t}-[m^{n}]_{0}-([m]_{t}-[m]_{0})\bigr]\leq\frac{\delta^{2}\epsilon^{2}}{C_{1}^{2}c_{n}^{2}}
to obtain
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}(m^{n}_{s}-m_{s})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}\bigr\rvert>\epsilon\bigr)\leq 3\delta
, which means that
\int_{0}^{t}\bigl\langle F_{1}(u_{s-}),\mathrm{d}(m^{n}_{s}-m_{s})\bigr\rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
converges uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
to zero in probability as well. This concludes our treatment of the convergence in (
A.1
).

We continue with (
A.2
). By appealing to the polarisation identity
[[m,\tilde{m}]]=\frac{1}{4}([[m+\tilde{m}]]-[[m-\tilde{m}]])
, we can reduce to the case
\tilde{m}^{n}=m^{n}
and
\tilde{m}=m
. Then similarly to Equation (
A.3
), we decompose

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(

\displaystyle F_{2}(u^{n}_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)-\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(F_{2}(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}[[m]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl((F_{2}(u^{n}_{s-})-F_{2}(u_{s-}))\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)+\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(F_{2}(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}([[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}-[[m]]^{c}_{s})\bigr).

For the first term we apply the inequality

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl((F_{2}(u^{n}_{s-})-F_{2}(u_{s-}))\,\mathrm{d}[[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}\bigr)\biggr\rvert\leq\int_{0}^{T}\bigl\lVert F_{2}(u^{n}_{t-})-F_{2}(u_{t-})\bigr\rVert\,\mathrm{d}[m^{n}]^{c}_{t},

(A.6)

where
\bigl\lVert F_{2}(u^{n}_{t-})-F_{2}(u_{t-})\bigr\rVert
denotes the operator norm of
F_{2}(u^{n}_{t-})-F_{2}(u_{t-})\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
and then proceed as above to show that the right-hand side converges to zero in probability as
n\to\infty
. To deal with the second term, we write
[[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}-[[m]]^{c}_{s}=[[m^{n}-m,m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}+[[m,m^{n}-m]]^{c}_{s}
and then apply the Kunita–Watanabe inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\biggl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(F_{2}

\displaystyle(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}([[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}-[[m]]^{c}_{s})\bigr)\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F_{2}(u_{t-})\rVert\,\mathrm{d}([m^{n}]_{t}+[m]_{t})\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F_{2}(u_{t-})\rVert\,\mathrm{d}[m^{n}-m]_{t}\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq\delta\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F_{2}(u_{t-})\rVert\,\mathrm{d}([m^{n}]_{t}+[m]_{t})+\frac{1}{2\delta}\int_{0}^{T}\lVert F_{2}(u_{t-})\rVert\,\mathrm{d}[m^{n}-m]_{t}.

The first expression on the right-hand can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
\delta>0
appropriately. The second term tends to zero in probability as
n\to\infty
. Hence,
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\bigl\lvert\int_{0}^{t}\trace\bigl(F_{2}(u_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}([[m^{n}]]^{c}_{s}-[[m]]^{c}_{s})\bigr)\bigr\rvert\to 0
in probability, which concludes the proof.
∎

A.2
Auxiliary Results for Section
4

Let
(E,d)
be a complete separable metric space. We let
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(E)
denote the space of square-integrable measures
m
on
[0,T]\times E
, i.e. those with
\int_{[0,T]\times E}d^{2}(x,x_{0})\,\mathrm{d}m(x)<\infty
for some fixed
x_{0}\in E
, such that
m([s,t]\times E)\leq t-s
for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
. We equip
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(E)
with the metric

d_{2}(m^{1},m^{2})=TW_{2}\bigl(\tfrac{1}{T}\tilde{m}^{1},\tfrac{1}{T}\tilde{m}^{2}\bigr)+\bigl\lvert m^{1}([0,T]\times E)-m^{2}([0,T]\times E)\bigr\rvert,

where
\tilde{m}^{i}
is defined by
\mathrm{d}\tilde{m}^{i}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}m^{i}(t,x)+(m^{i}_{t}(E)-1)\mathrm{d}\delta_{x_{0}}\,\mathrm{d}t
, where
(m^{i}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}\in L^{1}([0,T])
is the weak derivative of the map
t\mapsto m^{i}([0,t]\times E)
. Here
W_{2}
denotes the
2
-Wasserstein distance on the space of square-integrable probability measures on
[0,T]\times E
.

Lemma A.2
.

A sequence in
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(E)
is compact if and only if it is tight and uniformly square-integrable.

Proof.

This follows essentially from
[
Vil09
, Theorem 6.9]
.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Let
(E^{\prime},d^{\prime})
be another complete separable metric space and let
(m^{n})_{n}
be a convergent sequence in
\mathbb{M}_{\leq T}^{2}(E\times E^{\prime})
with limit
m
. Assume that there exists a family of probability measures
(p^{n}_{t,x})_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times E}
such that
\mathrm{d}m^{n}(t,x,y)=\mathrm{d}p^{n}_{t,x}(y)\mathrm{d}\pi^{\#}m^{n}(t,x)
, where
\pi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times E\times E^{\prime}\to[0,T]\times E
is the projection onto
[0,T]\times E
. Then the same holds for
m
, i.e. we find a family of probability measures
(p_{t,x})_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times E}
such that
\mathrm{d}m(t,x,y)=\mathrm{d}p_{t,x}(y)\mathrm{d}\pi^{\#}m(t,x)
.

Proof.

Let us define the space
E_{\ast}=E\times\{0\}\cup\{(x_{0},1)\}
, which we endow with the product metric
d_{\ast}((x,y),(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}))=d(x,x^{\prime})+\lvert y-y^{\prime}\rvert
for
x
,
x^{\prime}\in E
and
y
,
y^{\prime}\in\{0,1\}
. We will identify the elements
x
of
E
with
(x,0)\in E_{\ast}
and view
\ast=(x_{0},1)\in E_{\ast}
as an isolated point. Now, it holds that
\pi^{\#}m^{n}([s,t]\times E)\leq t-s
for any
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, so there exists a flow of subprobability measures
(v^{n}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, such that
\mathrm{d}\pi^{\#}(t,x)=\mathrm{d}v^{n}_{t}(x)\mathrm{d}t
. Using this disintegration, to any
m^{n}
, we can associate a measure
\bar{m}^{n}
on
[0,T]\times E_{\ast}\times E^{\prime}
by
\mathrm{d}\bar{m}^{n}(t,x,y)=\mathrm{d}m^{n}(t,x,y)+\mathrm{d}\delta_{y_{0}}(y)(1-v^{n}_{t}(E))\mathrm{d}\delta_{\ast}(x)\mathrm{d}t
for some fixed
y_{0}\in E^{\prime}
. The family
(\bar{m}^{n})_{n}
is tight and we let
\bar{m}
denote its limit along some convergent subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
. Since
\frac{1}{T}\bar{m}
is a probability measure and
\bar{m}([s,t]\times E_{\ast}\times E^{\prime})=t-s
for
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, we can disintegrate
\bar{m}
as
\mathrm{d}\bar{m}(t,x,y)=\mathrm{d}p_{t,x}(y)\mathrm{d}\bar{v}_{t}(x)\mathrm{d}t
for families of probability measures
(p_{t,x})_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times E_{\ast}}
and
(\bar{v}_{t})_{0\in[0,T]}
. We define the flow of subprobability measures
(v_{t})_{0\in[0,T]}
by
v_{t}(A)=\bar{v}_{t}(A)
for
A\in\mathcal{B}(E)
and claim that
\mathrm{d}m(t,x,y)=\mathrm{d}p_{t,x}(y)\mathrm{d}v_{t}(x)\mathrm{d}t
, which yields the desired disintegration of
m
. Let
\varphi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times E\times E^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
be continuous and bounded and define
\bar{\varphi}\in C_{b}([0,T]\times E_{\ast}\times E^{\prime})
by
\bar{\varphi}(t,x,y)=\varphi(t,x,y)
if
x\in E
and
\bar{\varphi}(t,x,y)=0
if
x=\ast
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\langle m,\varphi\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\langle m^{n_{k}},\varphi\rangle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\langle\bar{m}^{n_{k}},\bar{\varphi}\rangle=\langle\bar{m},\bar{\varphi}\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{E}\int_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\varphi}(t,x,y)\,\mathrm{d}p_{t,x}(y)\,\mathrm{d}\bar{v}_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\biggl(\int_{E^{\prime}}\bar{\varphi}(t,\ast,y)\,\mathrm{d}p_{t,x}(y)\biggr)\bar{v}_{t}(\{\ast\})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{E}\int_{E^{\prime}}\varphi(t,x,y)\,\mathrm{d}p_{t,x}(y)\,\mathrm{d}v_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\varphi
was arbitrary, this proves the claim.
∎

A.3
Regularisation of Functions

Proposition A.4
.

Let
\varphi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy the same conditions as
f_{1}
in Assumptions
2.4
and
2.6
. Then we can find a sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n}
of functions
\varphi_{n}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(i)

\varphi_{n}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
converges to
\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot)
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times G
for all
t\in[0,T]
;

(ii)

\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq C^{\prime}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})
for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
independent of
n
;

(iii)

there exists a function
h_{n}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
with
\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq h_{n}(x)
for all
(t,x,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G
;

(iv)

the map
g\mapsto\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)
is strictly convex and continuous differentiable, and
\partial_{g}\varphi_{n}
is bounded.

Proof.

We construct an approximation of
\varphi
in two steps. Let
n\geq 1
and define
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
through
\inf
-convolution, i.e. we set
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)=\inf_{h\in G}\bigl(\varphi(t,x,h)+n\lvert g-h\rvert^{2}\bigr)
. Since
g\mapsto\varphi(t,x,g)
is convex, the same holds for
g\mapsto\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)
. The second step is to mollify
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}
. Let
\eta\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be the standard mollifier and set
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d}}\eta(\frac{x}{\epsilon})
. We define
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)=(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,\cdot)(g)
and finally set
\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)=e^{-x^{2}/n}\bigl(\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)+\frac{1}{n}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\bigr)
.
We will now choose
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n}
to ensure the locally uniform convergence of
\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}
. We estimate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lvert\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)-\varphi(t,x,g)\rvert&\leq\lvert\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)\rvert+\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert\\
&\ \ \ \lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)-\varphi(t,x,g)\rvert.\end{split}

(A.7)

We will estimate the three terms on the right-hand side above. Let us fix a compact subset
K
of
\mathbb{R}
and let
\omega_{K}
be a modulus of continuity of
g\mapsto\varphi(t,x,g)
which is uniform in
[0,T]\times K
, i.e.
\lvert\varphi(t,x,g)-\varphi(t,x,g^{\prime})\rvert\leq\omega_{K}(\lvert g-g^{\prime}\rvert)
for all
g
,
g^{\prime}\in G
and
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times K
. Let us start with the third term on the right-hand side of (
A.7
). For
g\in G
, we estimate

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)-\varphi(t,x,g)\rvert

\displaystyle=\biggl\lvert\inf_{h\in G}\varphi(t,x,h)+n\lvert g-h\rvert^{2}-\varphi(t,x,g)\bigg\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\delta\in[0,\text{diam}(G)]}-\omega_{K}(\delta)+n\delta^{2}.

Define
\delta_{n}=\sup\{\delta\in[0,\text{diam}(G)]\mathpunct{:}-\omega_{K}(\delta)+n\delta^{2}\leq 0\}
. Clearly, it holds that
\delta_{n}\to 0
, so that

\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)-\varphi(t,x,g)\rvert\leq\inf_{\delta\in[0,\text{diam}(G)]}-\omega_{K}(\delta)+n\delta^{2}\leq\omega_{K}(\delta_{n})\to 0

as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, from the definition of
\tilde{\varphi}_{n}
, it immediately follows that
\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq\lvert\varphi(t,x,g)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})
for
(t,x,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G
.

Next, we consider the second expression on the right-hand side of (
A.7
). We have

\displaystyle\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\eta_{\epsilon}(g-h)\bigl\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,h)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)\bigr\rvert\,\mathrm{d}h

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in B_{\epsilon}(g)}\bigl\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,h)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{h\in B_{\epsilon}(g)}\sup_{y\in G}n\bigl\lvert\lvert g-y\rvert^{2}-\lvert h-y\rvert^{2}\bigr\rvert,

where
B_{\epsilon}(h)
denotes the ball of radius
\epsilon
centered at
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Since
G
is compact, the expression in the third line above tends to zero as
\epsilon\to 0
uniformly over
g\in G
. Consequently, we may choose
\epsilon_{n}>0
such that
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
and
\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}(t,x,g)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq\epsilon_{n}
for all
(t,x,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G
.

Lastly, we bound the first term on the right-hand side of (
A.7
). We have

\lvert\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq(1-e^{-x^{2}/n})\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)\rvert+\frac{1}{n}\sup_{g\in G}\lvert g\rvert^{2}.

(A.8)

Now, it holds that

\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\eta_{\epsilon}(h-g)\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,h)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}h\leq\sup_{h\in B_{\epsilon}(g)}\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,h)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}).

(A.9)

Inserting this into (
A.8
) yields

\lvert\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)-\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon}(t,x,g)\rvert\leq C(1-e^{-x^{2}/n})(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})+\frac{1}{n}\sup_{h\in G}\lvert h\rvert^{2}

and the bound on the right-hand side converges to zero uniformly in
\epsilon>0
and
x\in K
. Putting everything together implies that
\varphi_{n}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G\to G
defined by
\varphi_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}(t,x,g)=\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)
converges to
\varphi
uniformly on compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G
, so Property
(i)
holds. Moreover, by (
A.9
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq e^{-x^{2}/n}\lvert\tilde{\varphi}_{n}^{\epsilon_{n}}(t,x,g)\rvert+e^{-x^{2}/n}\frac{1}{n}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\leq Ce^{-x^{2}/n}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})+e^{-x^{2}/n}\frac{1}{n}\sup_{h\in G}\lvert h\rvert^{2}

for all
(t,x,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times G
, which establishes Property
(ii)
. Next, note that the left-hand side is in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
as a function of
x
, so Property
(iii)
is satisfied as well. Lastly, the strict convexity and continuous differentiability of
g\mapsto\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)
follow by construction and the boundedness of
\partial_{g}\varphi_{n}(t,x,g)
is a consequence of the prefactor
e^{-x^{2}/n}
. This establishes Property
(iv)
and concludes the proof.
∎

Proposition A.5
.

Let
\varphi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable map such that
(x,v)\mapsto\varphi(t,x,v)
is uniformly continuous for
(t,x,v)
in compact subsets of
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
and
\lvert\varphi(t,x,v)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}^{p}(v))
for some
p\in[1,2]
and
C>0
. Then there exists a sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n}
of measurable function
\varphi_{n}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(i)

\varphi_{n}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
converges to
\varphi(t,\cdot,\cdot)
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
for all
t\in[0,T]
;

(ii)

\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)\rvert\leq C^{\prime}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{p}+M_{2}^{p}(v))
for a constant
C^{\prime}>0
independent of
n
;

(iii)

there exists a function
h_{n}\in C_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with
\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)\rvert\leq h_{n}(x)
for all
(t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
;

(iv)

v\mapsto\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)
admits a linear functional derivative
D\varphi_{n}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\lvert D\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)(y)\rvert\leq h_{n}(y)
for all
(t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto D\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)(y)
is continuous with respect to
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
.

Proof.

We construct an approximation of
\varphi
in three steps. First we pick
n\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
and set
x_{i}=-n+i2^{-k}
for
i=0
, …,
2n2^{k}=L
. Then we let
(\psi_{i})_{i=1,\dots,L-1}
be a partition of unity subordinated to the open cover
((x_{i}-2^{-k},x_{i}+2^{-k}))_{i=1,\dots,L-1}
and set
\kappa_{n}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\psi_{i}(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Now, for
v\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
we define the subprobability measures
v_{n}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
by
\mathrm{d}v_{n}(x)=\kappa_{n}(x)\,\mathrm{d}v(x)
and
v_{n,k}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
by
\mathrm{d}v_{n,k}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\varpi_{i}(v)\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
, where
\varpi_{i}(v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}v(x)
and
\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)=c_{i}\psi_{i}(x)
with
c_{i}^{-1}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
. Next, for
w\in[0,\infty)^{L-1}
we let
m_{w}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R})
be defined by
\mathrm{d}m_{w}(x)=\sum_{i=1}w_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
. Then, we introduce the function
\Phi_{n,k}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{L-1}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
\Phi_{n,k}(t,x,w)=\varphi(t,x,m_{w^{+}})
, where
w^{+}=(w_{i}\lor 0)_{i=1,\dots,L-1}
for
w\in\mathbb{R}^{L-1}
. Finally, for
\epsilon>0
we set
\Phi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,w)=(\eta_{\epsilon}\ast\Phi_{n,k}(t,x,\cdot))(w)
and
\varphi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,v)=\Phi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,\varpi(v))
, where
\eta\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{L-1}\to\mathbb{R}
is the standard mollifier and
\eta_{\epsilon}(x)=\frac{1}{\epsilon^{L-1}}\eta(\frac{x}{\epsilon})
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{L-1}
. Our goal is to show that choosing first
\epsilon=\epsilon_{n,k}
small enough and then
k=k_{n}
sufficiently large, the resulting sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\geq 1}
, defined by
\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)=\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi_{n,k_{n}}^{\epsilon_{n,k_{n}}}(t,x,v)
, satisfies Properties
(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iv)
.

Let us start with Property
(i)
. Note that for
n\geq 1
, on the set
\mathcal{K}_{n}
of measures
v\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\supp v\subset[-n,n]
the metrics
d_{0}
and
d_{2}
are strongly equivalent, so there exists a constant
c_{n}>0
such that
d_{2}\leq c_{n}d_{0}
. Now, for
k\geq 1
, and
\epsilon>0
, we can write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lvert\varphi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,v)-\varphi(t,x,v)\rvert&\leq\lvert\varphi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,v)-\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})\rvert+\lvert\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})-\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\rvert\\
&\ \ \ +\lvert\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n})-\varphi(t,x,v)\rvert.\end{split}

(A.10)

We inspect the three terms in turn. For the first, we obtain

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,v

\displaystyle)-\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{L-1}}\eta_{\epsilon}(\varpi(v)-w)\bigl\lvert\Phi_{n,k}(t,x,w)-\Phi_{n,k}(t,x,\varpi(v))\bigr\rvert\,\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{w\in B_{\epsilon}(\varpi(v))}\lvert\varphi_{n,k}(t,x,m_{w^{+}})-\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})|,

(A.11)

where
B_{\epsilon}(w)
is the ball of radius
\epsilon
centered at
w\in\mathbb{R}^{L-1}
. For
w\in B_{\epsilon}(\varpi(v))
, we estimate

\displaystyle d_{2}(m_{w^{+}},v_{n,k})

\displaystyle\leq c_{n}d_{0}(m_{w^{+}},v_{n,k})

\displaystyle=c_{n}\sup_{\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\text{bLip}\leq 1}}\langle m_{w^{+}}-v_{n,k},\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=c_{n}\sup_{\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\text{bLip}\leq 1}}\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}((w_{i}\lor 0)-\varpi_{i}(v))\Phi(x)\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq c_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\lvert w_{i}-\varpi_{i}(v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq c_{n}(L-1)\epsilon.

(A.12)

For
t\in[0,T]
, let us define the modulus of continuity
\omega_{n}
by

\omega_{n}(\delta)=\sup\biggl\{\sup_{(t,x)\in[0,T]\times[-n,n]}\lvert\varphi(t,x,v)-\varphi(t,x,v^{\prime})\rvert\mathpunct{:}v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}_{n},\,d_{2}(v,v^{\prime})\leq\delta\biggr\}.

By assumption, this modulus is continuous at zero, i.e.
\omega_{n}(\delta)\to 0
as
\delta\to 0
. Inserting (
A.3
) into (
A.3
) and applying
\omega_{n}
yields

\bigl\lvert\varphi_{n,k}^{\epsilon}(t,x,v)-\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})\bigr\rvert\leq\omega_{n}\bigl(c_{n}(2n2^{k}-1)\epsilon\bigr).

Now, we chose
\epsilon_{n,k}>0
such that
\omega_{n}\bigl(c_{n}(2n2^{k}-1)\epsilon_{n,k}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{n}\bigr\}
, whence
\omega_{n}\bigl(c_{n}(2n2^{k}-1)\epsilon_{n,k}\bigr)=\frac{1}{n}\to 0
. This finishes our analysis of the first summand of (
A.10
).

We estimate the second term in (
A.10
) by

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})-\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\rvert\leq\omega^{n}_{t}(d_{2}(v_{n,k},v_{n}))\leq\omega_{n}(c_{n}d_{0}(v_{n,k},v_{n})).

We bound the
d_{0}
-metric appearing on the right-hand side by

\displaystyle d_{0}(v_{n,k},v_{k})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\text{bLip}\leq 1}}\langle v_{n,k}-v_{k},\Phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\sup_{\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\text{bLip}\leq 1}}\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi_{i}(v)\Phi(x)\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi(x)\psi_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}v(x)\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\lVert\Phi\rVert_{\text{bLip}\leq 1}}\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi_{i}(v)\Phi(x_{i})\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi(x_{i})\psi_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}v(x)\biggr)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varpi_{i}(v)\lvert x_{i}-x\rvert\bar{\psi}_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert x_{i}-x\rvert\psi_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}v(x)\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{-k}v(\mathbb{R}),

where we note that the expression in the third line vanishes. In view of the previous display, we obtain
\lvert\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k})-\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\rvert\leq\omega_{n}(c_{n}2^{-k})
. We set
k_{n}=\inf\{k\geq 1\mathpunct{:}\omega_{n}(c_{n}2^{-k})\leq\frac{1}{n}\}
.

Let us now study the last term in (
A.10
). Fix compact subsets
K
and
\mathcal{K}
of
\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, respectively and let
\omega_{t}
be a modulus of continuity of
K\times\mathcal{K}\ni(x,v)\mapsto\varphi(t,x,v)
. We estimate

d_{2}^{2}(v_{n},v)\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2})(1-\kappa_{n}(x))\,\mathrm{d}v(x),

(A.13)

where we use the coupling
\pi\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
of
\tilde{v}=v+(1-v(\mathbb{R}))\delta_{0}
and
\tilde{v}_{n}=v_{n}+(1-v_{n}(\mathbb{R}))\delta_{0}
defined by

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(x,y)\,\mathrm{d}\pi(x,y)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,x)\,\mathrm{d}v_{n}(x)+(1-v_{n}(\mathbb{R}))\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,0)(1-\kappa_{n}(x))\,\mathrm{d}v(x)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +(1-v(\mathbb{R}))(1-v_{n}(\mathbb{R}))h(0,0)

for
h\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
to estimate the
2
-Wasserstein distance between
\tilde{v}
and
\tilde{v}_{n}
. The right-hand side of Equation (
A.13
) vanishes uniformly in
v\in\mathcal{K}
as
n\to\infty
. Thus, we obtain that

\displaystyle\lvert\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,

\displaystyle v_{n})-\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v)\rvert+\lvert\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v)-\varphi(t,x,v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\omega_{t}(d_{2}(v_{n},v))+C\bigl(1+\lvert x\rvert^{p}+M_{2}^{p}(v)\bigr)\mathbf{1}_{x\notin[-(n-1),n-1]}\to 0

uniformly over
(x,v)\in K\times\mathcal{K}
. Setting
\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)=\varphi_{n,k_{n}}^{\epsilon_{n}}(t,x,v)
with
\epsilon_{n}=\epsilon_{n,k_{n}}
and putting everything together yields
\bigl\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)-\varphi(t,x,v)\bigr\rvert\to 0
uniformly over
(x,v)
in compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
as
n\to\infty
.

For Property
(ii)
, similarly to (
A.10
), we write

\displaystyle\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)-\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\rvert+\lvert\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\bigl\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)-\kappa_{n}(x)\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k_{n}})\bigr\rvert+\bigl\lvert\varphi(t,x,v_{n,k_{n}})-\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\ \ \ +C\bigl(1+\lvert x\rvert^{p}+M_{2}^{p}(v_{n})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n}+C\bigl(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{p}(v_{n})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq(C+2)(1+\lvert x\rvert^{p}+M_{2}^{p}(v_{n})\bigr),

so choosing
C^{\prime}=C+2
gives the uniform growth bound. Moreover, we can further estimate the right-hand side above by
(C+2)(1+\lvert x\rvert^{p}+n^{p}\bigr)
. Then noting that
\lvert\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)\rvert
vanishes outside of
[-n,n]
, immediately gives Property
(iii)
.

It remains to establish Property
(iv)
. Let us first prove that
v\mapsto\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)
has a linear functional derivative. For
v
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
, setting
\Phi_{n}(t,x,w)=\Phi_{n,k_{n}}^{\epsilon_{n}}(t,x,w)
, we compute

\displaystyle\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)-\varphi_{n}(t,x,v^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{w_{i}}\Phi_{n}\bigl(t,x,s\varpi(v)+(1-s)\varpi(v^{\prime})\bigr)(\varpi(v)-\varpi(v^{\prime}))\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{w_{i}}\Phi_{n}\bigl(t,x,w(sv+(1-s)v^{\prime})\bigr)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi_{i}(y)\,\mathrm{d}(v-v^{\prime})(y)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl\langle v-v^{\prime},D\varphi_{n}\bigl(t,x,sv+(1-s)v^{\prime}\bigr)\bigr\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s,

where we use in the second equality that the function
v\mapsto\varpi(v)
is linear and where we defined
D\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)(y)=\sum_{i=1}^{K-1}\partial_{w_{i}}\Phi_{n}(t,x,\varpi(v))\psi_{i}(y)
for
y\in\mathbb{R}
and
v\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
. For
y\in\mathbb{R}
, we have that
\lvert D\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)(y)\rvert\leq\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\lvert\partial_{w_{i}}\Phi_{n}(t,x,\varpi(v))\rvert\psi_{i}(y)
and for
i=1
, …,
K
it holds with
\epsilon_{n}=\epsilon_{n,k_{n}}
that

\displaystyle\lvert\partial_{w_{i}}

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(t,x,\varpi(v))\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{L-1}}\bigl\lvert\partial_{w_{i}}\eta_{\epsilon_{n}}(\varpi(v)-w)\bigr\rvert\kappa_{n}(x)\lvert\Phi_{n,k_{n}}(t,x,w)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}w

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\partial_{w_{i}}\eta_{\epsilon_{n}}\rVert_{\infty}\kappa_{n}(x)\sup_{w\in B_{\epsilon_{n}}(\varpi(v))}\lvert\Phi_{n,k_{n}}(t,x,w)\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\partial_{w_{i}}\eta_{\epsilon_{n}}\rVert_{\infty}\kappa_{n}(x)\biggl(\sup_{w\in B_{\epsilon_{n}}(\varpi(v))}\omega_{n}(d_{2}(m_{w^{+}},v_{n,k}))+\omega_{n}(d_{2}(v_{n,k},v_{n}))+\lvert\varphi(t,x,v_{n})\rvert\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\partial_{w_{i}}\eta_{\epsilon_{n}}\rVert_{\infty}\bigl(\tfrac{2}{n}+\kappa_{n}(x)C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{p}+n^{p})\bigr).

Hence,
\lvert D\varphi_{n}(t,x,v)(y)\rvert\leq\lVert\partial_{w_{i}}\eta_{\epsilon_{n}}\rVert_{\infty}\bigl(\tfrac{2}{n}+C(1+2n^{p})\bigr)\sum_{i=1}^{L-1}\psi_{i}(y)=h_{n}(y)
. Clearly, the function
h_{n}
has compact support and
\lVert h_{n}\rVert_{\infty}\leq C_{n}(1+C)
for an appropriately chosen
C_{n}>0
. Lastly, the continuity of
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\cap L^{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni v\mapsto D\varphi(t,x,v)(y)
with respect to the norm
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^{2}}
is a simple consequence of the truncation and mollification.
∎

A.4
Control of Conditional Processes

We briefly describe the setup of the control of conditional processes. On a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
equipped with a
d
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motion
W=(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and a
d
-dimensional
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
\xi
, we consider the SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\alpha_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X_{0}=\xi

(A.14)

for a
d
-dimensional Brownian motion
W=(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued process
\alpha=(\alpha_{t})_{t\geq 0}
such that
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\alpha_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. We call
\mathfrak{a}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P},W,\alpha)
a
weak control
and denote the space of all such objects by
\mathbb{A}
. The
cost functional
is given by

J(\mathfrak{a})=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[f(t,X_{t},\alpha_{t})|\tau>t]\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}[g(X_{T})|\tau>T],

(A.15)

where
\tau=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}X_{t}\notin D\}
for an open subset
D\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
C^{1}
-boundary and
\mathbb{E}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
. We let the
value

V
be the infimum of
J(\mathfrak{a})
over weak controls
\mathfrak{a}\in\mathbb{A}
. We could consider an extended setup with a drift and diffusion coefficient that depend on time and the location of the diffusion or even its conditional law
\mu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot|\tau>t)
. The general case introduces additional difficulties, so for the purpose of illustration we stick to this simple setting.

Assumption A.6
.

Let
f\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable and assume there exists
C>0
such that

(i)

for all
(t,x,a)
we have

\lvert f(t,x,a)\rvert+\lvert g(x)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}+\lvert a\rvert^{2});

(ii)

for all
t
the maps
(x,a)\mapsto f(t,x,a)
and
x\mapsto g(x)
are continuous;

(iii)

for all
(t,x)
the map
a\mapsto f(t,x,a)
is convex.

Our first goal is to prove the equivalence between the weak formulation just introduced and the closed-loop formulation. A
closed-loop control
is a measurable function
a\mathpunct{:}[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that the SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=a(t,X_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X_{0}=\xi

(A.16)

has a weak solution on some filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P})
equipped with a
d
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motion
W
and an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
\xi
, for which
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert a(t,X_{t})\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. Clearly, SDE (
A.16
) exhibits uniqueness in law for any measurable function
a\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, so we can introduce the cost functional

J_{\text{cl}}(a)=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\bigl[f\bigl(t,X_{t},a(t,X_{t})\bigr)\big|\tau>t\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}t+\mathbb{E}[g(X_{T})|\tau>T]

(A.17)

as well as the associated value
V_{\text{cl}}
, which is simply the infimum of
J_{\text{cl}}(a)
over all closed-loop controls. It is clear that any closed-loop control
a
induces a weak control
\mathfrak{a}
through the assignment
\alpha_{t}=a(t,X_{t})
, so that
V\leq V_{\text{cl}}
. We obtain the reverse inequality through a mimicking argument.

Proposition A.7
.

Let Assumption
A.6
be satisfied. For any
K>0
and any weak control
\mathfrak{a}=(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{F},\mathbb{P},W,\alpha)
with
\lvert\alpha_{t}\rvert\leq K
for
\leb\otimes\mathbb{P}
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
, there exists a closed-loop control
a
with corresponding weak solution
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}},\tilde{W},\tilde{\xi},\tilde{X})
to SDE (
A.16
) such that
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot|\tau>t)=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tilde{X}_{t}\in\cdot|\tilde{\tau}>t)
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
J_{\textup{cl}}(a)\leq J(\mathfrak{a})
, where
\tilde{\tau}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}\tilde{X}_{t}\notin D\}
. In particular, it holds that
V_{\textup{cl}}=V
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be as in the statement of the theorem and define
X^{\tau}
by
X^{\tau}_{t}=X_{\tau\land t}
. The process
X^{\tau}
satisfies the SDE

\mathrm{d}X^{\tau}_{t}=\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})\alpha_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X^{\tau}_{0}=\xi,

where
\chi_{D}\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\{0,1\}
is given by
\chi_{D}(x)=\mathbf{1}_{x\in D}
. Now, proceeding as in the proof of Theorem 3.7 in
[
Lac15
]
, using the convexity of
f
in the control argument guaranteed by Assumption
A.6

(iii)
, we can find a measurable function
a\mathpunct{:}[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
bounded by
K
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})\alpha_{t}\big|X^{\tau}_{t}\bigr]&=\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})a(t,X^{\tau}_{t}),\\
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})f(t,X^{\tau}_{t},\alpha_{t})\big|X^{\tau}_{t}\bigr]&\geq\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})f\bigl(t,X^{\tau}_{t},a(t,X^{\tau}_{t})\bigr)\end{split}

(A.18)

for
t\in[0,T]
. We may assume that
a(t,x)=0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
if
t>T
. Then the mimicking theorem by Brunick and Shreve
[
BS13
]
yields a filtered probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{Q}})
equipped with a
d
-dimensional
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
-Brownian motion
\tilde{B}
and a
d
-dimensional
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
-adapted process
\tilde{Y}
such that

\mathrm{d}\tilde{Y}_{t}=\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t})a(t,\tilde{Y}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t},\quad\tilde{Y}_{0}\sim\mathcal{L}(\xi)

and
\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{Y}_{t})=\mathcal{L}(X^{\tau}_{t})
. The latter implies together with (
A.18
) that

\displaystyle J(\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})f(t,X^{\tau}_{t},\alpha_{t})\bigr]}{\mathbb{E}\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{T})g(X^{\tau}_{T})]}{\mathbb{E}\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{T})}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathbb{E}\bigl[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})f\bigl(t,X^{\tau}_{t},a(t,X^{\tau}_{t})\bigr)\bigr]}{\mathbb{E}\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathbb{E}[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{T})g(X^{\tau}_{T})]}{\mathbb{E}\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{T})}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}\bigl[\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t})f\bigl(t,\tilde{Y}_{t},a(t,\tilde{Y}_{t})\bigr)\bigr]}{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}[\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{T})g(\tilde{Y}_{T})]}{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{T})}.

(A.19)

We wish to extend
\tilde{Y}
to a solution of SDE (
A.16
). For that, we perform the change of measure

\frac{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}{\mathrm{d}\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}\bigg|_{\tilde{\mathcal{F}}}=\mathcal{E}\biggl(\int_{0}^{\cdot}(1-\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t}))a(t,\tilde{X}_{t})\cdot\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}_{t}\biggr)_{\infty},

where the process
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is given by
\tilde{X}_{t}=\tilde{Y}_{t}
if
t<\tilde{\tau}
and
\tilde{X}_{t}=\tilde{Y}_{\tilde{\tau}}+\tilde{B}_{t}-\tilde{B}_{\tilde{\tau}}
otherwise. By Girsanov’s theorem, the process
\tilde{W}=(\tilde{W}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined by
\tilde{W}_{t}=\tilde{B}_{t}-\int_{0}^{t}(1-\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{s}))a(s,\tilde{X}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
is a
d
-dimensional
\tilde{\mathbb{F}}
-Brownian motion under
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
. Moreover, we claim that
\tilde{X}
is a weak solution to SDE (
A.16
) on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
with Brownian motion
\tilde{W}
and initial condition
\tilde{\xi}
. Indeed,
\tilde{X}
clearly satisfies SDE (
A.16
) on
[0,\tilde{\tau})
, since
\tilde{W}=\tilde{B}
on
[0,\tilde{\tau})
and
\tilde{Y}
solves SDE (
A.16
) on
[0,\tilde{\tau})
with Brownian motion
\tilde{B}
. Next, assume that
t>\tilde{\tau}
. Then, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\tilde{Y}_{\tilde{\tau}}+\tilde{B}_{t}-\tilde{B}_{\tilde{\tau}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{Y}_{0}+\int_{0}^{\tilde{\tau}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{s})a(s,\tilde{Y}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\tilde{B}_{\tilde{\tau}}+\tilde{B}_{t}-\tilde{B}_{\tilde{\tau}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{Y}_{0}+\int_{0}^{\tilde{\tau}}a(s,\tilde{X}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}(1-\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{s}))a(s,\tilde{X}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\tilde{W}_{t}

\displaystyle=\tilde{Y}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}a(s,\tilde{X}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s+\tilde{W}_{t},

so
\tilde{X}
also solves SDE (
A.16
) for
t>\tilde{\tau}
. Moreover, in view of Equation (
A.4
) we have that

\displaystyle J(\mathfrak{a})

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}\bigl[\chi_{D}(\tilde{X}_{t})f\bigl(t,\tilde{X}_{t},a(t,\tilde{X}_{t})\bigr)\bigr]}{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{X}_{t})}\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}[\chi_{D}(\tilde{X}_{T})g(\tilde{X}_{T})]}{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{X}_{T})}=J_{\text{cl}}(a),

where we used that
\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
coincide on
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\tilde{\tau}\land T}
. It remains to show that the conditional laws of
X_{t}
and
\tilde{X}_{t}
coincide. This follows from
\mathcal{L}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}(\tilde{Y}_{t})=\mathcal{L}(X^{\tau}_{t})
, since for
t\in[0,T]
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in A|\tau>t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t}\mathbf{1}_{X_{t}\in A}]}{\mathbb{E}\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t}}=\frac{\mathbb{E}[\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{X^{\tau}_{t}\in A}]}{\mathbb{E}\chi_{D}(X^{\tau}_{t})}=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}[\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{Y}_{t}\in A}]}{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{Q}}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{Y}_{t})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}[\chi_{D}(\tilde{X}^{\tilde{\tau}}_{t})\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{X}^{\tilde{\tau}}_{t}\in A}]}{\mathbb{E}^{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}}\chi_{D}(\tilde{X}^{\tilde{\tau}}_{t})}=\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(\tilde{X}_{t}\in A|\tilde{\tau}>t),

where
\tilde{\tau}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}\tilde{Y}\notin D\}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}\tilde{X}\notin D\}
.

The last statement follows from a simple limit procedure. Let us choose a sequence of weak controls
\mathfrak{a}^{k}=(\Omega^{k},\mathcal{F}^{k},\mathbb{F}^{k},W^{k},\alpha^{k})
such that
\lvert\alpha_{t}^{k}\rvert\leq k
and
\lim_{k\to\infty}J(\mathfrak{a}^{k})=V
. Such a sequence exists owing to the continuity condition from Assumption
A.6

(ii)
. According to what we proved above, for each
k\geq 1
, there exists a closed-loop control
a^{k}
such that
J_{\text{cl}}(a^{k})=J(\mathfrak{a}^{k})
. Consequently, we get

V_{\text{cl}}\leq\limsup_{k\to\infty}J_{\text{cl}}(a^{k})=\limsup_{k\to\infty}J(\mathfrak{a}^{k})=V,

which concludes the proof.
∎
